The chronicle of England's western march is not merely a record of battles won or lands seized, but a tapestry woven from the threads of destiny, folly, and the relentless pull of the horizon. It begins with the vision of Mr. Oxenham, who saw a white bird—a symbol of purity and perhaps a warning—hovering over the mundane affairs of Plymouth, setting in motion a chain of events that would span continents and centuries.

From the quiet return of Amyas, the first time he walked home bearing the weight of his youth, to the dashing exploits of two Welsh gentlemen who hunted hounds yet ran with deer, the narrative explores the dualities of the human heart, particularly in matters of love where paths diverge sharply. We are introduced to Clovelly Court in its prime, a bastion of old-world order, before our gaze shifts to the Combes of the Far West, where the soil promises gold but demands a price in blood.

The true and tragical history of John Oxenham of Plymouth serves as the anchor, grounding the soaring adventures of the Noble Brotherhood of the Rose. Here, we witness the founding of a chivalric order dedicated to exploration and faith, and the solemn observance of Christmas by Amyas, a man whose life would soon be tested by the most cruel irony: the Mayor of Biddeford baiting his hook with his own flesh.

As the story expands, it traverses the globe. Eustace Leigh meets the Pope's Legate in a clash of ideologies; Biddeford Bridge feasts at Annery House in a banquet of political maneuvering; and the Golden Hind, that vessel of legend, returns to harbor after voyages that defy imagination. The narrative delves into the savage beauty of the New World, recounting how Salvation Yeo slew the King of the Gubbings and how Mr. John Brimblecombe understood the terrifying nature of an oath sworn in foreign lands.

The chivalrous adventure of the Good Ship Rose leads them to Barbadòs, a land devoid of men, and Margarita, where pearls were taken from the depths. At La Guayra, Spanish bloodhounds and English mastiffs clashed in a metaphorical war, followed by communion taken under the tree at Higuerote amidst the shadow of the Inquisition in the Indies. Along the banks of the Meta River, temptation awaited Amyas, a test of spirit that nearly broke him.

Yet, through it all, the resolve of the brothers held. They took the gold-train and the great galleon, securing the wealth of the New World. Salvation Yeo found his lost maid, and Amyas returned home the third time, a changed man. The Virginia Fleet was stopped by the Queen's command, and Admiral John Hawkins testified against Croakers, standing firm against the rising tide of tyranny.

When the Great Armada loomed on the horizon, threatening to swallow England whole, Amyas made his most profound sacrifice, throwing his sword into the sea to signal a surrender of arms but not of will. And so, the tale concludes with the simple, profound image of Amyas letting the apple fall—a moment of gravity that signifies the end of one era and the beginning of another, leaving the reader to ponder the vast, uncharted spaces that lie between the known world and the promise of the west.

"The hollow oak our palace is,
Our heritage the sea."

Any traveler who has wandered through the lush, undulating landscape of North Devon must inevitably arrive at the little white town of Bideford. It climbs gently from its broad river, paved with golden sands, past the many-arched bridge where salmon await the autumn floods, rising toward the pleasant uplands to the west. Above the town, the hills close in, cushioned with deep oak forests, through which jagged crags of fern-fringed slate jut defiantly; below, they soften into rounded knolls and fertile squares of red and green, sinking finally into the wide, hazy expanse of salt marshes and rolling sand-dunes where the Torridge meets the Taw. Together, they flow quietly toward the great Atlantic swell, an everlasting thunder that defines the coast.

Here, the old town stands beneath a soft, Italian sky, fanned perpetually by the fresh ocean breeze that banishes both the biting winter frosts and the stifling heat of the midlands. It has stood thus for perhaps eight hundred years, since the first Grenville, a cousin of the Conqueror, returned from the conquest of South Wales. He gathered around him trusty Saxon serfs, free Norse rovers with their golden curls, and dark Silurian Britons from the Swansea shore, blending their blood into the unique strength and beauty that still characterizes the seaward folk of the region.

Yet at the time of this writing, Bideford was more than merely a pleasant country town. It was one of England's chief ports, furnishing seven ships to fight the Armada. Even a century later, chroniclers noted that it sent more vessels to the northern trade than any port in England save London and Topsham. It was the center of a local civilization and enterprise, small perhaps compared to the vast efforts of today, but who dares despise the day of small things if it proves to be the dawn of mighty ones? England owes the foundation of her naval and commercial glory to the sea-life and labor of Bideford, Dartmouth, Topsham, and Plymouth—then a petty place—and many another little western town. It was the men of Devon, the Drakes, Hawkins, Gilberts, Raleighs, Grenvilles, and Oxenhams, and a host of "forgotten worthies," to whom she owes her commerce, her colonies, and her very existence. Had they not first crippled the ill-gotten resources of the Spaniard through West Indian raids, and then crushed his last huge effort in Britain's Salamis in 1588, what would we be but a popish appanage of a world-tyranny as cruel as heathen Rome?

It is in memory of these men—their voyages, battles, faith, valor, heroic lives, and no less heroic deaths—that I write this book. If I seem to warm into a style somewhat too stilted and pompous, let me be excused for my subject's sake. It is a tale fit rather to be sung than said, a proclamation to all true English hearts, not as a mere novel but as an epic, echoing the great messages of Troy, the Persian Wars, and the trophies of Marathon and Salamis.

One bright summer's afternoon in the year of grace 1575, a tall and fair boy came lingering along the Bideford quay. Clad in a scholar's gown, carrying a satchel and slate, he watched wistfully the shipping and the sailors until, just after passing the bottom of the High Street, he found himself opposite a tavern overlooking the river. In the open bay window sat merchants and gentlemen discoursing over their afternoon draught of sack; outside the door, a group of sailors gathered, listening earnestly to a speaker standing in the midst. The boy, all alive for any sea-news, stepped up to join the sailor-lads peeping and whispering under the men's elbows.

The speech that followed was delivered in a loud, bold voice, accented strongly with Devonshire and sprinkled liberally with oaths.

"If you don't believe me, go and see, or stay here and grow all over blue mould. I tell you, as I am a gentleman, I saw it with these eyes, and so did Salvation Yeo there through a window in the lower room. We measured the heap, as I am a christened man, seventy feet long, ten feet broad, and twelve feet high, of silver bars, each weighing between thirty and forty pounds. And says Captain Drake: 'There, my lads of Devon, I've brought you to the mouth of the world's treasure-house, and it's your own fault now if you don't sweep it out as empty as a stock-fish.'"

"Why didn't you bring some of them home, then, Mr. Oxenham?"

"Why weren't you there to help carry them? We would have brought them away, safe enough, and young Drake and I had already broken the door abroad. But Captain Drake goes off in a dead faint. When we came to look, he had a wound in his leg you might have laid three fingers in, and his boots were full of blood, having been so for an hour or more. But the heart of him was that he never knew it till he dropped. Then his brother and I got him away to the boats, him kicking and struggling, bidding us let him go on with the fight though every step he took in the sand was in a pool of blood. So we got off. Tell me, ye sons of shotten herrings, wasn't it worth more to save him than the dirty silver? For silver we can get again, brave boys; there's more fish in the sea than ever came out of it, and more silver in Nombre de Dios than would pave all the streets in the west country. But of such captains as Franky Drake, Heaven never makes but one at a time. And if we lose him, good-bye to England's luck, say I. And who don't agree, let him choose his weapons, and I'm his man."

The speaker was a tall, sturdy personage with a florid black-bearded face and bold, restless dark eyes. Leaning against the wall with crossed legs and arms akimbo, he seemed to the schoolboy a very magnifico, some prince or duke at least. He was dressed, contrary to all sumptuary laws of the time, in a suit of crimson velvet, a little the worse for wear. By his side lay a long Spanish rapier and a brace of daggers, gaudy enough about the hilts; his fingers sparkled with rings, and he wore two or three gold chains about his neck and large earrings. Behind one of these, a red rose was stuck jauntily among his glossy black curls. On his head sat a broad velvet Spanish hat, in which, instead of a feather, a great gold clasp held a whole Quetzal bird, whose gorgeous plumage of fretted golden-green shone like one entire precious stone.

As he finished his speech, he removed the hat and looked at the bird.

"Look ye, my lads, did you ever see such a fowl as that before? That is the bird which the old Indian kings of Mexico let no one wear but their own selves. And therefore I wear it—I, John Oxenham of South Tawton—as a sign to all brave lads of Devon that, as the Spaniards are the masters of the Indians, we are the masters of the Spaniards." He replaced his hat.

A murmur of applause followed, though one hinted that he doubted the Spaniards were too many for them.

"Too many? How many men did we take Nombre de Dios with? Seventy-three were we, and no more when we sailed out of Plymouth Sound. Before we saw the Spanish Main, half were *gastados*, used up, as the Dons say, with the scurvy. In Port Pheasant, Captain Rawse of Cowes fell in with us, and that gave us some thirty hands more. With that handful, my lads, only fifty-three in all, we picked the lock of the new world! And whom did we lose but our trumpeter, who stood braying like an ass in the middle of the square instead of taking care of his neck like a Christian? I tell you, those Spaniards are rank cowards, as all bullies are. They pray to a woman, the idolatrous rascals! And no wonder they fight like women."

"You're right, captain," sang out a tall, gaunt fellow standing close to him. "One west-country-man can fight two easterlings, and an easterling can beat three Dons any day. Eh! my lads of Devon?"

"For O! it's the herrings and the good brown beef,
And the cider and the cream so white;
O! they are the making of the jolly Devon lads,
For to play, and eke to fight."

"Come," said Oxenham. "Come along! Who lists? Who lists? Who'll make his fortune?"

"Oh, who will join, jolly mariners all?
And who will join, says he, O!
To fill his pockets with the good red gold,
By sailing on the sea, O!"

"Who'll list?" cried the gaunt man again. "Now's your time! We've got forty men to Plymouth now, ready to sail the minute we get back. We want a dozen out of you Bideford men, and just a boy or two, and then we're off and away, and make our fortunes, or go to heaven."

"Our bodies in the sea so deep,
Our souls in heaven to rest!
Where valiant seamen, one and all,
Hereafter shall be blest!"

"Now," said Oxenham, "you won't let the Plymouth men say that the Bideford men daren't follow them? North Devon against South, it is. Who'll join? Who'll join? It is but a step of a way, after all, and sailing as smooth as a duck-pond as soon as you're past Cape Finisterre. I'll run a Clovelly herring-boat there and back for a wager of twenty pounds, and never ship a bucketful all the way. Who'll join? Don't think you're buying a pig in a poke. I know the road, and Salvation Yeo here, too, who was the gunner's mate, as well as I do the narrow seas, and better. You ask him to show you the chart of it now, and see if he don't tell you over the ruttier as well as Drake himself."

On this, the gaunt man pulled from under his arm a great white buffalo horn covered with rough etchings of land and sea, and held it up to the admiring ring.

"See here, boys all, and behold the picture of the place, drawn out so natural as ever was life. I got it from a Portuguese, down to the Azores. He'd pricked it out, pricked it out, wheresoever he'd sailed, and whatsoever he'd seen. Take it in your hands now, Simon Evans, take it in your hands; look it over, and I'll warrant you'll know the way in five minutes so well as ever a shark in the seas."

The horn was passed from hand to hand, while Oxenham, seeing his hearers becoming moved, called through the open window for a great tankard of sack and passed that from hand to hand after the horn.

The schoolboy, who had been devouring everything with his eyes and ears and had contrived to edge himself into the inner ring, now stood face to face with the hero of the emerald crest, getting as many peeps as he could at the wonder. But when he saw the sailors, one after another, turn it over and offer to join Mr. Oxenham, his soul burned within him for a nearer view of that wondrous horn, as magical in its effects as that of Tristram or the enchanter's in Ariosto. When the group had somewhat broken up and Oxenham was going into the tavern with his recruits, the boy asked boldly for a nearer sight of the marvel, which was granted at once.

To his astonished gaze, cities and harbors, dragons and elephants, whales fighting sharks, plate ships of Spain, islands with apes and palm-trees, each with its name overwritten, appeared. Here and there, "Here is gold"; again, "Much gold and silver"—inserted most probably, as the words were in English, by the hands of Mr. Oxenham himself. Lingeringly and longingly, the boy turned it round and round, thinking the owner more fortunate than Khan or Kaiser. Oh, if he could but possess that horn, what needed he on earth beside to make him blest?

"I say, will you sell this?"

"Yea, marry, or my own soul, if I can get the worth of it."

"I want the horn—I don't want your soul; it's somewhat of a stale sole, for aught I know; and there are plenty of fresh ones in the bay."

And therewith, after much fumbling, he pulled out a tester—the only one he had—and asked if that would buy it?

"That! No, nor twenty of them."

The boy thought over what a good knight-errant would do in such a case, and then answered, "Tell you what: I'll fight you for it."

"Thank 'ee, sir!"

"Break the jackanapes's head for him, Yeo," said Oxenham.

"Call me jackanapes again, and I break yours, sir." And the boy lifted his fist fiercely.

Oxenham looked at him a minute, smilingly. "Tut! Tut! My man, hit one of your own size, if you will, and spare little folk like me!"

"If I have a boy's age, sir, I have a man's fist. I shall be fifteen years old this month, and know how to answer any one who insults me."

"Fifteen, my young cockerel? You look liker twenty," said Oxenham, with an admiring glance at the lad's broad limbs, keen blue eyes, curling golden locks

"Si taceant homines, facient te sidera notum,
Sol nescit comitis immemor esse sui."
—Old Epigram on Drake.

Five years have slipped away. It is nine o'clock on a still, bright November morning, yet the bells of Bideford church ring for the daily service two hours past their usual time. They do not toll soberly; instead, they burst forth every five minutes into a jocund peal, tumbling head over heels in ecstasies of joy. The streets of Bideford have transformed into a flower-garden of every color, swarming with seamen, burghers, and the wives and daughters of the town, all clad in holiday attire. Garlands span the thoroughfares, and tapestries adorn every window. The ships in the harbor are dressed in full array of flags, unleashing a tumultuous roar of ordnance from every size. Every stable is crammed with horses, and Sir Richard Grenville's house resembles a tavern in full swing, filled with eating, drinking, unsaddling, and the frantic running of grooms and serving-men.

Along the little churchyard, packed to capacity with women, flows the gentle blood of North Devon—tall, stately men and fair ladies worthy of an era when the English gentry led the people by personal prowess and beauty as much as by intellect and education. Leading the procession is my lady Countess of Bath, escorted by Sir Richard Grenville with cap in hand, for her good Earl Bourchier remains in London with the Queen. Following are the Bassets from Umberleigh, the Carys from Clovelly, the Fortescues of Wear, Buckland, and all quarters, the Coles from Slade, the Stukelys from Affton, the St. Legers from Annery, the Coffins from Portledge, and even the Coplestones from Eggesford, thirty miles away. Last, but certainly not least, comes Sir John Chichester of Ralegh, followed in single file, in the good old patriarchal fashion, by his eight daughters and three of his five famous sons—one fighting valiantly in Ireland to avenge his murdered brother, destined to rule wisely there as Lord Deputy and Baron of Belfast. He meets at the gate his cousin of Arlington, behind whom trails a train of four daughters and nineteen sons; the last has not yet passed the town-hall, while the first is at the Lychgate, laughing and making way for the elder, though shorter, branch of that most fruitful tree.

They enter the church, where all are seated according to their degrees, or as near as possible, not without a few sour looks, shoving, and whispering from one high-born matron to another. The churchwardens and sidesmen, never before having arranged such a goodly company, bustle themselves hot, red, and frantic, until they imploringly seek the help of the great Sir Richard himself to identify everyone, distinguish the elder from the younger branches, and determine who carries eight quarterings in their arms and who only four, lest they set half the fine ladies of North Devon at deadly feud. The old men are safely packed away in the corporation pews, while the young ones care only to secure a vantage point to gaze upon the ladies.

Finally, silence falls. Eyes turn toward the door, and then distant music arrives—flutes, hautboys, drums, and trumpets braying, screaming, and thundering merrily up to the very church doors before ceasing. The churchwardens and sidesmen rush down to the entrance, rods in hand. There is a general whisper and rustle, mingled with glad tears and blessings from many women and some men, as the wonder of the day enters. The rector begins, not the morning service, but the good old thanksgiving for a victory at sea.

What has thus sent old Bideford wild with that "goodly joy and pious mirth"? Why are all eyes fixed with greedy admiration on those four weather-beaten mariners, decked out with knots and ribbons by loving hands? And why even more on that gigantic figure walking before them—a beardless boy yet possessing the frame and stature of a Hercules, towering like Saul of old, a head and shoulders above the congregation, with golden locks flowing down over his shoulders? Why, as the five instinctively ascend to the altar and fall on their knees before the rails, are all eyes turned to the pew where Mrs. Leigh of Burrough hides her face between her hands, her hood rustling and shaking with joyful sobs?

Because there was a fellowship of old in merry England, in county and town. These are Devon men, men of Bideford: Amyas Leigh of Burrough, John Staveley, Michael Heard, and Jonas Marshall of Bideford, and Thomas Braund of Clovelly. They are the first of all English mariners to have sailed round the world with Francis Drake, come hither to give thanks to God.

It is a long story. To explain how it happened, we must go back almost to the point from whence we started in the last chapter.

Somewhat more than a twelvemonth after Mr. Oxenham's departure, young Amyas had gone on quietly enough, according to promise, save for certain occasional outbursts of fierceness common to all young male animals, and especially to boys of strong character. His scholarship progressed no better than before, but his home education flourished healthily. He was fast becoming, young as he was, a right good archer, rider, and swordsman (after the old school of buckler practice). Then his father, having gone down on business to the Exeter Assizes, caught—as was too common in those days—the gaol-fever from the prisoners. He sickened in the very court and died within a week.

Now Mrs. Leigh was left to God and her own soul, with this young lion-cub in leash, to tame and train for this life and the life to come. She had loved her husband fervently and holily. He had been often peevish and melancholy; a disappointed man with an estate impoverished by his father's folly and his own youthful ambition, which had led him to Court, wasting his heart and purse in following a vain shadow. He was one of those men who possess almost every gift except the power to use them. Though a scholar, courtier, and soldier, he found himself, past forty, without settled employment or aim in life, hindered by a certain shyness, pride, or delicate honor that kept him from playing a winning game in the very world after whose prizes he hankered, revenging himself by continual grumbling.

At last, by good luck, he met a fair young Miss Foljambe of Derbyshire, then at Queen Elizabeth's Court, who was as tired as he of the sins of the world, though she had seen less of them. The two contrived to please each other so well that, though the Queen grumbled a little at the lady for marrying and the gentleman for adoring anyone but her royal self, they obtained leave to vanish from the little Babylon at Whitehall and settle in peace at Burrough. In her, he found a treasure, and he knew what he had found.

Mrs. Leigh was, and had been from her youth, one of those noble old English churchwomen, without superstition and without severity, among the fairest features of that heroic time. Yet there was a certain melancholy about her; the recollections of her childhood carried her back to times when it was an awful thing to be a Protestant. She could remember, twenty-five years ago, the burning of poor blind Joan Waste at Derby, and of Mistress Joyce Lewis, too, a lady born. Sometimes, even now, in her nightly dreams, rang in her ears her mother's bitter cries to God, either to spare her that fiery torment or to give her strength to bear it, as she whom she loved had borne it before her. Her mother, of a good family in Yorkshire, had been one of Queen Catherine's bedchamber women and the bosom friend and disciple of Anne Askew. She had sat in Smithfield, with blood curdled by horror, to see the hapless Court beauty, a month before the paragon of Henry's Court, carried in a chair (so crippled was she by the rack) to her fiery doom at the stake, beside her fellow-courtier, Mr. Lascelles, while the very heavens seemed to the shuddering mob around to speak their wrath and grief in solemn thunder peals and heavy drops hissing upon the crackling pile.

Therefore a sadness hung upon her all her life, deepening in the days of Queen Mary, when, as a notorious Protestant and heretic, she had to hide for her life among the hills and caverns of the Peak. She was only saved by the love of her husband's tenants and his bold declaration that, good Catholic as he was, he would run through the body of any constable, justice, priest, yea, bishop or cardinal, who dared to serve the Queen's warrant upon his wife.

So she escaped. But, as I said, a sadness hung upon her all her life; and the skirt of that dark mantle fell upon the young girl who had been the partner of her wanderings and hidings among the lonely hills. After marriage, she gave herself utterly up to God.

And yet, in giving herself to God, Mrs. Leigh gave herself to her husband, her children, and the poor of Northam Town. She was none the less welcome to the Grenvilles, Fortescues, Chichesters, and all the gentle families round, who honored her husband's talents and enjoyed his wit. She accustomed herself to austerities, which often called forth the kindly rebukes of her husband, yet she did so without one superstitious thought of appeasing the fancied wrath of God or giving Him pleasure by any pain of hers. Her spirit had been trained in the freest and loftiest doctrines of Luther's school; and that little mystic "Alt-Deutsch Theologie"—to which the great Reformer said he owed more than to any book save the Bible and St. Augustine—was her counsellor and comforter by day and night.

Now, at little past forty, she was left a widow: lovely still in face and figure, and still more lovely from the divine calm which brooded, like the dove of peace and the Holy Spirit of God, over every look, word, and gesture. It was a sweetness ripened by storm as well as by sunshine, which this world had not given and could not take away. No wonder that Sir Richard and Lady Grenville loved her; no wonder that her children worshipped her; no wonder that the young Amyas, when the first burst of grief was over and he knew again where he stood, felt that a new life had begun for him. His mother was no longer to think and act for him only, but he must think and act for his mother.

Thus, on the very day after his father's funeral, when school-hours were over, instead of coming straight home, he walked boldly into Sir Richard Grenville's house and asked to see his godfather.

"You must be my father now, sir," said he, firmly.

Sir Richard looked at the boy's broad, strong face and swore a great and holy oath, like Glasgerion's, "by oak, and ash, and thorn," that he would be a father to him and a brother to his mother, for Christ's sake. Lady Grenville took the boy by the hand and walked home with him to Burrough. There the two fair women fell on each other's necks and wept together—the one for the loss which had been, the other, by a prophetic instinct, for the like loss which was to come to her also. For the sweet St. Leger knew well that her husband's fiery spirit would never leave his body on a peaceful bed, but that death (as he prayed almost nightly that it might) would find him sword in hand upon the field of duty and of fame. There those two vowed everlasting sisterhood and kept their vow. After that, all things went on at Burrough as before. Amyas rode, shot, boxed, and wandered on the quay at Sir Richard's side. Mrs. Leigh was too wise a woman to alter one tittle of the training her husband had thought best for his younger boy. It was enough that her elder son had of his own accord taken to that form of life in which she, in her secret heart, would fain have moulded both her children.

For Frank, God's wedding gift to that pure love of hers, had won himself honor at home and abroad. First at the school at Bideford, then at Exeter College, where he became a friend of Sir Philip Sidney's and many another young man of rank and promise. Next, in the summer of 1572, on his way to the University of Heidelberg, he went to Paris, with letters of recommendation to Walsingham at the English Embassy. By these letters he not only fell in a second time with Philip Sidney but saved his own life (as Sidney did his) in the Massacre of St. Bartholomew's Day. At Heidelberg he stayed two years, winning fresh honor from all who knew him and resisting all Sidney's entreaties to follow him into Italy. Scorning to be a burden to his parents, he became tutor to two young German princes, whom, after living with them at their father's house for a year or more, he finally took down to Padua "to perfect them," as he wrote home, "according to his insufficiency, in all princely studies."

Sidney was now returned to England, but Frank found friends enough without him. Such letters of recommendation and diplomas did he carry from princes, magnificos, and learned doctors who had fallen in love with the learning, modesty, and virtue of the fair young Englishman. Ere Frank returned to Germany, he had satiated his soul with all the wonders of that wondrous land. He had talked over the art of sonneteering with Tasso, the art of history with Sarpi; he had listened, between awe and incredulity, to the daring theories of Galileo; he had taken his pupils to Venice to be painted by Paul Veronese; he had seen the palaces of Palladio, the merchant princes on the Rialto, the argosies of Rag

"I know that Deformed; he has been a vile thief this seven years;
he goes up and down like a gentleman: I remember his name."--Much
Ado About Nothing.

Amyas slept that night a tired and yet a troubled sleep; and his mother
and Frank, as they bent over his pillow, could see that his brain was
busy with many dreams.

And no wonder; for over and above all the excitement of the day, the
recollection of John Oxenham had taken strange possession of his mind; and all that evening, as he sat in the bay-windowed room where he had
seen him last, Amyas was recalling to himself every look and gesture
of the lost adventurer, and wondering at himself for so doing, till
he retired to sleep, only to renew the fancy in his dreams. At last he
found himself, he knew not how, sailing westward ever, up the wake of
the setting sun, in chase of a tiny sail which was John Oxenham's.
Upon him was a painful sense that, unless he came up with her in time,
something fearful would come to pass; but the ship would not sail. All
around floated the sargasso beds, clogging her bows with their long
snaky coils of weed; and still he tried to sail, and tried to fancy that
he was sailing, till the sun went down and all was utter dark. And then
the moon arose, and in a moment John Oxenham's ship was close aboard;
her sails were torn and fluttering; the pitch was streaming from her
sides; her bulwarks were rotting to decay. And what was that line of
dark objects dangling along the mainyard?--A line of hanged men! And,
horror of horrors, from the yard-arm close above him, John Oxenham's
corpse looked down with grave-light eyes, and beckoned and pointed, as
if to show him his way, and strove to speak, and could not, and pointed
still, not forward, but back along their course. And when Amyas looked
back, behold, behind him was the snow range of the Andes glittering in
the moon, and he knew that he was in the South Seas once more, and that
all America was between him and home. And still the corpse kept pointing
back, and back, and looking at him with yearning eyes of agony, and lips
which longed to tell some awful secret; till he sprang up, and woke with
a shout of terror, and found himself lying in the little coved chamber
in dear old Burrough, with the gray autumn morning already stealing in.

Feverish and excited, he tried in vain to sleep again; and after an
hour's tossing, rose and dressed, and started for a bathe on his beloved
old pebble ridge. As he passed his mother's door, he could not help
looking in. The dim light of morning showed him the bed; but its
pillow had not been pressed that night. His mother, in her long white
night-dress, was kneeling at the other end of the chamber at her
prie-dieu, absorbed in devotion. Gently he slipped in without a word,
and knelt down at her side. She turned, smiled, passed her arm around
him, and went on silently with her prayers. Why not? They were for him,
and he knew it, and prayed also; and his prayers were for her, and for
poor lost John Oxenham, and all his vanished crew.

At last she rose, and standing above him, parted the yellow locks from
off his brow, and looked long and lovingly into his face. There was
nothing to be spoken, for there was nothing to be concealed between
these two souls as clear as glass. Each knew all which the other meant;
each knew that its own thoughts were known. At last the mutual gaze was
over; she stooped and kissed him on the brow, and was in the act to
turn away, as a tear dropped on his forehead. Her little bare feet were
peeping out from under her dress. He bent down and kissed them again and
again; and then looking up, as if to excuse himself,--

"You have such pretty feet, mother!"

Instantly, with a woman's instinct, she had hidden them. She had been a
beauty once, as I said; and though her hair was gray, and her roses had
faded long ago, she was beautiful still, in all eyes which saw deeper
than the mere outward red and white.

"Your dear father used to say so thirty years ago."

"And I say so still: you always were beautiful; you are beautiful now."

"What is that to you, silly boy? Will you play the lover with an old
mother? Go and take your walk, and think of younger ladies, if you can
find any worthy of you."

And so the son went forth, and the mother returned to her prayers.

He walked down to the pebble ridge, where the surges of the bay have
defeated their own fury, by rolling up in the course of ages a rampart
of gray boulder-stones, some two miles long, as cunningly curved, and
smoothed, and fitted, as if the work had been done by human hands, which
protects from the high tides of spring and autumn a fertile sheet of
smooth, alluvial turf. Sniffing the keen salt air like a young sea-dog,
he stripped and plunged into the breakers, and dived, and rolled, and
tossed about the foam with stalwart arms, till he heard himself hailed
from off the shore, and looking up, saw standing on the top of the
rampart the tall figure of his cousin Eustace.

Amyas was half-disappointed at his coming; for, love-lorn rascal, he had
been dreaming all the way thither of Rose Salterne, and had no wish
for a companion who would prevent his dreaming of her all the way back.
Nevertheless, not having seen Eustace for three years, it was but civil
to scramble out and dress, while his cousin walked up and down upon the
turf inside.

Eustace Leigh was the son of a younger brother of Leigh of Burrough, who
had more or less cut himself off from his family, and indeed from his
countrymen, by remaining a Papist. True, though born a Papist, he had
not always been one; for, like many of the gentry, he had become a
Protestant under Edward the Sixth, and then a Papist again under Mary.
But, to his honor be it said, at that point he had stopped, having
too much honesty to turn Protestant a second time, as hundreds did, at
Elizabeth's accession. So a Papist he remained, living out of the way
of the world in a great, rambling, dark house, still called "Chapel,"
on the Atlantic cliffs, in Moorwinstow parish, not far from Sir Richard
Grenville's house of Stow. The penal laws never troubled him; for, in
the first place, they never troubled any one who did not make conspiracy
and rebellion an integral doctrine of his religious creed; and next,
they seldom troubled even them, unless, fired with the glory of
martyrdom, they bullied the long-suffering of Elizabeth and her council
into giving them their deserts, and, like poor Father Southwell in
after years, insisted on being hanged, whether Burleigh liked or not.
Moreover, in such a no-man's-land and end-of-all-the-earth was that old
house at Moorwinstow, that a dozen conspiracies might have been hatched
there without any one hearing of it; and Jesuits and seminary priests
skulked in and out all the year round, unquestioned though unblest; and
found a sort of piquant pleasure, like naughty boys who have crept
into the store-closet, in living in mysterious little dens in a lonely
turret, and going up through a trap-door to celebrate mass in a secret
chamber in the roof, where they were allowed by the powers that were to
play as much as they chose at persecuted saints, and preach about hiding
in dens and caves of the earth. For once, when the zealous parson of
Moorwinstow, having discovered (what everybody knew already) the
existence of "mass priests and their idolatry" at Chapel House, made
formal complaint thereof to Sir Richard, and called on him, as the
nearest justice of the peace, to put in force the act of the fourteenth
of Elizabeth, that worthy knight only rated him soundly for a
fantastical Puritan, and bade him mind his own business, if he wished
not to make the place too hot for him; whereon (for the temporal
authorities, happily for the peace of England, kept in those days
a somewhat tight hand upon the spiritual ones) the worthy parson
subsided,--for, after all, Mr. Thomas Leigh paid his tithes regularly
enough,--and was content, as he expressed it, to bow his head in the
house of Rimmon like Naaman of old, by eating Mr. Leigh's dinners as
often as he was invited, and ignoring the vocation of old Father Francis,
who sat opposite to him, dressed as a layman, and calling himself the
young gentleman's pedagogue.

But the said birds of ill-omen had a very considerable lien on the
conscience of poor Mr. Thomas Leigh, the father of Eustace, in the form
of certain lands once belonging to the Abbey of Hartland. He more than
half believed that he should be lost for holding those lands; but he did
not believe it wholly, and, therefore, he did not give them up; which
was the case, as poor Mary Tudor found to her sorrow, with most of her
"Catholic" subjects, whose consciences, while they compelled them to
return to the only safe fold of Mother Church (extra quam nulla salus),
by no means compelled them to disgorge the wealth of which they had
plundered that only hope of their salvation. Most of them, however, like
poor Tom Leigh, felt the abbey rents burn in their purses; and, as John
Bull generally does in a difficulty, compromised the matter by a second
folly (as if two wrong things made one right one), and petted foreign
priests, and listened, or pretended not to listen, to their plottings
and their practisings; and gave up a son here, and a son there, as a
sort of a sin-offering and scapegoat, to be carried off to Douay, or
Rheims, or Rome, and trained as a seminary priest; in plain English, to
be taught the science of villainy, on the motive of superstition. One of
such hapless scapegoats, and children who had been cast into the fire to
Moloch, was Eustace Leigh, whom his father had sent, giving the fruit of
his body for the sin of his soul, to be made a liar of at Rheims.

And a very fair liar he had become. Not that the lad was a bad fellow at
heart; but he had been chosen by the harpies at home, on account of his
"peculiar vocation;" in plain English, because the wily priests had seen
in him certain capacities of vague hysterical fear of the unseen (the
religious sentiment, we call it now-a-days), and with them that tendency
to be a rogue, which superstitious men always have. He was now a tall,
handsome, light-complexioned man, with a huge upright forehead, a very
small mouth, and a dry and set expression of face, which was always
trying to get free, or rather to seem free, and indulge in smiles and
dimples which were proper; for one ought to have Christian love, and
if one had love one ought to be cheerful, and when people were cheerful
they smiled; and therefore he would smile, and tried to do so; but his
charity prepense looked no more alluring than malice prepense would have
done; and, had he not been really a handsome fellow, many a woman who
raved about his sweetness would have likened his frankness to that of a
skeleton dancing in fetters, and his smiles to the grins thereof.

He had returned to England about a month before, in obedience to the
proclamation which had been set forth for that purpose (and certainly
not before it was needed), that, "whosoever had children, wards,
etc., in the parts beyond the seas, should send in their names to the
ordinary, and within four months call them home again." So Eustace was
now staying with his father at Chapel, having, nevertheless, his private
matters to transact on behalf of the virtuous society by whom he
had been brought up; one of which private matters had brought him to
Bideford the night before.

So he sat down beside Amyas on the pebbles, and looked at him all over
out of the corners of his eyes very gently, as if he did not wish to
hurt him, or even the flies on his back; and Amyas faced right round,
and looked him full in the face with the heartiest of smiles, and held
out a lion's paw, which Eustace took rapturously, and a great shaking of
hands ensued; Amyas gripping with a great round fist, and a quiet quiver
thereof, as much as to say, "I AM glad to see you;" and Eustace pinching
hard with white, straight fingers, and sawing the air violently up and
down, as much as to say, "DON'T YOU SEE how glad I am to see you?" A
very different greeting from the

"I could not love thee, dear, so much,
Loved I not honor more."—LOVELACE.

And what, amidst all this turmoil, has become of the fair breaker of so many hearts, to whom I have not yet even introduced my readers?

She was sitting in the little farmhouse beside the mill, buried in the green depths of the valley of Combe, halfway between Stow and Chapel. She was sulking as much as her sweet nature would permit, chafing at being shut out from the grand doings of Bideford and forced to keep a Martinmas Lent in that far western glen. So lonely was she, in fact, that though she regarded Eustace Leigh with some aversion—and, being a good Protestant, with a great deal of suspicion—she could not find it in her heart to avoid a chat with him whenever he came down to the farm and its mill. He contrived to do so, on I know not what errand, almost every day.

Her uncle and aunt at first looked stiff enough at these visits, and the latter took care always to make a third in every conversation; but still Mr. Leigh was a gentleman's son, and it would not do to be rude to a neighboring squire and a good customer. Rose was the rich man's daughter and they poor cousins, so it would not do either to quarrel with her; and besides, the pretty maid, half by wilfulness and half by her sweet winning tricks, generally contrived to get her own way wheresoever she went. She herself had been wise enough to beg her aunt never to leave them alone—for she "could not a-bear the sight of Mr. Eustace, only she must have some one to talk with down here." On which her aunt considered that she herself was but a simple country-woman, that townsfolk's ways must be very different, and that people knew their own business best, so she let things go on their own way.

Eustace, meanwhile, who knew well that the difference in creed between him and Rose was likely to be the hardest obstacle in the way of his love, took care to keep his private opinions well in the background. Instead of trying to convert the folk at the mill, he daily bought milk or flour from them and gave it away to the old women in Moorwinstow (who agreed that after all, for a Papist, he was a godly young man enough). At last, having taken counsel with Campian and Parsons on certain political plots then on foot, they came to the conclusion that they would all three go to church the next Sunday. Where Messrs. Evan Morgans and Morgan Evans, having crammed up the rubrics beforehand, behaved themselves in a most orthodox and unexceptionable manner; as did also poor Eustace, to the great wonder of all good folks. He went home flattering himself that he had taken in parson, clerk, and people; not knowing in his simple unsimplicity and cunning foolishness that each good wife in the parish was saying to the other, "He turned Protestant? The devil turned monk! He's only after Mistress Salterne, the young hypocrite."

But if the two Jesuits found it expedient, for the holy cause in which they were embarked, to reconcile themselves outwardly to the powers that were, they were none the less busy in private in plotting their overthrow.

Ever since April last they had been playing at hide-and-seek through the length and breadth of England, and now they were only lying quiet till expected news from Ireland should give them their cue, and a great "rising of the West" should sweep from her throne that stiff-necked, persecuting, excommunicate, reprobate, illegitimate, and profligate usurper, who falsely called herself the Queen of England.

For they had as stoutly persuaded themselves in those days, as they have in these (with a real Baconian contempt of the results of sensible experience), that the heart of England was really with them, and that the British nation was on the point of returning to the bosom of the Catholic Church, and giving up Elizabeth to be led in chains to the feet of the rightful Lord of Creation, the Old Man of the Seven Hills. And this fair hope, which has been skipping just in front of them for centuries, always a step farther off, like the place where the rainbow touches the ground, they used to announce at times in language which terrified old Mr. Leigh.

One day, indeed, as Eustace entered his father's private room after his usual visit to the mill, he could hear voices high in dispute; Parsons as usual blustering, Mr. Leigh peevishly deprecating, and Campian, who was really the sweetest-natured of men, trying to pour oil on the troubled waters. Whereat Eustace (for the good of the cause, of course) stopped outside and listened.

"My excellent sir," said Mr. Leigh, "does not your very presence here show how I am affected toward the holy cause of the Catholic faith? But I cannot in the meanwhile forget that I am an Englishman."

"And what is England?" said Parsons. "A heretic and schismatic Babylon, whereof it is written, 'Come out of her, my people, lest you be partaker of her plagues.' Yea, what is a country? An arbitrary division of territory by the princes of this world, who are naught, and come to naught. They are created by the people's will; their existence depends on the sanction of him to whom all power is given in heaven and earth—our Holy Father the Pope. Take away the latter, and what is a king?—the people who have made him may unmake him."

"My dear sir, recollect that I have sworn allegiance to Queen Elizabeth!"

"Yes, sir, you have, sir; and, as I have shown at large in my writings, you were absolved from that allegiance from the moment that the bull of Pius the Fifth declared her a heretic and excommunicate, and thereby to have forfeited all dominion whatsoever. I tell you, sir, what I thought you should have known already, that since the year 1569, England has had no queen, no magistrates, no laws, no lawful authority whatsoever; and that to own allegiance to any English magistrate, sir, or to plead in an English court of law, is to disobey the apostolic precept, 'How dare you go to law before the unbelievers?' I tell you, sir, rebellion is now not merely permitted, it is a duty."

"Take care, sir; for God's sake, take care!" said Mr. Leigh. "Right or wrong, I cannot have such language used in my house. For the sake of my wife and children, I cannot!"

"My dear brother Parsons, deal more gently with the flock," interposed Campian. "Your opinion, though probable, as I well know, in the eyes of most of our order, is hardly safe enough here; the opposite is at least so safe that Mr. Leigh may well excuse his conscience for accepting it. After all, are we not sent hither to proclaim this very thing, and to relieve the souls of good Catholics from a burden which has seemed to them too heavy?"

"Yes," said Parsons, half-sulkily, "to allow all Balaams who will to sacrifice to Baal, while they call themselves by the name of the Lord."

"My dear brother, have I not often reminded you that Naaman was allowed to bow himself in the house of Rimmon? And can we therefore complain of the office to which the Holy Father has appointed us, to declare to such as Mr. Leigh his especial grace, by which the bull of Pius the Fifth (on whose soul God have mercy!) shall henceforth bind the queen and the heretics only; but in no ways the Catholics, at least as long as the present tyranny prevents the pious purposes of the bull?"

"Be it so, sir; be it so. Only observe this, Mr. Leigh, that our brother Campian confesses this to be a tyranny. Observe, sir, that the bull does still bind the so-called queen, and that she and her magistrates are still none the less usurpers, nonentities, and shadows of a shade. And observe this, sir, that when that which is lawful is excused to the weak, it remains no less lawful to the strong. The seven thousand who had not bowed the knee to Baal did not slay his priests; but Elijah did, and won to himself a good reward. And if the rest of the children of Israel sinned not in not slaying Eglon, yet Ehud's deed was none the less justified by all laws human and divine."

"For Heaven's sake, do not talk so, sir! or I must leave the room. What have I to do with Ehud and Eglon, and slaughters, and tyrannies? Our queen is a very good queen, if Heaven would but grant her repentance, and turn her to the true faith. I have never been troubled about religion, nor any one else that I know of in the West country."

"You forget Mr. Trudgeon of Launceston, father, and poor Father Mayne," interposed Eustace, who had by this time slipped in; and Campian added softly—

"Yes, your West of England also has been honored by its martyrs, as well as my London by the precious blood of Story."

"What, young malapert?" cried poor Leigh, facing round upon his son, glad to find any one on whom he might vent his ill-humor; "are you too against me, with a murrain on you? And pray, what the devil brought Cuthbert Mayne to the gallows, and turned Mr. Trudgeon (he was always a foolish hot-head) out of house and home, but just such treasonable talk as Mr. Parsons must needs hold in my house, to make a beggar of me and my children, as he will before he has done."

"The Blessed Virgin forbid!" said Campian.

"The Blessed Virgin forbid? But you must help her to forbid it, Mr. Campian. We should never have had the law of 1571, against bulls, and Agnus Deis, and blessed grains, if the Pope's bull of 1569 had not made them matter of treason, by preventing a poor creature's saving his soul in the true Church without putting his neck into a halter by denying the queen's authority."

"What, sir?" almost roared Parsons, "do you dare to speak evil of the edicts of the Vicar of Christ?"

"I? No. I didn't. Who says I did? All I meant was, I am sure—Mr. Campian, you are a reasonable man, speak for me."

"Mr. Leigh only meant, I am sure, that the Holy Father's prudent intentions have been so far defeated by the perverseness and invincible misunderstanding of the heretics, that that which was in itself meant for the good of the oppressed English Catholics has been perverted to their harm."

"And thus, reverend sir," said Eustace, glad to get into his father's good graces again, "my father attaches blame, not to the Pope—Heaven forbid!--but to the pravity of his enemies."

"And it is for this very reason," said Campian, "that we have brought with us the present merciful explanation of the bull."

"I'll tell you what, gentlemen," said Mr. Leigh, who, like other weak men, grew in valor as his opponent seemed inclined to make peace, "I don't think the declaration was needed. After the new law of 1571 was made, it was never put in force till Mayne and Trudgeon made fools of themselves, and that was full six years. There were a few offenders, they say, who were brought up and admonished, and let go; but even that did not happen down here, and need not happen now, unless you put my son here (for you shall never put me, I warrant you) upon some deed which had better be left alone, and so bring us all to shame."

"Your son, sir, if not openly vowed to God, has, I hope, a due sense of that inward vocation which we have seen in him, and reverences his spiritual fathers too well to listen to the temptations of his earthly father."

"What, sir, will you teach my son to disobey me?"

"Your son is ours also, sir. This is strange language in one who owes a debt to the Church, which it was charitably fancied he meant to pay in the person of his child."

These last words touched poor Mr. Leigh in a sore point, and breaking all bounds, he swore roundly at Parsons, who stood foaming with rage.

"A plague upon you, sir, and a black assizes for you, for you will come to the gallows yet! Do you mean to taunt me in my own house with that Hartland land? You had better go back and ask those who sent you where the dispensation to hold the land is, which they promised to get me years ago, and have gone on putting me off, till they have got my money, and my son, and my conscience, and I vow before all the saints, seem now to want my head over and above. God help me!"—and the poor man's eyes fairly filled with tears.

Now was Eustace's turn to be roused; for, after all, he was an Englishman and a gentleman; and he said kindly enough, but firmly—

"Courage, my dearest father. Remember that I am still your son, and not a Jesuit yet; and whether I ever become one, I promise you, will depend mainly on the treatment which you meet with at the hands of these reverend gentlemen, for whom I, as having brought them hither, must consider myself as surety to you."

If a powder-barrel had exploded in the Jesuits' faces, they could not have been more amazed. Campian looked blank at Parsons, and Parsons at Campian; till the stouter-hearted of the two, recovering his breath at last—

"Sir! do you know, sir, the curse pronounced on those who, after putting their hand to the plough, look back?"

Eustace was one

"It was among the ways of good Queen Bess,
Who ruled as well as ever mortal can, sir,
When she was stogg'd, and the country in a mess,
She was wont to send for a Devon man, sir."

West Country Song.

The following morning, Amyas Leigh was nowhere to be found. He had not fled to drown his sorrows by the Torridge, nor ridden off in a frenzy of despair. Frank discovered that he had simply departed for Stow to visit Sir Richard Grenville. His mother, sensing the truth immediately—that he had gone to seek a commission in the Irish army—dispatched Frank to retrieve him and urge him to reconsider.

Frank mounted his horse and rode the ten miles to Stow, but finding no inn on the road, he turned back toward Clovelly Court to seek hospitality from Mr. Cary, the local squire.

Entering the long, dark hall of the court, where the walls were lined with heavy wainscoting, Frank found the scene chaotic. Seated at the long table was the massive figure of Amyas, alternately burying his face in a pasty and the pasty in his face, his grief seemingly sharpening his appetite. Opposite him, kneeling on the bench with elbows on the table, was young Will Cary, laying down the law fiercely in a low voice.

"Hillo! lad," cried Amyas, "come here and rescue me from this fire-eater. I believe he intends to kill me, or at least someone else."

"Ah! Mr. Frank," said Will Cary, regarding the visitor with the high honor due to a young gentleman of fashion, "welcome. I was longing for your advice on half a dozen matters. Sit down and eat; there is the ale."

"None so early, thank you."

"Ah no!" Amyas muttered, burying his head in the tankard, then mimicking Frank's tone. "Avoid strong ale in the morning. It heats the blood, thickens the animal spirits, and clouds the pure reason with frenzied idols. Eh? Young Plato, young Daniel, come to judgment! Yet, though I cannot see the bottom of the tankard, I see plainly enough that Will shall not fight."

"Shall I not, eh? Who says that? Mr. Frank, I appeal to you; only listen."

"We are in the judgment-seat," Frank said, settling into the pasty. "Proceed, appellant."

"Well, I was telling Amyas that Tom Coffin of Portledge... I will stand him no longer."

"Let him be," said Amyas. "He could stand very well by himself when I last saw him."

"Plague on you, hold your tongue. Does he have any right to look at me as he does whenever I pass?"

"That depends on how he looks; a cat may look at a king, provided she doesn't mistake him for a mouse."

"Oh, I know how he looks, and what he means. He shall stop, or I will stop him. And the other day, when I spoke of Rose Salterne—" "Ah!" groaned Frank, "Here comes the apple again!"—"(never mind what I said) he burst out laughing in my face. Is that not a fair quarrel? And I know he wrote a sonnet and sent it to her via a market woman. What right has he to write sonnets when I cannot? It is not fair play, Mr. Frank, or I am a Jew, a Spaniard, and a Papist; it is not!" Will smote the table until the plates danced.

"My dear knight of the burning pestle," Frank said, suddenly shifting his tone to one of winning sweetness, "do not imagine I cannot feel for you, or that I, like you, have not known the stings of love and the bitterer sting of jealousy. But oh, Mr. Cary, does it not seem an awful waste to squander that divine wrath—which Plato calls the root of all virtues—upon your own petty quarrel? That wrath has been given to you to spend in the service of her whom all bad souls fear and all virtuous souls adore: our peerless queen. Who dares, while she rules England, claim his sword or courage as his own, rather than hers? Are there no Spaniards to conquer, no wild Irish to deliver from oppression, that two gentlemen of Devon can find no better place to test their blades than in each other's hearts?"

"By heaven!" cried Amyas. "Frank speaks like a book. I think Christian gentlemen may leave love quarrels to bulls and rams."

"And that the heir of Clovelly," Frank smiled, "may find nobler examples than the stags in his own deer park."

"Well," said Will, penitently, "you are a great scholar, Mr. Frank, and you speak like one; but gentlemen must fight sometimes, or where would be their honor?"

"I speak," Frank said, with quiet pride, "not merely as a scholar, but as a gentleman who has fought ere now, and to whom it has happened, Mr. Cary, to kill a man (on whose soul may God have mercy). But it is my pride to remember that I have never yet fought in my own quarrel, and my trust in God that I never shall. For as there is nothing more noble than to fight for those we love, so to fight for ourselves is forbidden to a Christian man, unless refusal means utter loss of life or honor; and even then, it is better to overcome evil with good."

"And I can tell you, Will," said Amyas, "I am not troubled by ghosts; but when I cut off the Frenchman's head, I told myself, 'If that braggart had been slandering me instead of her gracious majesty, I should expect to see that head on my pillow every night.'"

"God forbid!" said Will, shuddering. "But what shall I do? To the market tomorrow I will go, even if it were choke-full of Coffins and a ghost in each coffin."

"Leave the matter to me," said Amyas. "I have a device, as does Scholar Frank. If there is a quarrel in the market tomorrow, see if I do not—"

"Well, you are two good fellows," said Will. "Let us have another tankard."

"And drink the health of Mr. Coffin and all gallant lads of the North," Frank added. "Now to my business. I must take this runaway youth home to his mother; if he will not go quietly, I have orders to carry him across my saddle."

"I hope your nag has a strong back," said Amyas. "But I must go on to see Sir Richard, Frank. It is all very well to jest, but my mind is made up."

"Stop," said Cary. "You must stay here tonight; first, for fellowship's sake; and next, because I want the advice of our Phoenix here, our oracle, our paragon. There, Mr. Frank, can you construe that for me? Speak low, though; there comes my father."

"Eh, company here?" the old gentleman asked, settling onto a great bench inside the chimney. "Young men, you are always welcome. How is your good mother, Frank? Where have I been, Will? Round the house-farm, looking at the beeves. That sheeted heifer of Prowse's is all wrong; her coat stares like a hedgehog's. Tell Jewell to bring her in before night. And then up the forty acres; sprang two coveys, picked a leash. The Irish hawk flies as wild as any haggard and will never make a bird. I had to hand her to Tom and take the little peregrine. Give me a Clovelly hawk against the world, after all! Heigh ho, I am very hungry! Half-past twelve and dinner not served? What, Master Amyas, spoiling your appetite with strong ale? Better have tried sack, lad; have some now with me."

The worthy old gentleman finished his oration, settled himself by the warm peat-ashes, and placed his hawk on a perch over his head, while his son pulled off his boots amid warnings to take care of his corns.

"Come, Master Amyas, a pint of white wine and sugar, and a bit of a shoeing-horn to it ere we dine. Some pickled prawns, or a rasher off the coals?"

"Thank you," quoth Amyas. "I have drunk a mort of outlandish liquors in the last three years, better and worse, and never found aught to come up to good ale, which needs neither shoeing-horn before nor after, but takes care of itself and all honest stomachs."

"You speak like a book, boy," said old Cary. "And after all, what a plague comes of these newfangled hot wines and aqua vitae, which have come in since the wars, maddening the brains and fevering the blood?"

"I fear we have not seen the end of that yet," said Frank. "My friends write from the Netherlands that our men are falling into a swinish trick of swilling like the Hollanders. Heaven grant they may not bring the fashion home."

"A man must drink, they say, or die of the ague in those vile swamps," said Amyas. "When they get home here, they will not need it."

"Heaven grant it," said Frank. "I should be sorry to see Devonshire a drunken county; and there are many of our men out there with Mr. Champernoun."

"Ah," said Cary, "there, as in Ireland, we are proving her majesty's saying true, that Devonshire is her right hand, and the young children thereof like the arrows in the hand of the giant."

"They may well be," said his son, "when some of them are giants themselves, like my tall school-fellow opposite."

"He will be up and doing again presently," said old Cary.

"And that I shall," quoth Amyas. "I have been devising brave deeds; and see in the distance enchanters to be bound, dragons choked, empires conquered, though not in Holland."

"You do?" asked Will, sharply, suspecting more was meant than met the ear.

"Yes," said Amyas, turning serious. "I go to what Raleigh calls the Land of the Nymphs. Another month, I hope, will see me abroad in Ireland."

"Abroad? Call it rather at home," said old Cary. "For it is full of Devon men from end to end. George Bourchier from Tawstock has the army in Munster; Warham St. Leger is marshal; George Carew is with Lord Grey of Wilton. After the defeat last year, when that villain Desmond cut off Herbert and Price, the companies were made up with six hundred Devon men, and Arthur Fortescue at their head. So the old county holds her head as proudly in the Land of Ire as she does in the Low Countries and the Spanish Main."

"And where," asked Amyas, "is Davils of Marsland, who used to teach me how to catch trout when I stayed at Stow? Is he in Ireland too?"

"Ah, my lad," said Mr. Cary, "that is a sad story. I thought all England had known it."

"You forget, sir, I am a stranger. Surely he is not dead?"

"Murdered foully, lad! Murdered like a dog, and by the man whom he had treated as his son, and who pretended, the false knave, to call him father."

"His blood is avenged?" said Amyas, fiercely.

"No, by heaven, not yet! Stay, don't cry out again. I am getting old—I must tell my story my own way. It was last July. Over comes to Ireland Saunders, one of those Jesuit foxes, as the Pope's legate, with money, bulls, and a banner hallowed by the Pope. With him was James Fitzmaurice, the same fellow who had sworn on his knees to Perrott in Kilmallock to be a true liegeman to Queen Elizabeth, confirmed by all his saints and a world of Irish howling. Well, he had been practicing with the King of France but got nothing but laughter, so he went over to the Most Catholic King, promising to join Ireland to Spain and set up Popery again. He fits him out and sends him off on such another errand as Stukely's—though I will say, for the honor of Devon, if Stukely lived like a fool, he died like an honest man."

"Sir Thomas Stukely dead too?" said Amyas.

"Wait a while, lad, and you shall have that tragedy afterwards. Well, where was I? Fitzmaurice and the Jesuits land at Smerwick with three ships, choose a place for a fort, bless it with holy water, and purify it from the stain of heretic dominion. Meanwhile, a Courtenay of Haccombe, lying at anchor near by in a ship of war, cuts out the three ships and cuts off the Dons from the sea. John and James Desmond, with some small rabble, go over to the Spaniards. Earl Desmond will not join them, nor fight them, but stands by to take the winning side. Then in comes poor Davils, sent down by the Lord Deputy to charge Desmond and his brothers in the queen's name to assault the Spaniards. Folks say it was rash of his lordship; but I say, what could be better done? Every one knows there was never a stouter or shrewder soldier than Davils; and the young Desmonds used to look on him as their father. But he found out what it was to trust Englishmen turned Irish. The Desmonds found out on a sudden that the Dons were such desperate Paladins that it was madness to meddle, though they were five to one. Poor Davils, seeing there was no fight in them, goes back for help and sleeps that night at Tralee. Arthur Carter of Bideford, St. Leger's lieutenant, as stout an old soldier as Davils himself, sleeps in the same bed with him; the lacquey-boy, who is now with

"Far, far from hence
The Adriatic breaks in a warm bay
Among the green Illyrian hills, and there
The sunshine in the happy glens is fair..."

These lines of Matthew Arnold spoke of a distant, sun-drenched Italy, yet they found a haunting echo in the rugged, shadowed valleys of Devon and Cornwall. Here, the landscape was not a gentle embrace but a jagged confrontation with the Atlantic. The "combs" of the west were deep, narrow gorges where the land rose abruptly from the sea, creating a world of stark contrasts. Inland, the earth was rich with oak woodlands and soft, fertile meadows; seaward, it dissolved into a terrifying wilderness of dark green furze, smooth turf, and towering cliffs of ironstone that stretched like the ribs of a leviathan into the deep.

Each cove possessed its own unique character: a crystal trout stream winding through gray stone mills, black rock pools teeming with salmon, and beaches strewn with golden trefoil and crimson lady's finger. But the most striking feature was the "mouth" of the cove itself—a black field of shark's-tooth rock paved with shell sand and laced with white foam. These formations, tilted by ancient earthquakes, formed a natural jaw, a barrier so formidable that a single rasp of its teeth could grind the timbers of the stoutest ship to splinters. To the landward side lay peace and richness; to the sea, a howling waste of rock and roller, barren to the fisherman and hopeless to the shipwrecked.

Among these mouths, one stood apart: Marsland. Protected by a long sea-wall of rock, it was the sanctuary of Lucy Passmore, the region's infamous "White Witch." It was here, under the pale light of a bright October moon, that two figures stood on the lonely beach just before midnight: Rose Salterne and Lucy herself. Rose, driven by a feverish mix of curiosity and superstition, had kept her appointment, drawn by the very danger of the spell.

"You be safe enough here to-night, miss," Lucy said, her voice cutting through the silence. "My old man is snoring soundly, and no soul sets foot here save the mermaids now and then. Goodness, Father, where's our boat? It ought to be up on the pebbles."

Rose pointed to a strip of sand forty yards nearer the water, where the small vessel lay abandoned.

"Oh, the lazy old villain!" Lucy exclaimed, bustling toward the boat with Rose trailing behind. "He's been chasing pollock around the rocks and forgot to haul her up. I'll trounce him when I get home. I only hope he made her fast, that's all! He's more plague to me than my money ever will be."

The goodwife checked the mooring line, relieved to find the boat secure, and the oars aboard. "Well, I never! Oh, the lazy thief, to leave them here to be stolen!" She sat down in the boat, coiling herself comfortably. "I'll just watch, dear, while you go down to the sea. You must be entirely alone, or you'll see nothing. There's the looking-glass; now go, dip your head three times, and mind you don't look to land or sea until you've spoken the words. Be quick, it's just upon midnight."

Rose hesitated, then slipped off her clothes, shivering in the cool night air. She stepped into the water, wading cautiously between two walls of rock. On her left, a towering cliff hid her in deepest shade; on her right, a lower rock face caught the full blaze of the moonlight, draped in festoons of live and purple sea-weed that shaded dark, goblin-filled crevices. Above, the peaks frowned black; above that, the downs slept bright and cold.

The breeze had died away. Not even a wave broke the perfect stillness of the cove. The gulls were asleep on the ledges. An autumn silence hung heavy, a silence so profound it seemed audible. Rose stood awed, listening for any sign of life beside her own.

A faint bleat, like a newborn lamb, drifted from above. She started, looking up. Then a wail from the cliffs, like a child in pain, answered by another from the opposite rocks. They were merely passing snipe and an otter calling to her brood, but to Rose, they sounded like supernatural goblins answering her call. Nevertheless, they only quickened her expectation. The witch had told her not to fear them. If she performed the rite duly, nothing could harm her.

Yet, as she stepped forward, mirror in hand, she felt the hammering of her own heart. She waded hastily as far as she dared, then stopped, aghast.

A ring of flame encircled her waist. Every limb was bathed in lambent light. The myriad life of the autumn sea, stirred by her approach, flashed suddenly into glory. Around her, the lamps of the sea nymphs swam—heavy, panting, fiery globes. Rainbows of crimson, azure, and emerald shattered into star-showers, lighting the wine-dark depths of the crystal. She saw every shell crawling on the white sand, every rock-fish playing in the crannies with broad, bright eyes staring at her. The great palmate oarweeds waving along the chasm seemed to beckon her down with long brown hands to a grave among their chilly bowers.

She turned to flee, but she had gone too far. Hastily dipping her head three times, she hurried to the sea's edge and, looking through her dripping locks at the magic mirror, pronounced the incantation:

"A maiden pure, here I stand,
Neither on sea, nor yet on land;
Angels watch me on either hand.
If you be landsman, come down the strand;
If you be sailor, come up the sand;
If you be angel, come from the sky,
Look in my glass, and pass me by;
Look in my glass, and go from the shore;
Leave me, but love me for evermore."

The words were hardly finished when her eyes strained into the mirror, seeing only the sparkle of water droplets from her hair, when the sound of rattling pebbles echoed down the beach—the hasty feet of men and horses.

Rose darted into a cavern of the high rock and hastily dressed. Peering out, half-dead with terror, she saw four men. Two had just leaped from their horses, leaving them to drift, and were helping the others run the boat down.

From the stern sheets arose, like an angry ghost, the portly figure of Lucy Passmore. She shrieked in a shrillest treble: "Eh! ye villains, ye roogs, what do ye want staling poor folks' boats by night like this?"

The party recoiled in terror. One turned to run up the beach, shouting, "'Tis a marmaiden—a marmaiden asleep in Willy Passmore's boat!"

"I wish it were any such good luck," Rose heard Will say; "'tis my wife, oh dear!" He cowered, expecting the hearty cuff he received duly. The White Witch, leaping from the boat, dared any man to touch her and thundered to her husband to go home to bed.

The wily dame, as Rose guessed, was using this delay to buy time for her pupil. But she had more solid reasons for making the fight as hard as possible. She, like Rose, had already discerned in the ungainly figure of one of the party the suspicious Welsh gentleman whose calling she had divined long ago. As a loyal subject, she held in extreme horror her husband's meddling with such "Popish skulkers"—unless on consideration of a very handsome sum of money.

In vain Parsons thundered, Campian entreated, Mr. Leigh's groom swore, and her husband danced round in an agony of mingled fear and covetousness.

"No," she cried, "as I am an honest woman and loyal! This is why you left the boat down to the shoore, you old traitor, you, is it? To help off such noxious trade as this out of the hands of her majesty's quorum and rotulorum? Eh? Stand back, cowards! Will you strike a woman?"

This speech, as usual, was merely indicative of her intention to strike. Getting out one of the oars, she swung it round and round fiercely, finally catching Father Parsons such a crack across the shins that he retreated with a howl.

"Lucy, Lucy!" shrieked her husband in a shrillest Devon falsetto. "Be you mazed? Be you mazed, lass? They promised me two gold nobles before I'd lend them the boot!"

"Tu?" shrieked the matron with a tone of ineffable scorn. "And do you call yourself a man?"

"Tu nobles! tu nobles!" shrieked he again, hopping about at oar's length.

"Tu? And would you sell your soul under ten?"

"Oh, if that is it," cried poor Campian, "give her ten, give her ten, brother Pars—Morgans, I mean—and take care of your shins, Offa Cerbero, you know—Oh, virago! Furens quid faemina possit! Certainly she is some Lamia, some Gorgon, some—"

"Take that, for your Lamys and Gorgons to an honest woman!" In a moment, poor Campian's thin legs were cut from under him. The virago, mounting on his trunk astride like the famous one on Hudibras, cried, "Ten nobles, or I'll keep ye here till morning!" And the ten nobles were paid into her hand.

Now the boat, its dragon guardian pacified, was run down to the sea, passing close to the nook where poor little Rose was squeezing herself into the farthest, darkest corner among wet sea-weed and rough barnacles, holding her breath as they approached.

They passed her, and the boat's keel was already in the water. Lucy had followed them close, for reasons of her own. Perceiving a dark cavern near the water's edge, she cunningly surmised it contained Rose and planted her ample person right across its mouth. She grumbled at her husband, the strangers, and above all at Mr. Leigh's groom, to whom she prophesied pretty plainly Launceston gaol and the gallows. The wretched serving-man, who would as soon have dared to leap off Welcombe Cliff as to return railing for railing to the White Witch, vainly entreated her mercy, trying by all possible dodging to keep one of the party between himself and her, lest her redoubted eye should "overlook" him once more to his ruin.

But the night's adventures were not ended. Just as the boat was launched, a faint halloo was heard upon the beach. A minute later, a horseman plunged down the pebbles and along the sand, pulling his horse up on its haunches close to the terrified group before dropping, rather than leaping, from the saddle.

The serving-man, though he dared not tackle a witch, knew well enough how to deal with a swordsman. Drawing his weapon, he sprang upon the newcomer, then recoiled.

"God forgive me, it's Mr. Eustace! Oh, dear sir, I took you for one of Sir Richard's men! Oh, sir, you're hurt!"

"A scratch, a scratch!" almost moaned Eustace. "Help me into the boat, Jack. Gentlemen, I must with you."

"Not with us, surely, my dear son, vagabonds upon the face of the earth?" said kind-hearted Campian.

"With you, forever. All is over here. Whither God and the cause lead"—and he staggered toward the boat.

As he passed Rose, she saw his ghastly, bleeding face, half bound up with a handkerchief that could not conceal the convulsions of rage, shame, and despair twisting it from all its usual beauty. His eyes glared wildly round—and once, right into the cavern. They met hers, so full, keen, and dreadful, that forgetting she was utterly invisible, the terrified girl was on the point of shrieking aloud.

"He has overlooked me!" she said, shuddering to herself, recalling his threat of yesterday.

"Who has wounded you?" asked Campian.

"My cousin—Amyas—and taken the letter!"

"The devil take him, then!" cried Parsons, stamping up and down upon the sand in fury.

"Ay, curse him—you may! I dare not! He saved me—sent me here!" With a groan, he made an effort to enter the boat.

"Oh, my dear young gentleman," cried Lucy Passmore, her woman's heart bursting out at the sight of pain. "You must not go forth with a grane wound like that. Do ye let me just bind mun up—do ye now!" She advanced.

Eustace thrust her back.

"No! Better bear it, I deserve it—devils! I deserve it! On board, or we shall all be lost—William Cary is close behind me!"

At that news, the boat was thrust into the sea faster than ever it had gone before, only in time. It was just rounding the rocks, out of sight, when the rattle of Cary's horsehoofs was heard above.

"That rascal of Mr. Leigh's will catch it now, the Popish villain!" said Lucy Passmore, aloud. "You lie still there, dear life, and settle your spirits; you're so safe as ever was a rabbit to a burrow. I'll see what happens, if I die for it!" So saying, she squeezed herself up through a cleft to a higher ledge, from whence she could see what passed in the valley.

"There mun is! in the meadow, trying to catch the horses! There comes Mr. Cary! Goodness, Father, how a rid'th! he's over the wall already! Ron, Jack! ron then! A'll get to the river! No, a wain't! Goodness, Father

"The fair breeze blew, the white foam flew;
The furrow follow'd free;
We were the first that ever burst
Into that silent sea."

The Ancient Mariner.

Last night was too dark and late to see the old house at Stow. But this bright October day, with the sun high in the sky, Sir Richard and Amyas pace the terraced garden to the south. Amyas has slept until luncheon, an hour ago, but Sir Richard, undeterred by the bustle of the previous evening, has been up since six, recreating the spirits of himself and two terrier dogs by chasing badgers through the valley.

Old Stow House stands—or rather stood—four miles beyond the Cornish border, on the northern slope of the largest and loveliest of those combes I mentioned in the last chapter. Eighty years after Sir Richard's time, a huge Palladian pile, bedizened with every monstrosity of bad taste, was built there, supposedly by Charles the Second for Sir Richard's great-grandson, the heir of that famous Sir Bevil who defeated the Parliamentary troops at Stratton and died fighting valiantly at Lansdowne. Like most creations of the Stuarts, it rose only to fall again. An old man who saw the foundation laid as a boy lived to see it pulled down, the bricks and timber sold on the spot. Since then, the stables have become a farmhouse, the tennis-court a sheep-cote, and the great quadrangle a rick-yard; civilization, spreading so rapidly elsewhere, has surged back to this lonely corner of the land—let us hope, only for a while.
