But I am not writing of that great new Stow House, nor of the past glories whereof quaint pictures still hang in neighboring houses; nor of that famed Sir Bevil, most beautiful and gallant of his generation, on whom old Prince has his pompous epigram:

"Where next shall famous Grenvil's ashes stand?
Thy grandsire fills the sea, and thou the land."

I have to deal with a simpler age and a sterner generation; and with the old house, which had stood there, in part at least, from gray and mythic ages. When the first Sir Richard, son of Hamon Dentatus, Lord of Carboyle, settled at Bideford after slaying the Prince of South-Galis and the Lord of Glamorgan, and gave to the Cistercian monks of Neath all his conquests in South Wales. It was a huge rambling building, half castle, half dwelling-house, such as may be seen still, almost uniquely, in Compton Castle near Torquay, the dwelling-place of Humphrey Gilbert, Walter Raleigh's half-brother, and Richard Grenville's bosom friend. On three sides, to the north, west, and south, the lofty walls of the old ballium still stood, with their machicolated turrets, loopholes, and dark downward crannies for dropping stones and fire on besiegers, relics of a more unsettled age. But the southern court had become a flower-garden, with quaint terraces, statues, knots of flowers, clipped yews and hollies, and all the pedantries of the topiarian art. Toward the east, where the vista of the valley opened, the old walls were gone, and the frowning Norman keep, ruined in the Wars of the Roses, had been replaced by the rich and stately architecture of the Tudors. Altogether, the house, like the time, was in a transitionary state, representing faithfully the passage of the old middle age into the new life which had just burst into blossom throughout Europe.

From the house on three sides, the hill sloped steeply down, and the garden where Sir Richard and Amyas were walking gave a truly English prospect. At one turn they could catch, over the western walls, a glimpse of the blue ocean flecked with passing sails; and at the next, spread far below them, range on range of fertile park, stately avenue, yellow autumn woodland, and purple heather moors, lapping over and over each other up the valley to the old British earthwork, which stood black and furze-grown on its conical peak. Standing out against the sky on the highest bank of hill which closed the valley to the east was the lofty tower of Kilkhampton church, rich with the monuments and offerings of five centuries of Grenvilles. A yellow eastern haze hung soft over park, wood, and moor; the red cattle lowed to each other as they stood brushing away the flies in the rivulet far below; the colts in the horse-park close on their right whinnied as they played together, and their sires from the Queen's Park, on the opposite hill, answered them in fuller though fainter voices. A rutting stag made the still woodland rattle with his hoarse thunder, and a rival far up the valley gave back a trumpet note of defiance, and was himself defied from heathery brows which quivered far away above, half seen through the veil of eastern mist. And close at home, upon the terrace before the house, amid romping spaniels and golden-haired children, sat Lady Grenville herself, the beautiful St. Leger of Annery, the central jewel of all that glorious place. She looked down at her noble children, then up at her more noble husband, and round at that broad paradise of the West, till life seemed too full of happiness, and heaven of light.

And all the while up and down paced Amyas and Sir Richard, talking long, earnestly, and slow; for they both knew that the turning point of the boy's life was come.

"Yes," said Sir Richard, after Amyas, in his blunt simple way, had told him the whole story about Rose Salterne and his brother, "yes, sweet lad, thou hast chosen the better part, thou and thy brother also, and it shall not be taken from you. Only be strong, lad, and trust in God that He will make a man of you."

"I do trust," said Amyas.

"Thank God," said Sir Richard, "that you have yourself taken from my heart that which was my great anxiety for you, from the day that your good father, who sleeps in peace, committed you to my hands. For all best things, Amyas, become, when misused, the very worst; and the love of woman, because it is able to lift man's soul to the heavens, is also able to drag him down to hell. But you have learnt better, Amyas; and know, with our old German forefathers, that, as Tacitus saith, *Sera juvenum Venus, ideoque inexhausta pubertas*. And not only that, Amyas; but trust me, that silly fashion of the French and Italians, to be hanging ever at some woman's apron string, so that no boy shall count himself a man unless he can *vagghezziare le donne*, whether maids or wives, alas! matters little; that fashion, I say, is little less hurtful to the soul than open sin; for by it are bred vanity and expense, envy and heart-burning, yea, hatred and murder often; and even if that be escaped, yet the rich treasure of a manly worship, which should be kept for one alone, is squandered and parted upon many, and the bride at last comes in for nothing but the very last leavings and *caput mortuum* of her bridegroom's heart, and becomes a mere ornament for his table, and a means whereby he may obtain a progeny. May God, who has saved me from that death in life, save you also!" And as he spoke, he looked down toward his wife upon the terrace below; and she, as if guessing instinctively that he was talking of her, looked up with so sweet a smile, that Sir Richard's stern face melted into a very glory of spiritual sunshine.

Amyas looked at them both and sighed; and then turning the conversation suddenly—

"And I may go to Ireland to-morrow?"

"You shall sail in the 'Mary' for Milford Haven, with these letters to Winter. If the wind serves, you may bid the master drop down the river tonight, and be off; for we must lose no time."

"Winter?" said Amyas. "He is no friend of mine, since he left Drake and us so cowardly at the Straits of Magellan."

"Duty must not wait for private quarrels, even though they be just ones, lad: but he will not be your general. When you come to the marshal, or the Lord Deputy, give either of them this letter, and they will set you work—and hard work too, I warrant."

"I want nothing better."

"Right, lad; the best reward for having wrought well already, is to have more to do; and he that has been faithful over a few things, must find his account in being made ruler over many things. That is the true and heroical rest, which only is worthy of gentlemen and sons of God. As for those who, either in this world or the world to come, look for idleness, and hope that God shall feed them with pleasant things, as it were with a spoon, Amyas, I count them cowards and base, even though they call themselves saints and elect."

"I wish you could persuade my poor cousin of that."

"He has yet to learn what losing his life to save it means, Amyas. Bad men have taught him (and I fear these Anabaptists and Puritans at home teach little else), that it is the one great business of every one to save his own soul after he dies; every one for himself; and that that, and not divine self-sacrifice, is the one thing needful, and the better part which Mary chose."

"I think men are inclined enough already to be selfish, without being taught that."

"Right, lad. For me, if I could hang up such a teacher on high as an enemy of mankind, and a corrupter of youth, I would do it gladly. Is there not cowardice and self-seeking enough about the hearts of us fallen sons of Adam, that these false prophets, with their baits of heaven, and their terrors of hell, must exalt our dirtiest vices into heavenly virtues and the means of bliss? Farewell to chivalry and to desperate valor, farewell to patriotism and loyalty, farewell to England and to the manhood of England, if once it shall become the fashion of our preachers to bid every man, as the Jesuits do, take care first of what they call the safety of his soul. Every man will be afraid to die at his post, because he will be afraid that he is not fit to die. Amyas, do thou do thy duty like a man, to thy country, thy queen, and thy God; and count thy life a worthless thing, as did the holy men of old. Do thy work, lad; and leave thy soul to the care of Him who is just and merciful in this, that He rewards every man according to his work. Is there respect of persons with God? Now come in, and take the letters, and to horse. And if I hear of thee dead there at Smerwick fort, with all thy wounds in front, I shall weep for thy mother, lad; but I shall have never a sigh for thee."

If any one shall be startled at hearing a fine gentleman and a warrior like Sir Richard quote Scripture, and think Scripture also, they must be referred to the writings of the time; which they may read not without profit to themselves, if they discover therefrom how it was possible then for men of the world to be thoroughly ingrained with the Gospel, and yet to be free from any taint of superstitious fear, or false devoutness. The religion of those days was such as no soldier need have been ashamed of confessing. At least, Sir Richard died as he lived, without a shudder, and without a whine; and these were his last words, fifteen years after that, as he lay shot through and through, a captive among Popish Spaniards, priests, crucifixes, confession, extreme unction, and all other means and appliances for delivering men out of the hands of a God of love:

"Here die I, Richard Grenville, with a joyful and quiet mind; for that I have ended my life as a true soldier ought, fighting for his country, queen, religion, and honor: my soul willingly departing from this body, leaving behind the lasting fame of having behaved as every valiant soldier is in his duty bound to do."

Those were the last words of Richard Grenville. The pulpits of those days had taught them to him.

But to return. That day's events were not over yet. For, when they went down into the house, the first person whom they met was the old steward, in search of his master.

"There is a manner of roog, Sir Richard, a masterless man, at the door; a very forward fellow, and must needs speak with you."

"A masterless man? He had better not to speak to me, unless he is in love with gaol and gallows."

"Well, your worship," said the steward, "I expect that is what he does want, for he swears he will not leave the gate till he has seen you."

"Seen me? Halidame! he shall see me, here and at Launceston too, if he likes. Bring him in."

"Fegs, Sir Richard, we are half afeard. With your good leave—"

"Hillo, Tony," cried Amyas, "who was ever afeard yet with Sir Richard's good leave?"

"What, has the fellow a tail or horns?"

"Massy no: but I be afeard of treason for your honor; for the fellow is pinked all over in heathen patterns, and as brown as a filbert; and a tall roog, a very strong roog, sir, and a foreigner too, and a mighty staff with him. I expect him to be a manner of Jesuit, or wild Irish, sir; and indeed the grooms have no stomach to handle him, nor the dogs neither, or he had been under the pump before now, for they that saw him coming up the

"It is virtue, yea virtue, gentlemen, that maketh gentlemen; that maketh the poor rich, the base-born noble, the subject a sovereign, the deformed beautiful, the sick whole, the weak strong, the most miserable most happy. There are two principal and peculiar gifts in the nature of man, knowledge and reason; the one commandeth, and the other obeyeth: these things neither the whirling wheel of fortune can change, neither the deceitful cavillings of worldlings separate, neither sickness abate, neither age abolish."—LILLY'S Euphues, 1586.

Now it falls to my lot to recount the founding of that most chivalrous brotherhood of the Rose, which, after a few years, became not only famous in its native Devon but formidable throughout Ireland, the Netherlands, the Spanish Main, and the heart of South America. Should this chapter seem Quixotic or fantastical to any modern reader, let them recall that the generation who spoke and acted thus in matters of love and honor were, nevertheless, practiced and valiant soldiers, prudent politicians, and sharp diplomats. He who wrote the "Arcadia" was simultaneously one of Europe's subtlest statesmen; the poet of the "Faerie Queene" also authored "The State of Ireland." If critics quote against me with a sneer regarding Lilly's "Euphues," I shall only ask: Have they ever read it? For if they have, I pity them if they have not found it, despite occasional pedantry, as a brave, righteous, and pious book. I wish for no better proof of the nobility of the Elizabethan age than the fact that "Euphues" and the "Arcadia" were the two popular romances of the day. While Sir Walter Scott may have ridiculed the Euphuists in his "Sir Piercie Shafton," we deny that Lilly's book could produce such a coxcomb in the hands of any man of common sense.

Indeed, there is a double anachronism in Sir Piercie; for he belongs not to the days of Sidney, but to the profligate days of James, after the breaking of Elizabeth's heart. Of them, perhaps, hereafter. Meanwhile, let those who have not read "Euphues" believe that, if they could train a son after the fashion of his Ephoebus, all fathers, even in these money-making days, would rise up and call them blessed. Let us rather open our eyes and see in these old Elizabethan gallants our own ancestors, showing forth with the luxuriant wildness of youth all the virtues which still go to the making of a true Englishman. Let us recognize in their commercial and military daring, their political astuteness, and their deep reverence for law the examples of our own time. Let us confess that their chivalry is only another garb of that beautiful tenderness and mercy which is now, as it was then, the twin sister of English valor. Even in their extravagant fondness for Continental manners, let us recognize that old Anglo-Norman teachableness and wide-heartedness which has enabled us to profit by the wisdom of all ages without prejudice to our own distinctive national character.

And so I go to my story. Amyas could not sail the next day, or the day after, for the southwester freshened and blew three parts of a gale dead into the bay. Having got the "Mary Grenville" down the river into Appledore pool, ready to start with the first shift of wind, he went quietly home. When his mother started on a pillion behind the old serving-man to ride to Clovelly, where Frank lay wounded, Amyas went in with her as far as Bideford. There he met, coming down the High Street, a procession of horsemen headed by Will Cary, clad cap-a-pie in shining armor, sword on thigh, and helmet at saddle-bow, looking as gallant a young gentleman as ever Bideford dames peeped at from door and window. Behind him, upon country ponies, came four or five stout serving-men carrying his lances and baggage, and their own long-bows, swords, and bucklers; and behind all, in a horse-litter to Mrs. Leigh's great joy, Master Frank himself. Frank deposed that his wounds were only flesh-wounds, the dagger having turned against his ribs; that he must see the last of his brother; and that with her good leave he would not come home to Burrough, but take up his abode with Cary in the Ship Tavern, close to the Bridge-foot. This he did forthwith, settling himself on a couch and holding his levee there in state, mobbed by all the gossips of the town, not without white fibs as to who had brought him into that sorry plight.

In the meanwhile, Frank and Amyas concocted a scheme put into effect the next day, being market-day. First, the innkeeper began under Amyas's orders a bustle of roasting, boiling, and frying unparalleled in the annals of the Ship Tavern. Next, Amyas himself went out into the market and invited as many of his old schoolfellows, one by one apart, as Frank had pointed out to him, to a merry supper and a "rowse" thereon consequent. By this crafty scheme, in came each of Rose Salterne's gentle admirers and found himself, to his considerable disgust, seated at the same table with six rivals, to none of whom had he spoken for the last six months. However, all were too well bred to let the Leighs discern as much; and they, though of course they knew all, settled their guests, Frank on his couch lying at the head of the table, and Amyas taking the bottom. They contrived, by filling all mouths with good things, to save them the pain of speaking to each other till the wine should have loosened their tongues and warmed their hearts. In the meanwhile, both Amyas and Frank, ignoring the silence of their guests with the most provoking good-humor, chatted, joked, and told stories, making themselves such good company that Will Cary, who always found merriment infectious, melted into a jest, and then into another. Finding good-humor far more pleasant than bad, he tried to make Mr. Coffin laugh, and only made him bow; to make Mr. Fortescue laugh, and only made him frown; and unabashed nevertheless, began playing his light artillery upon the waiters till he drove them out of the room bursting with laughter.

So far so good. And when the cloth was drawn, and sack and sugar became the order of the day, and "Queen and Bible" had been duly drunk with all the honors, Frank tried a fresh move.

"I have a toast, gentlemen—here it is. 'The gentlemen of the Irish wars; and may Ireland never be without a St. Leger to stand by a Fortescue, a Fortescue to stand by a St. Leger, and a Chichester to stand by both.'"

Which toast of course involved the drinking of the healths of the three representatives of those families, their returning thanks, and paying a compliment each to the other's house. Thus the ice cracked a little further, and young Fortescue proposed the health of "Amyas Leigh and all bold mariners." To which Amyas replied by a few blunt kindly words that he wished to know no better fortune than to sail round the world again with the present company as fellow-adventurers, and so give the Spaniards another taste of the men of Devon.

By this time, the wine going down sweetly, caused the lips of them that were asleep to speak, till the ice broke up altogether, and every man began talking like a rational Englishman to the man who sat next him.

"And now, gentlemen," said Frank, who saw that it was the fit moment for the grand assault which he had planned all along, "let me give you a health which none of you, I dare say, will refuse to drink with heart and soul as well as with lips—the health of one whom beauty and virtue have so ennobled, that in their light the shadow of lowly birth is unseen; the health of one whom I would proclaim as peerless in loveliness, were it not that every gentleman here has sisters, who might well challenge from her the girdle of Venus: and yet what else dare I say, while those same lovely ladies who, if they but use their own mirrors, must needs be far better judges of beauty than I can be, have in my own hearing again and again assigned the palm to her? Surely, if the goddesses decide among themselves the question of the golden apple, Paris himself must vacate the judgment-seat. Gentlemen, your hearts, I doubt not, have already bid you, as my unworthy lips do now, to drink 'The Rose of Torridge.'"

If the Rose of Torridge herself had walked into the room, she could hardly have caused more blank astonishment than Frank's bold speech. Every guest turned red, and pale, and red again, and looked at the other as much as to say, "What right has any one but I to drink her? Lift your glass, and I will dash it out of your hand." But Frank, with sweet effrontery, drank "The health of the Rose of Torridge, and a double health to that worthy gentleman, whosoever he may be, whom she is fated to honor with her love!"

"Well done, cunning Frank Leigh!" cried blunt Will Cary. "None of us dare quarrel with you now, however much we may sulk at each other. For there's none of us, I'll warrant, but thinks that she likes him the best of all; and so we are bound to believe that you have drunk our healths all round."

"And so I have: and what better thing can you do, gentlemen, than to drink each other's healths all round likewise: and so show yourselves true gentlemen, true Christians, ay, and true lovers? For what is love (let me speak freely to you, gentlemen and guests), what is love, but the very inspiration of that Deity whose name is Love? Be sure that not without reason did the ancients feign Eros to be the eldest of the gods, by whom the jarring elements of chaos were attuned into harmony and order. How, then, shall lovers make him the father of strife? Shall Psyche wed with Cupid, to bring forth a cockatrice's egg? or the soul be filled with love, the likeness of the immortals, to burn with envy and jealousy, division and distrust? True, the rose has its thorn: but it leaves poison and stings to the nettle. Cupid has his arrow: but he hurls no scorpions. Venus is awful when despised, as the daughters of Proetus found: but her handmaids are the Graces, not the Furies. Surely he who loves aright will not only find love lovely, but become himself lovely also. I speak not to reprehend you, gentlemen; for to you (as your piercing wits have already perceived, to judge by your honorable blushes) my discourse tends; but to point you, if you will but permit me, to that rock which I myself have, I know not by what Divine good hap, attained; if, indeed, I have attained it, and am not about to be washed off again by the next tide."

Frank's rapid and fantastic oratory, utterly unexpected as it was, had as yet left their wits no time to set their tempers on fire; but when, weak from his wounds, he paused for breath, there was a haughty murmur from more than one young gentleman, who took his speech as an impertinent interference with each man's right to make a fool of himself. Mr. Coffin, who had sat quietly bolt upright and looking at the opposite wall, now rose as quietly, and with a face which tried to look utterly unconcerned, was walking out of the room. Another minute, and Lady Bath's prophecy about the feast of the Lapithae might have come true.

But Frank's heart and head never failed him.

"Mr. Coffin!" said he, in a tone which compelled that gentleman to turn round, and so brought him under the power of a face which none could have beheld for five minutes and borne malice, so imploring, tender, earnest was it. "My dear Mr. Coffin! If my earnestness has made me forget even for a moment the bounds of courtesy, let me entreat you to forgive me. Do not add to my heavy griefs, heavy enough already, the grief of losing a friend. Only hear me patiently to the end (generously, I know, you will hear me); and then, if you are still incensed, I can but again entreat your forgiveness a second time."

Mr. Coffin, to tell the truth, had at that time never been to Court; and he was therefore somewhat jealous of Frank, and his Court talk, and his Court clothes, and his Court company. Moreover, being the eldest of the guests and only two years younger than Frank himself, he was a little nettled at being classed in the same category with some who were scarce eighteen. And if Frank had given the least hint which seemed to assume his own superiority, all had been lost: but when, instead thereof, he sued in forma pauperis and threw himself upon Coffin's mercy, the latter, who was a true-hearted man enough and after all had known Frank ever since either of them could walk, had nothing to do but to sit down again and submit, while Frank went on more earnestly than ever.

"Believe me; believe me, Mr. Coffin, and gentlemen all, I no more arrogate to myself a superiority over you than does the sailor hurled on shore by the surge fancy himself better than his comrade who is still battling with the foam. For I too, gentlemen—let me confess it, that by confiding in you I may, perhaps, win you to confide in me—have loved, ay and do love, where you love also. Do not start. Is it a matter of wonder that the sun which has dazzled you has dazzled me; that the lodestone which has drawn you has drawn me? Do not frown, either, gentlemen. I have learnt to love you

"Take aim, you noble musqueteers,
And shoot you round about;
Stand to it, valiant pikemen,
And we shall keep them out.
There's not a man of all of us
A foot will backward flee;
I'll be the foremost man in fight,
Says brave Lord Willoughby!"

Elizabethan Ballad.

It was the blessed Christmas afternoon. The light was fading; the evening song was done. The good folk of Bideford were trooping home in merry groups—the father with his children, the lover with his sweetheart—to cakes, ale, flapdragons, and mummer's plays, celebrating the happy sports of Christmas night. Yet one lady only, wrapped close in her black muffler and followed by her maid, walked swiftly, yet sadly, toward the long causeway leading to Northam town.

Sir Richard Grenville and his wife caught her up and stopped her courteously.

"You will come home with us, Mrs. Leigh," said Lady Grenville, "and spend a pleasant Christmas night?"

Mrs. Leigh smiled sweetly, laying one hand on Lady Grenville's arm and pointing with the other to the westward.

"I cannot well spend a merry Christmas night while that sound is in my ears."

The whole party looked in the direction she indicated. Above them, the soft blue sky faded into gray, and misty stars peeped out. But to the westward, where the downs and woods of Raleigh closed in with those of Abbotsham, the blue was webbed with delicate white flakes. Iridescent spots marked the path where the sun had sunk, showing all the colors of the dying dolphin, while low on the horizon lay a long band of grassy green.

"What sound troubles Mrs. Leigh?" Sir Richard asked. "None of us, with our merry hearts and ears dulled by the town's din, had heard it till now. And yet—listen!"

It was dead calm. There was not a breath to stir a blade of grass. Yet the air was full of sound, a low, deep roar hovering over down, wood, salt-marsh, and river, like the roll of a thousand wheels or the tramp of endless armies. It was the thunder of a mighty surge upon the boulders of the pebble ridge.

"The ridge is noisy tonight," said Sir Richard. "There has been wind somewhere."

"There is wind now, where my boy is, God help him!" said Mrs. Leigh. All knew she spoke truly. The spirit of the Atlantic storm had sent forward the token of his coming in the smooth ground-swell heard inland, two miles away. Tomorrow, the pebbles rattling down with each retreating wave might leap to the ridge top and be hurled like round-shot far ashore by the force of the advancing hurricane.

"God help my boy!" Mrs. Leigh repeated.

"God is as near him by sea as by land," said good Sir Richard.

"True, but I am a lone mother; and one that has no heart just now but to go home and pray."

So Mrs. Leigh went onward up the lane, spending all that night listening between her prayers to the thunder of the surge until it was drowned, long ere sunrise, in the thunder of the storm.

And where was Amyas on this same Christmas afternoon?

Amyas sat bareheaded in the stern of a boat in Smerwick Bay, the spray whistling through his curls as he shouted cheerfully, "Pull, and with a will, my merry men all, and never mind shipping a sea. Cannon balls are a cargo that don't spoil by taking salt-water."

His mother's presage had been true enough. Christmas Eve had been the last of the still, dark, steaming nights of early winter, and the western gale had been roaring for the last twelve hours upon the Irish coast.

The short light of the winter day was fading fast. Behind him was a leaping line of billows lashed into mist by the tempest. Beside him, green foam-fringed columns rushed up the black rocks and fell again in a thousand cataracts of snow. Before him lay the deep, sheltered bay, but only four miles out at sea could they see clearly. Some sloping roof of thick gray cloud stretched over their heads, cutting the cliffs off at mid-height, hiding the Kerry mountains and darkening the distant firths into the blackness of night. Underneath that awful roof of whirling mist, the storm howled inland, sweeping before it great foam-sponges and gray salt spray until all the land was hazy, dim, and dun.

Let it howl on! For there was more mist than ever salt spray made, flying before that gale; more thunder than ever sea-surge wakened among the cliffs of Smerwick Bay. Along the sand-hills flashed in the evening gloom red sparks that never came from heaven. That fort, now christened by the invaders Fort Del Oro, where flaunted the hated golden flag of Spain, held San Josepho and eight hundred of the foe. But three nights ago, Amyas and Yeo, and the rest of Winter's shrewdest hands, had slung four culverins out of the Admiral's main deck, floated them ashore, and dragged them up to the battery among the sand-hills. Now it would be seen whether Spanish and Italian condottieri could hold their own on British ground against the men of Devon.

Small blame to Amyas if he was thinking not of his lonely mother at Burrough Court, but of those quick bright flashes on the sand-hill and the fort, where Salvation Yeo was hurling eighteen-pound shot with deadly aim. He watched with a cool, bitter smile of triumph the flying of the sand and the crashing of the gabions. Amyas and his party had been on board at the risk of their lives for a fresh supply of shot; Winter's battery was out of ball and had been firing stones for the last four hours. They ran the boat on shore through the surf, almost careless whether she stove or not, scrambling over the sand-hills with each man carrying a brace of shot slung across his shoulder. Amyas leaped into the trenches and shouted cheerfully to Salvation Yeo:

"More food for the bull-dogs, Gunner, and plums for the Spaniards' Christmas pudding!"

"Don't speak to a man at his business, Master Amyas," Yeo replied grimly. "Five mortal times have I missed; but I will have that accursed Popish rag down, as I'm a sinner."

"Down with it, then; nobody wants you to shoot crooked. Take good iron to it, and not footy paving-stones."

"I believe, sir, that the foul fiend is there, turning my shot aside. I thought I saw him once: but, thank Heaven, here's ball again. Ah, sir, if one could but cast a silver one! Now, stand by, men!"

Once again Yeo's eighteen-pounder roared, and away. Oh, glory! The great yellow flag of Spain, which streamed in the gale, lifted clean into the air, flagstaff and all, and then pitched wildly down head-foremost, far to leeward.

A hurrah from the sailors, answered by the soldiers of the opposite camp, shook the very cloud above them. Ere its echoes had died away, a tall officer leapt upon the parapet of the fort, holding the fallen flag in his hand. Rearing it as well as he could upon his lance point, he held it firmly against the gale while the fallen flagstaff was raised again within.

In a moment a dozen longbows were bent at the daring foe, but Amyas behind shouted, "Shame, lads! Stop and let the gallant gentleman have due courtesy!"

So they stopped. Amyas sprang on the rampart of the battery, took off his hat, and bowed to the flag-holder, who, as soon as relieved of his charge, returned the bow courteously and descended.

It was all but dark, and the firing began to slacken. Salvation and his brother gunners, having covered up their slaughtering tackle with tarpaulings, retired for the night. Leaving Amyas, who had volunteered to take the watch till midnight, the rest of the force lay down under arms among the sand-hills, grumbling themselves to sleep after a scanty supper of biscuit, for provisions were running very short.

Amyas had paced up and down in the gusty darkness for an hour or more, exchanging passing words with the sentinel, when two men entered the battery, chatting busily. One was in complete armor; the other wrapped in the plain short cloak of a man of pens and peace. But the talk of both was neither of sieges nor of sallies, catapults, nor culverins, but simply of English hexameters.

Fancy not, gentle reader, that the two were fiddling while Rome burned. For the commonweal of poetry and letters in that critical year, 1580, was in far greater danger from those hexameters than the common woe of Ireland was from the Spaniards. Imitating classic metres, "versifying," as it was called in contradistinction to rhyming, was becoming fast the fashion among the more learned. Stonyhurst and others had tried their hands at hexameter translations from the Latin and Greek epics, which seemed doggerel enough. Ever and anon some youthful wit broke out in iambics, sapphics, elegiacs, and what not, to the great detriment of the Queen's English and her subjects' ears.

Whether Mr. William Webbe had yet given to the world fragments of his precious hints for the "Reformation of English Poetry" was uncertain, but the Cambridge Malvolio, Gabriel Harvey, had succeeded in arguing Spenser, Dyer, Sidney, and probably Sidney's sister, into following his model of:

"What might I call this tree? A laurel? O bonny laurel!
Needes to thy bowes will I bowe this knee, and vail my bonetto;"

after snubbing the first book of "that Elvish Queene," which was then in manuscript, as a base declension from the classical to the romantic school.

And now Spenser, perhaps in mere melancholy wilfulness, was wasting his mighty genius upon doggerel which he fancied antique. Some piratical publisher had just given to the world "Three proper witty and familiar Letters, lately past between two University men, touching the Earthquake in April last, and our English reformed Versifying," which had set all town wits a-buzzing. It was a correspondence between Spenser and Harvey, proving to the world forever the correctness and melody of lines such as:

"For like magnificoes, not a beck but glorious in show,
In deede most frivolous, not a looke but Tuscanish always."

Let them pass—Alma Mater has seen as bad hexameters since. But the matter was serious. There was a story that Spenser was half bullied into re-writing the "Faerie Queene" in hexameters, had not Raleigh, a true romanticist, persuaded him to follow his better genius. The great dramatists had not yet arisen to form completely that truly English school, of which Spenser, unconscious of his own vast powers, was laying the foundation. Indeed, it was not till Daniel, twenty years after, in his admirable apology for rhyme, had smashed Mr. Campian and his "eight several kinds of classical numbers," that the matter was finally settled.

So Raleigh answered somewhat waspishly to some quotation of Spenser's from the three letters of "Immerito and G. H."

"Tut, tut, Colin Clout, much learning has made thee mad. A good old fishwives' ballad jingle is worth all your sapphics and trimeters, and 'riff-raff thurlery bouncing.' Hey? Have I you there, old lad? Do you mind that precious verse?"

"But, dear Wat, Homer and Virgil—"

"But, dear Ned, Petrarch and Ovid—"

"But, Wat, what have we that we do not owe to the ancients?"

"Ancients, quotha? Why, the legend of King Arthur, and Chevy Chase too, of which even your fellow-sinner Sidney cannot deny that every time he hears it even from a blind fiddler it stirs his heart like a trumpet-blast. Speak well of the bridge that carries you over, man! Did you find your Redcross Knight in Virgil, or such a dame as Una in old Ovid? No more than you did your Pater and Credo, you renegado baptized heathen, you!"

"Yet, surely, our younger and more barbarous taste must bow before divine antiquity, and imitate afar—"

"As dottrels do fowlers. If Homer was blind, lad, why dost not poke out thine eye? Ay, this hexameter is of an ancient house, truly, Ned Spenser, and so is many a rogue: but he cannot make way on our rough English roads. He goes hopping and twitching in our language like a three-legged terrier over a pebble-bank, tumble and up again, rattle and crash."

"Nay, hear, now—"

"'See ye the blindfolded pretty god that feathered archer,
Of lovers' miseries which maketh his bloody game?'"

True, the accent gaped in places, as Harvey had often confessed, but—

"Harvey be hanged for a pedant, and the whole crew of versifiers, from Lord Dorset to yourself! Why delude you into playing Procrustes as he does with the Queen's English, racking one word till its joints be pulled asunder, and squeezing the next all a-heap as the Inquisitors do heretics in their banca cava? Out upon him and you, and Sidney, and the whole kin. You have not made a verse among you, and never will, which is not as lame a gosling as Harvey's own—"

"'Oh thou weathercocke, that stands on the top of Allhallows,
Come thy ways down, if thou dar'st for thy crown, and take the wall
On us.'"

"Hark, now! There is our young

Thus it befell to Chaucer's knight in prison; thus it befell to Don Guzman; and thus it happened on this particular evening in Bideford.

After his release on parole, the Spaniard settled into quarters of considerable comfort, a stark contrast to the lean days of his captivity. He adopted the demeanor of a seasoned soldier of fortune, accepting the flow of events with a quiet resilience. It was barely a month into his stay when Old Salterne, the Mayor of Bideford, arrived for supper.

Though Don Guzman initially bristled at the peculiar English custom of inviting burghers to dine "above the salt" alongside nobility, his innate sense of gentility prevailed. He recognized that Richard Grenville, his host, acted with strict adherence to propriety. Shrugging off his initial discomfort, the Spaniard submitted to the arrangement, sharing the table with a merchant who managed ledgers and trained apprentices. To his surprise, finding the Mayor conversant and unpretentious, Don Guzman found himself engaging in dialogue with him. He discovered Salterne to be a prudent and courteous man, acutely aware of the Spaniard's superior rank yet confident in asserting his own wisdom as an elder statesman—a manner of speaking reminiscent of the merchant princes of Genoa and Venice.

At supper's end, Salterne invited the Don to return the following night. Turning to the Spaniard, he acknowledged the humility required for a nobleman of Spain to dine with a simple merchant, yet assured him that the fare was fit for any rank. Graciously accepting the invitation, Don Guzman secured an excellent supper and, if he chose to drink, fine wine.

Mr. Salterne, a shrewd merchant eager for adventure abroad—evidenced later by his pivotal role in settling Virginia—was equally eager to extract knowledge from guests who seemed familiar with distant lands. He attempted first to loosen the Spaniard's tongue with generous sack, then to interrogate him regarding the Spanish Main, Peru, the Moluccas, and the Indies. The first scheme failed; the Spaniard was as abstemious as a monk, drinking little but water. The second met with limited success; Don Guzman was as cunning as a fox, offering little but wind.

Into this verbal stalemate stepped the Rose of Torridge, radiant as ever. Hearing the topic of conversation, she artlessly added her own questions to her father's. Don Guzman, unable to ignore her inquiries, provided answers that amused both host and hostess without revealing vital commercial secrets.

Little Eros, though sometimes called the eldest of the gods, is by other authorities described as the child of idleness and full bellies. This calumny resonated deeply with Don Guzman. Idle by necessity as a captive, and well-fed by Sir Richard's generous table, he had begun to seek amusement in matters of the heart. Lady Grenville, the nearest option, was considered first; yet, as a man of honor and mindful of his host, he suppressed the desire for such a pursuit, dismissing it as a dangerous chase. He did not, however, dwell on its sinfulness, a moral blindness shared by many Southern Europeans of his time and, regrettably, by the English in the Stuart era. Having discarded Lady Grenville, he allowed the Rose of Torridge to occupy his thoughts. It was not with malicious intent, but rather to amuse himself and because he could not help it. There was an innocent freshness about Rose, fond of admiration yet possessing a charm that was new and captivating to him. "The train of the peacock, yet the heart of the dove," he mused. If he could persuade her to love only him, perhaps he might foolishly reciprocate. This thought struck him with a panic of prudence, resolving him to keep his distance, yet the days dragged on slowly. Lady Grenville, when at home, spoke of nothing but her husband; when she departed for Stow, leaving the Don alone in the great house, what choice did he have but to lounge in the butt-gardens, display his fine black cloak and feather, play rackets with the youth, bowls with the elders, and eventually receive an invitation to supper from Mr. Salterne?

There, he ruled the roast with royal authority, driven not only by rank but by the value of his travels. These were the dawn days of English commerce. Every merchant in Bideford and England burned with imagination regarding discoveries, companies, patents, and settlements. They rivaled the adventures of Sir Edward Osborne and his London Company of Turkey Merchants; they celebrated the privileges granted by Sultan Murad Khan; they honored the Levant voyages of Roger Bodenham, John Fox, Lawrence Aldersey, and John Rule. The crushing of Venetian power at Famagusta and the alliance between Elizabeth and the Grand Turk had opened vast doors for Mediterranean trade. Not a word fell from the Spaniard about the Mediterranean that did not take root in fertile soil. Furthermore, Master Edmund Hogan had successfully embassied to the Emperor of Morocco; John Hawkins and George Fenner had traded in Guinea for musk, civet, gold, and grain; and just two months prior, Captain Hare had sailed for Brazil with adventurers carrying English hardware and textiles, facilitated by John Whithall, who held an ingenio and slaves in Santos. Drake had proven the viability of plundering American coasts and establishing an East Indian trade; Frobisher and Davis were boring through northern ice seeking a passage to China; and Anthony Jenkinson had penetrated deep into Russia, Persia, Armenia, and the Arctic wastes. Everywhere, under Elizabeth's rule, English commerce spread its roots.

As Don Guzman conversed with his new friends, he perceived clearly that they belonged to a race that must be extinguished if Spain were to remain the mistress of the world. Seizing the New World in the Pope's name, crushing the Hollanders, and reducing the Venetians and Genoese to servitude was insufficient. These fierce islanders remained to assert, with cunning policy and scripture, or with sharp shot and cold steel, the principle of free seas and free trade. He saw it, and his countrymen saw it too; hence the Spanish Armada. But of that, another time. Don Guzman also knew, from hard experience, that these islanders, who spoke broad Devon through their noses, were not mere money-counters. Though they preferred profit to fighting, they could fight with a dogged ferocity surpassing the blue blood of Spain. They sent merchant ships armed to the teeth, manned by men trained from childhood to wield those arms, ordered to use them without mercy against any who dared stop their commerce.

One evening, after civilly hinting that Englishmen venturing where they had no right might be sent back, he was met with a volley of retorts: "We'll see that, sir." "Depends on who says 'No right.'" "You found might makes right when you claimed the Indian seas; we may find right makes might when we try." "Try them, then, gentlemen, if it pleases your worships; and find the sacred flag of Spain as invincible as ever was the Roman eagle." "We have, sir. Did you ever hear of Francis Drake?" "Or of George Fenner and the Portugals at the Azores, one against seven?" "Or of John Hawkins, at St. Juan d'Ulloa?"

"You are insolent burghers," Don Guzman declared, rising to depart.

"Sir," interjected Old Salterne, "as you say, we are burghers and plain men, and some of us have forgotten ourselves, perhaps. We beg your forgiveness for our want of manners, attributing it to the strength of my wine. We never meant to be insolent, especially to a noble gentleman and a foreigner."

But the Don refused to be pacified. He walked out, cursing himself as an ass and a blinkard for demeaning himself to such company, forgetting that he had provoked the incident.

The next day, prompted by Mammon, Salterne approached him, begging pardon again and promising that no such rude individuals would be invited if he would deign to visit once more. Appeased, the Don returned the very next evening, sneering at himself throughout the journey.

"Fool that I am!" he muttered. "That girl has bewitched me. I must go and eat my share of dirt for her sake."

Arriving, he cunningly hinted to Old Salterne that he had developed a fondness for the host and felt bound by his courtesy. He suggested he might reveal certain things he would not share with everyone, noting that Spaniards were not jealous of single traders but of general attempts to deprive them of their hard-earned wealth. He implied that opportunities for individual enrichment still existed.

Old Salterne, shrewd though he was, had a weakness, and the Spaniard had touched it. Delighted by the prospect of learning the mysteries of the Spanish monopoly, the merchant often encouraged Rose to draw out the Don. Blind as money made men, he feared nothing lest she be drawn in herself. First, he deemed it impossible she would consider marrying a Popish Spaniard; second, he believed it impossible the Spaniard would marry a burgher's daughter. He trusted that religious difference and family pride would keep them as separate as beings of different species. As for love without marriage, the thought was rendered absurd: on Rose's part by her virtue, on which the old man would stake every farthing; and on the Don's part by a certain human fondness for the continuity of life, for which Mr. Salterne gave him credit. Thus, for weeks and months, the merchant's house became the Don's favorite haunt. He saw the Rose of Torridge daily, and she heard him.

For her part, the poor child had never seen such a man. He possessed the high-bred grace of Frank yet was cast in a manlier mold. He held just enough of his nation's proud self-assertion to make a woman bow before him, yet possessed the tact to prevent it from degenerating into the boastfulness often attributed to Spaniards. He had marvels to tell of flood and field, surpassing even Amyas in number and grace. He was present, whereas the Leighs and her old lovers were not. What choice did she have but to amuse herself with the only person available?

Soon, more ladies in the country found the same attraction. The north was bare of young gallants due to the Netherland and Irish wars. The Spaniard became welcome at every house for miles around, receiving unwonted attention from fair young dames. His head might have turned, and Rose Salterne might have escaped, had not Sir Richard delicately signaled that, despite the free manners of English ladies, brothers were as jealous and ladies' honors as inexpugnable as in the land of demureness and duennas. Don Guzman took the hint well, maintaining good terms with country gentlemen and their daughters. The cunning soldier of fortune found it advantageous to be intimate with all the ladies he could, preventing Old Salterne from suspecting any peculiar predilection for Mistress Rose.

Nevertheless, Mr. Salterne's parlor remained his most common haunt. While he discoursed for hours about "Antres vast and deserts idle" and cannibals, satisfying Rose's fancy, he seasoned his tales with scraps of mercantile information, keeping the old merchant expectant and eager to invite him again.

His stories were indeed worth hearing. He seemed everywhere: born in Peru, sent home to Spain at ten, raised in Italy, a soldier in the Levant, an adventurer in the East Indies, back in America, then Rome, then Ireland. Shipwrecked, captive among savages, gazing into volcanic craters, fighting Turks, Indians, lions, elephants, and alligators. By thirty-five, he had seen enough for three lives and knew how to make the best of it.

He had shared the horrors of the siege of Famagusta, escaping the victorious Turks who would have flayed or impaled captive officers, avoiding a life as a Janissary. He had witnessed the Battle of the Three Kings, seen Stukely borne down by a hundred lances unconquered even in death, and held upon his knee the head of the dying King of Portugal.

"What have I left on earth, but a heart trampled as hard as the pavement?" he asked Rose one evening. "Whom do I have to love? Who loves me? I have nothing for which to live but fame: and even that is denied to me, a prisoner in a foreign land."

"Have you no kindred, then?" asked pitying Rose.

"My two sisters are in a convent; they had neither money nor beauty, so they are dead to me. My brother is a Jesuit, so he is dead to me. My father fell by the hands of Indians in Mexico; my mother, a penniless widow, is a companion or duenna, carrying fans and lapdogs for some princess in Seville, of no better blood than herself. And I—devil! I have lost even my sword—and so fares the house of De Soto."

Don Guzman intended to be pitied, and he was accordingly. Then he would turn the conversation to Italian stories, tailored to his audience: the pathetic ones when Rose was present, the racy ones when she was absent. Rose wept over the sorrows of Juliet and Desdemona long before they were enacted on an English stage, while the ribs of the Bideford worthies shook to many a jest that Cinthio and Bandello's ghosts must revisit if they wished them remembered.

Before a year had passed, Rose Salterne was far more in love with Don Guzman than he with her. Both suspected each other's minds, though neither hinted at the truth; she from fear, he from sheer Spanish pride of blood. He soon realized he must compromise that blood by marrying the heretic burgher's daughter, or all his labor would be thrown away.

He observed with astonishment and practiced with pleasure the graceful old English fashion of saluting every lady on the cheek at meeting—a custom that often caused scandal to coarse-minded Romish visitors. He saw more than one Bideford burgher, redolent of onions and profane in manner, fume with jealous rage as they kissed the velvet cheek of Rose Salterne.

The spring of 1582–83 brought a sky of bruised gray, curdling high above the jagged black peaks of Ireland. A wind, sharp and dry as a scourge, swept across a desolate expanse of bog, still stained red and yellow by the lingering frost of winter. Upon a solitary brown knoll rising from this waste, a few leafless oaks stretched their moss-grown limbs like the hairy claws of giants, looming over a desolate pool that shivered in the biting breeze. From its edge rose a mournful cry, faint and fitful, swallowed by the howling gale.

Along the bog's crumbling edge, a company of English soldiers pressed forward. Clad in helmets and quilted jerkins, with arquebuses resting on shoulders and pikes trailing behind, they were men hardened by two years of war, having fought at Smerwick and seen countless bloody frays. Two captains rode ahead on shaggy ponies. One, the taller, wore armor stained and rusted by storms and battle; the other, a figure of flamboyant contrast, donned a brilliant inlaid cuirass, a gaudy sash, a plumed helmet, and a sword hilt glittering with gold. Yet his mount was a meager garron, struggling under the weight of his finery. Beside them trotted a bare-legged Irish kerne, his only garment a ragged yellow mantle, his unkempt hair wild and matted. He was their guide in the hunt for the rebel Baltinglas, and woe to him if he betrayed them.

"A pleasant country, truly, Captain Raleigh," remarked the dapper officer to his companion. "I marvel how, having once escaped this mire to the splendor of Whitehall, you possess the courage to return and soil that gay suit with bog-water and mud."

"A very pleasant country, my friend Amyas," Raleigh replied earnestly. "What you say in jest, I say in truth."

"Hillo! Our tastes seem to have swapped places," Amyas countered, his tone weary. "I am already sick of it, just as you predicted. Oh, that I could hear of some adventure Westward-ho! I long to swing in a hammock once more. Pray, what has suddenly made you in love with bog and rock? I thought you had long since spied out the nakedness of this land."

"Bog and rock? Nakedness?" Raleigh laughed, a sound bright against the gloom. "What is needed here is merely prudence, skill, justice, and law. This soil is fertile enough if men would till it. These rocks—think of the minerals they may hold! I hear of gold and jewels found in divers parts. My brother Humphrey's German assayer, Daniel, assures me these stones are akin to those yielding silver in Peru. Tut, man! If Her Gracious Majesty would but grant me a few square miles of this wilderness, in seven years' time, by God's good help, I would make it blossom like a rose."

"Humph!" Amyas sighed. "Then I should be more inclined to stay."

"So you shall, and be my agent," Raleigh insisted, leaning forward. "If you will, you shall collect my mine-rents, my corn-rents, and my fishery-rents. Could you keep accounts, old knight of the bear's-paw?"

"Well enough for such short reckonings as yours would be, on the profit side at least. No, no—I'd sooner carry lime from Cauldy to Bideford all my days than spend another year in the land of Ire among the children of wrath. There is a curse upon the face of the earth, I believe."

"There is no curse save the old one of man's sin: 'Thorns and thistles it shall bring forth to thee,'" Raleigh corrected, speaking with a daring boldness that would later earn him the imputation of atheism. "But if you root up the thorns, Amyas, I know no fiend who can prevent you from growing wheat. If you till the ground like a man, you plough away nature's curse and the fables of the schoolmen."

"It is sword and bullet, I think, that are needed here before plough and harrow," Amyas said grimly. "Until a few more of these Irish lords are gone where the Desmonds are, there is no peace for Ireland."

"Humph! Not so far wrong, I fear. And yet... Irish lords? These very traitors are better English blood than we who hunt them down. When Yeo here slew the Desmond the other day, he shed no more Irish blood than if he had slain the Lord Deputy himself."

"His blood be on his own head," Yeo grunted. "He looked as wild a savage as the worst of them, more shame to him. The ancient here nearly cut off his arm before he told us who he was. And then, your worship, having a price on his head and bleeding to death..."

"Enough, enough, good fellow," Raleigh interrupted gently. "Thou hast done what was given thee to do. Strange, Amyas, is it not? Noble Normans sunk into savages—*Hibernis ipsis hiberniores!* Is there some uncivilizing venom in the air?"

"Some venom, at least, which makes English men traitors. But the Irish themselves are well enough, if their tyrants would let them be. See now, what more faithful liegeman has Her Majesty than Inchiquin, who, they say, is Prince of Thomond and should be king of all Ireland if every man had his right?"

"Don't talk of rights in the land of wrongs, man," Raleigh muttered. "But Inchiquin knows well that the true Irish Esau has no worse enemy than his supplanter, the Norman Jacob. And yet, Amyas, are even these men worse than we might be, if we had been bred up masters over the bodies and souls of men in some remote land where law and order had never come? Look at this Desmond: brought up a savage among savages, a Papist among Papists, a despot among slaves. A thousand easy maidens deem it honor to serve his pleasure; a thousand wild ruffians deem it piety to fulfill his revenge. Let him that is without sin among us cast the first stone."

Amyas fell silent as the conversation drifted. Raleigh's mind turned inward, wrestling with shadows. *What hadst thou been, Raleigh, hadst thou been that Desmond whose lands thou now desirest?* he asked himself. *What wilt thou be when thou hast them? Will thy children sink downwards, as these noble barons sank? Will the genius of tyranny and falsehood find soil within thy heart? What guarantee hast thou for doing better here than those who went before thee? And yet, cannot I do justice and love mercy? Can I not establish plantations, build and sow, and make the desert valleys laugh with corn? Shall I not have my Spenser with me to fill me with noble thoughts? Is not this true knight-errantry, to redeem to peace and use a generous soil and a more generous race? Trustful and tenderhearted they are... and if they be fickle and passionate, will not that very softness of temper make them as easy to be led towards good?*

*Yes,* he thought, *here, away from courts, among a people who should bless me as their benefactor... what golden days might be mine! And yet—is this but another angel's mask from that same cunning fiend, Ambition's stage? And will my house be indeed the house of God, with foundations of loyalty and bulwarks of righteousness, or the house of fame, whose walls are of soap-bubbles and whose floor a sea of glass mingled with fire? I would be good and great. When will the day come when I shall be content to be good, and yet not great, like this simple Leigh, toiling on by my side to do his duty with no more thought for the morrow than the birds of God?*

*Greatness? I have tasted that cup within the last twelve months. Do I not know it is sweet in the mouth but bitter in the belly? Greatness? And was not Essex great? And John of Austria? And Desmond, whose race stood for ages higher than I shall ever hope to climb—castles, lands, slaves by thousands, five hundred gentlemen who vowed to forswear God before they forswore Him, and well have they kept their vow! And now, dead in a turf-hovel, like a coney in a burrow!*

"Leigh, what noise was that?" Raleigh snapped back to reality.

"An Irish howl, I fancied," the ancient replied. "But it came from off the bog. It may be only a plover's cry."

"Something not quite right, sir captain, to my mind," the ancient added. "They have ugly stories here of pucks and banshees. There it was again, wailing just like a woman. They say the banshee cried all night before Desmond was slain."

"Perhaps, then, this one may be crying for Baltinglas; for his turn is likely to come next—not that I believe in such old wives' tales."

"Shamus, my man," Amyas addressed the guide. "Do you hear that cry in the bog?"

The guide put on a stolid face and answered in broken English. "Shamus hear naught. Perhaps... what you call him? Fishing in ta pool."

"An otter, he means, and I believe he is right. Stay, no! Did you not hear it then, Shamus? It was a woman's voice."

"Shamus is shick in his ears ever since Christmas."

"Shamus will go after Desmond if he lies," Amyas warned. "Ancient, we had better send a few men to see what it is. There may be a poor soul taken by robbers, or perhaps starving to death, as I have seen many a one."

"And I too, poor wretches; and by no fault of their own or ours either: but if their lords will fall to quarrelling, and then drive each other's cattle, and waste each other's lands, sir, you know—"

"I know," Amyas said impatiently. "Why dost not take the men, and go?"

"Cry you mercy, noble captain, but—I fear nothing born of woman."

"Well, what of that?" Amyas smiled.

"But these pucks, sir. The wild Irish do say that they haunt the pools. They do no manner of harm when you are coming up to them; but when you are past, sir, they jump on your back like apes, sir—and who can tackle that manner of fiend?"

"Why, then, by thine own showing, ancient," Raleigh interjected, "thou may'st go and see all safely enough. And then, if the puck jumps on thee as thou comest back, just run in with him here, and I'll buy him of thee for a noble; or thou may'st keep him in a cage and make money in London by showing him for a monster."

"Good heavens forefend, Captain Raleigh! But you talk rashly! But if I must, Captain Leigh—"

"Lads, who'll follow me?"

"Thou askest for volunteers, as if thou wert to lead a forlorn hope. Pull away at the usquebaugh, man, and swallow Dutch courage, since thine English is oozed away. Stay, I'll go myself."

"And I with you," Raleigh declared. "As the queen's true knight-errant, I am bound to be behindhand in no adventure. Who knows but we may find a wicked magician just going to cut off the head of some saffron-mantled princess?" He dismounted.

"Oh, sirs, sirs, to endanger your precious—"

"Pooh," said Raleigh. "I wear an amulet, and have a spell of art-magic at my tongue's end, whereby, sir ancient, neither can a ghost see me, nor I see them. Come with us, Yeo, the Desmond-slayer, and we will shame the devil, or be shamed by him."

"He may shame me, sir, but he will never frighten me," quoth Yeo. "But the bog, captains?"

"Tut! Devonshire men, and heath-trotters born, and not know our way over a peat moor!"

And the three strode away.

They splashed and scrambled for some quarter of a mile to the knoll, while the cry grew louder and more distinct.

"That's neither ghost nor otter, sirs, but a true Irish howl, as Captain Leigh said; and I'll warrant Master Shamus knew as much long ago," Yeo observed.

Indeed, they could now hear plainly the *"Ochone, Ochonorie"* of some wild woman. Scrambling over the boulders of the knoll, they came upon her in another minute.

She was a young girl, sluttish and unkempt, yet fair enough. Her only covering was the ample yellow mantle. There she sat upon a stone, tearing her black dishevelled hair, and every now and then throwing up her head to burst into a long, mournful cry, "for all the world," as Yeo noted, "like a dumb four-footed hound, and not a Christian soul."

On her knees lay the head of a man of middle age, clad in the long soutane of a Romish priest. One look at the attitude of his limbs told them he was dead.

The two paused in awe. Raleigh's spirit, susceptible to all poetical images, felt keenly the strangeness of the scene: the bleak and bitter sky, the shapeless bog, the stunted trees, the savage girl alone with the corpse in that utter desolation. As she bent her head over the still face, calling wildly to him who heard her not, and then, utterly unmindful of the intruders, sent up again that dreary wail into the dreary air, they felt a sacred horror that almost made them turn away and leave her unquestioned. But

"Three lords sat drinking late yestreen,
And ere they paid the lawing,
They set a combat them between,
To fight it in the dawing"—Scotch Ballad.

Every soul who knows Bideford knows Bideford Bridge; it is the very navel, the center, and the soul around which the town has organized itself. Just as Edinburgh is defined by its castle, Rome by its Capitol, and Egypt by its pyramids, so Bideford is Bideford by virtue of its bridge. Yet few understand the occult powers that have animated this wondrous structure for five centuries, making it, according to Prince and Fuller, the chief wonder of Devon. It is an inspired bridge, a soul-saving bridge, an alms-giving bridge, an educational bridge, a sentient bridge, and lastly, a dinner-giving bridge. Few know how, when construction began half a mile upstream, invisible hands carried the stones downstream each night until Sir Richard Gurney, the parish parson, beheld a vision of an angel bidding him build where the materials had been transported, for there alone lay a sure foundation amid the shifting sands. Few know how Bishop Grandison of Exeter proclaimed indulgences and spiritual blessings to all who promoted the bridging of that dangerous ford, allowing them to "make the best of both worlds."

Few know, nor do I, that though the bridge's foundation rests on wool, it shakes at the slightest step of a horse; or that though it boasts twenty-three arches, one William Alford carried four bushels of salt water on his back across its entire length for a wager. The bridge is a veritable esquire, bearing arms of a ship and bridge on a plain field, owning lands and tenements in many parishes. With these holdings, it has founded charities, built schools, waged lawsuits, and, most importantly for our tale, provided yearly dinners, maintaining the finest cellar of wines in all Devon.

In the year 1583, all the notabilities of Bideford were invited to one such dinner, alongside Mr. St. Leger of Annery, brother of the Marshal of Munster and of Lady Grenville. He was a worthy and hospitable gentleman who, finding riches a snare, parted with them so freely that he prevented his children from falling into temptation. Between Mr. St. Leger and one of the bridge trustees arose an argument regarding whether a salmon caught below the bridge was superior to one caught above. To settle this weighty question by experiment, Mr. St. Leger vowed that, having received a good dinner from the bridge, he would give one in return. He offered a bet of five pounds that he could produce a salmon from the pool below Annery as firm and flaky as the Appledore one they had just consumed. In the fulness of his heart, he invited the entire company to dine with him at Annery three days later, each bringing a wife or daughter, and Don Guzman was included.

Thus, a mighty feast was held in the great hall at Annery, such as had seldom occurred since Judge Hankford feasted Edward the Fourth there. While guests ate their best and drank their worst, Rose Salterne and Don Guzman pretended not to see one another, watching each other all the more closely. Rose, however, had to be exceedingly careful; her father sat at the table, and directly opposite her were Messrs. William Cary and Arthur St. Leger, lieutenants in Her Majesty's Irish army, recently returned on furlough.

Rose and the Spaniard had not exchanged a word in six months, though they had met often. The Spaniard did not avoid her company, save in her father's house; instead, he carefully obeyed her by appearing unconscious of her presence beyond the stateliest salutations at entry and departure. Yet, whenever he was in her presence, he laid himself out to the utmost advantage, becoming more witty, eloquent, romantic, and full of wonderful tales than ever before. The cunning Don had found his first tactic foiled; he now employed a far more formidable one. First, Rose deserved severe punishment for daring to refuse the love of a Spanish nobleman. What greater punishment could he inflict than withdrawing the honor of his attentions and the sunshine of his smiles? There was conceit in that notion, but also cunning. None knew better than the Spaniard that women, like the world, value a man at his own price; the more he demands, the more they will give.

Now he would put a high price on himself and pique her pride, for she was too accustomed to worship to be won by flattering it. He might have paid attention to someone else, but he was too wise to employ such a coarse method, which might raise indignation or despair but would never bring a woman of worth to his feet. So, quietly and unobtrusively, he showed her he could do without her. She, poor fool, began to ask herself why? What hidden treasure or reserve force made him independent of her while she could not say the same of him? Did he possess a secret? How pleasant to know it! Some huge ambition? Some mysterious knowledge? Some capacity of love beyond the common? He must be greater, wiser, richer-hearted, and better-born. Ah, if his wealth would but supply her poverty! Step by step, she was being led to sue in forma pauperis to the very man she had spurned when he sued in like form to her. The temptation of possessing some mysterious private treasure, of being the priestess of a hidden sanctuary, and of thanking Heaven for not being like other women, became too much for Rose, as it is for most. For none knew better than the Spaniard how much more fond women are, by the very law of their sex, of worshipping than of being worshipped, and of obeying than of being obeyed; how their coyness and scorn are often masks hiding their consciousness of weakness, masks of which they themselves will often tire.

Rose was utterly tired of that mask as she sat at the Annery table that day. Don Guzman saw it in her uneasy, downcast looks. Thinking (conceited coxcomb) that she must be sufficiently punished, he stole glances at her, unashamed when he saw her drop her eyes upon meeting his. He noted her silence and abstraction increasing, and a blush stealing into her cheeks. So he pretended to be as downcast and abstracted as she was, continuing his glances until he found her looking at him to see if he was looking at her. Then he knew his prey was safe. He asked her with his eyes, "Do you forgive me?" and saw her stop dead in her talk, falter, drop her eyes, and raise them again after a minute in search of his, so he might repeat the pleasant question. Then what could she do but answer with all her face and every bend of her pretty neck, "And do you forgive me in turn?"

Whereupon Don Guzman broke out jubilant, like a nightingale on a bough, with story, jest, and repartee; he became the soul of the company and the most charming of all cavaliers. Poor Rose knew she was the cause of his sudden change of mood, blamed herself for what she had done, shuddered and blushed at her own delight, and longed for the feast to end so she might hurry home to hide herself alone with sweet fancies about a love whose reality she dared not face.

It was a beautiful sight, the great terrace at Annery that afternoon. Smart dames in gaudy dresses paraded in twos and threes before the stately house or looked down upon the park, with its old oaks, deer, and broad land-locked river spread out like a lake beneath, all bright in the glare of the midsummer sun. They listened obsequiously to the two great ladies who did the honors, Mrs. St. Leger the hostess, and her sister-in-law, fair Lady Grenville. All chatted, laughed, eyed each other's dresses, and gossiped about husbands and servants; only Rose Salterne kept apart, longing to get into a corner to laugh or cry, she knew not which.

"Our pretty Rose seems sad," said Lady Grenville, coming up to her. "Cheer up, child! We want you to come and sing to us."

Rose answered she knew not what and obeyed mechanically.

She took the lute and sat on a bench beneath the house while the rest grouped around her.

"What shall I sing?"

"Let us have your old song, 'Earl Haldan's Daughter.'"

Rose shrank from it. It was a loud, dashing ballad that chimed little with her thoughts; Frank had praised it in happier days long gone by. She thought of him and others, of her pride and carelessness, and the song seemed ominous. Yet, for that very reason, she dared not refuse, fearing suspicion where none existed, so she began per force.

"It was Earl Haldan's daughter, She look'd across the sea; She look'd
across the water, And long and loud laugh'd she; 'The locks of six
princesses Must be my marriage-fee, So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny
boat! Who comes a wooing me?'

"It was Earl Haldan's daughter, She walk'd along the sand; When she was
aware of a knight so fair, Come sailing to the land. His sails were all
of velvet, His mast of beaten gold, And 'hey bonny boat, and ho bonny
boat! Who saileth here so bold?'

"'The locks of five princesses I won beyond the sea; I shore their
golden tresses, To fringe a cloak for thee. One handful yet is wanting,
But one of all the tale; So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat! Furl up
thy velvet sail!'

"He leapt into the water, That rover young and bold; He gript Earl
Haldan's daughter, He shore her locks of gold; 'Go weep, go weep, proud
maiden, The tale is full to-day. Now hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Sail Westward-ho, and away!'"

As she ceased, a measured voice with a foreign accent thrilled through her.

"In the East, they say the nightingale sings to the rose; Devon, more
happy, has nightingale and rose in one."

"We have no nightingales in Devon, Don Guzman," said Lady Grenville; "but our little forest thrushes sing, as you hear, sweetly enough to content any ear. But what brings you away from the gentlemen so early?"

"These letters," said he, "which have just been put into my hand; and as they call me home to Spain, I was loath to lose a moment of that delightful company from which I must part so soon."

"To Spain?" asked half-a-dozen voices; for the Don was a general favorite.

"Yes, and thence to the Indies. My ransom has arrived, and with it the promise of an office. I am to be Governor of La Guayra in Caracas. Congratulate me on my promotion."

A mist was over Rose's eyes. The Spaniard's voice was hard and flippant. Did he care for her, after all? And if he did, was it nevertheless hopeless? How her cheeks glowed! Everybody must see it! Anything to turn away their attention from her, and in that nervous haste which makes people speak foolishly, she asked—

"And where is La Guayra?"

"Half round the world, on the coast of the Spanish Main. The loveliest place on earth, and the loveliest governor's house, in a forest of palms at the foot of a mountain eight thousand feet high: I shall only want a wife there to be in paradise."

"I don't doubt that you may persuade some fair lady of Seville to accompany you thither," said Lady Grenville.

"Thanks, gracious madam: but the truth is, that since I have had the bliss of knowing English ladies, I have begun to think that they are the only ones on earth worth wooing."

"A thousand thanks for the compliment; but I fear none of our free English maidens would like to submit to the guardianship of a duenna. Eh, Rose? how should you like to be kept under lock and key all day by an ugly old woman with a horn on her forehead?"

Poor Rose turned so scarlet that Lady Grenville knew her secret on the spot and would have tried to turn the conversation. But before she could speak, some burgher's wife blundered out a commonplace about the jealousy of Spanish husbands; and another, to make matters better, giggled out something more true than delicate about West Indian masters and fair slaves.

"Ladies," said Don Guzman, reddening, "believe me that these are but the calumnies of ignorance. If we be more jealous than other nations, it is because we love more passionately. If some of us abroad are profligate, it is because they, poor men, have no helpmate, which, like the amethyst, keeps its wearer pure. I could tell you stories, ladies, of the constancy and devotion of Spanish husbands, even in the Indies, as strange as ever romancer invented."

"Can you? Then we challenge you to give us one at least."

"I fear it would be too long, madam."

"The longer the more pleasant, senor. How can we spend an hour better this afternoon, while the gentlemen within are finishing their wine?"

Story-telling, in those old times when books were rarer than now, was a common amusement. As the Spaniard's accomplishments in that line were well known, all the ladies crowded round him; the servants brought chairs and benches; and Don Guzman, taking his seat in the midst with a proud humility at Lady Grenville's feet, began.

"Your perfections, fair and illustrious ladies, must doubtless have heard, ere now, how Sebastian Cabota, some forty-five years ago, sailed forth with a commission from my late master, the Emperor Charles the Fifth, to discover the golden lands of Tarshish, Ophir, and Cipango; but

"The spirits of your fathers shall start from every wave; for the deck it was their field of fame, and ocean was their grave."

Thus spake Campbell, and the words hung heavy in the air of Mrs. Hawkins's study. The room was a testament to the new wealth of Plymouth, panelled in oak and adorned with gilding, Spanish silver vessels gleaming on the sideboard, and strange skins and charts hanging like maps of a conquered world. Above the fireplace, where the portrait of the old Captain Will Hawkins watched, fluttered the Spanish ensign taken at Rio de la Hacha—a trophy of violence that seemed to mock the peace the room otherwise tried to maintain.

In this chamber sat Adrian Gilbert, a tall man whose broad forehead was deeply wrinkled by the fires of the laboratory, and Mrs. Hawkins, once comely, now aged and worn by the sorrows of a sailor's wife.

"So you see, my dear Mrs. Hawkins," Adrian began, his voice trembling slightly with the fever of his ambition, "having the silver beside the ores of lead, manganese, and copper—and above all this gossan, which I suspect is the very crude form of all metals—if my recipes from Doctor Dee succeed even half as well as I hope, I shall refine out the luna, lay it by, and transmute the remaining ores into sol, gold. Whereupon Peru and Mexico become superfluities, and England the mistress of the globe. Strange, no doubt; distant, no doubt: but possible, my dear madam, possible!"

"And what good to you if it be, Mr. Gilbert?" Mrs. Hawkins replied, her voice soft but firm. "If you could find a philosopher's stone to turn sinners into saints, now—naught save God's grace can do that; and that last seems ofttimes over long in coming." She sighed, a sound that carried the weight of nine years of waiting.

"But indeed, my dear madam, conceive now. The Comb Martin mine thus becomes a gold mine, perhaps inexhaustible; yields me wherewithal to carry out my North-West patent. Meanwhile, my brother Humphrey holds Newfoundland, and builds me fresh ships year by year—for the forests of pine are boundless—for my China voyage."

"Sir Humphrey has better thoughts in his dear heart than gold, Mr. Adrian; a very close and gracious walker he has been these seven years. I wish my Captain John were so too."

"And how do you know I have naught better in my mind's eye than gold? Or, indeed, what better could I have? Is not gold the Spaniard's strength—the very mainspring of Antichrist? By gold only, therefore, can we out-wrestle him. You shake your head, but say, dear madam (for gold England must have), which is better: to make gold bloodlessly at home, or take it bloodily abroad?"

"Oh, Mr. Gilbert, Mr. Gilbert! Is it not written that those who make haste to be rich pierce themselves through with many sorrows? Oh, Mr. Gilbert! God's blessing is not on it all."

"Not on you, madam? Be sure that brave Captain John Hawkins's star told me a different tale when I cast his nativity for him. Born under stormy planets, truly, but under right royal and fortunate ones."

"Ah, Mr. Adrian! I am a simple body, and you a great philosopher, but I hold there is no star for the seaman like the Star of Bethlehem; and that goes with 'peace on earth and good will to men,' and not with such arms as that, Mr. Adrian. I can't abide to look upon them."

She pointed upward to one of the bosses of the ribbed oak roof, where was emblazoned the fatal crest Clarencieux Hervey had granted years before to her husband: *The Demi-Moor proper, bound.*

"Ah, Mr. Gilbert! Since first he went to Guinea after those poor negroes, little lightness has my heart known. And the very day that that crest was put up in our grand new house, as the parson read the first lesson, there was this text in it: 'Woe to him that buildeth his house by iniquity, and his chambers by wrong. Shalt thou live because thou closest thyself in cedar?' It went into my ears like fire, Mr. Gilbert, and into my heart like lead.
