And when the parson went on, 'Did not thy father eat and drink, and do judgment and justice? Then it was well with him,' I thought of good old Captain Will; and—I tell you, Mr. Gilbert, those negroes are on my soul from morning until night! We are all mighty grand now, and money comes in fast, but the Lord will require the blood of them at our hands yet, He will!"

"My dearest madam, who can prosper more than you? If your husband copied the Dons too closely once or twice in the matter of those negroes—which I do not deny—was he not punished at once when he lost ships, men, all but life, at St. Juan d'Ulloa?"

"Ay, yes," she said, "and that did give me a bit of comfort, especially when the Queen—God save her tender heart!—was so sharp with him for pity of the poor wretches. But it has not mended him. He is growing fast like the rest now, Mr. Gilbert, greedy to win, and niggardly to spend (God forgive him!), and always fretting and plotting for some new gain, and envying and grudging at Drake, and all who are deeper in the snare of prosperity than he is. Gold, gold, nothing but gold in every mouth—there it is! Ah! I mind when Plymouth was a quiet little God-fearing place as God could smile upon: but ever since my John, and Sir Francis, and poor Mr. Oxenham found out the way to the Indies, it's been a sad place. Not a sailor's wife but is crying 'Give, give,' like the daughters of the horse-leech; and every woman must drive her husband out across seas to bring her home money to squander on hoods and farthingales, and go mincing with outstretched necks and wanton eyes; and they will soon learn to do worse than that, for the sake of gain. But the Lord's hand will be against their tires and crisping-pins, their mufflers and farthingales, as it was against the Jews of old. Ah, dear me!"

The two sat in silence for a moment, the weight of the future pressing down upon them. Adrian, half ashamed of his own ambitious dreams, looked at her with a mixture of pity and awe.

"The Lord be with you, dearest lady. Strange, how you women sit at home to love and suffer, while we men rush forth to break our hearts and yours against rocks of our own seeking! Ah well! Were it not for Scripture, I should have thought that Adam, rather than Eve, had been the one who plucked the fruit of the forbidden tree."

"We women, I fear; did the deed nevertheless; for we bear the doom of it our lives long."

"You always remind me, madam, of my dear Mrs. Leigh of Burrough, and her counsels."

"Do you see her often? I hear of her as one of the Lord's most precious vessels."

"I would have done more ere now than see her," said he with a blush, "had she allowed me: but she lives only for the memory of her husband and the fame of her noble sons."

As he spoke, the door opened, and in walked, wrapped in his rough sea-gown, none other than one of those said noble sons.

Adrian turned pale.

"Amyas Leigh! What brings you hither? How fares my brother? Where is the ship?"

"Your brother is well, Mr. Gilbert. The Golden Hind is gone on to Dartmouth, with Mr. Hayes. I came ashore here, meaning to go north to Bideford, ere I went to London. I called at Drake's just now, but he was away."

"The Golden Hind? What brings her home so soon?"

"Yet welcome ever, sir," said Mrs. Hawkins. "This is a great surprise, though. Captain John did not look for you till next year."

Amyas was silent.

"Something is wrong!" cried Adrian. "Speak!"

Amyas tried, but could not.

"Will you drive a man mad, sir? Has the adventure failed? You said my brother was well."

"He is well."

"Then what? Why do you look at me in that fashion, sir?" Springing up, Adrian rushed forward and held the candle to Amyas's face.

Amyas's lip quivered as he laid his hand on Adrian's shoulder. "Your great and glorious brother, sir, is better bestowed than in settling Newfoundland."

"Dead?" shrieked Adrian.

"He is with the God whom he served!"

"He was always with Him, like Enoch: parable me no parables, if you love me, sir!"

"And, like Enoch, he was not; for God took him."

Adrian clasped his hands over his forehead and leaned against the table. "Go on, sir, go on. God will give me strength to hear all."

Gradually, Amyas opened to Adrian that tragic story which Mr. Hayes had long ago told far too well to allow a second edition. He spoke of the unruliness of the men, ruffians caught up at hap-hazard; of conspiracies to carry off the ships, the plunder of fishing vessels, and desertions multiplying daily. Licenses from the general to the lazy and fearful to return home had turned the fleet into a collection of cowards.

"From him? Conspired against him? Deserted from him? Dotards, buzzards! Where would they have found such another leader?" Adrian broke out with a groan.

"Your illustrious brother, sir," said Amyas, "if you will pardon me, was a very great philosopher, but not so much of a general."

"General, sir? Where was braver man?"

"Not on God's earth, but that does not make a general, sir. If Cortez had been brave and no more, Mexico would have been Mexico still. The truth is, sir, Cortez, like my Captain Drake, knew when to hang a man; and your great brother did not."

Amyas was right. Gilbert was a man who could be angry enough at baseness or neglect, but who was too kindly to punish it; he could form the wisest and best-digested plans, but he could not stoop to that hail-fellow-well-met drudgery among his subordinates which has been the talisman of great captains.

Then Amyas went on to tell the rest of his story. They had set sail from St. John's to discover the southward coast. Sir Humphrey, in a chivalrous determination to prove his courage, had gone in the little *Squirrel* of only ten tons—"overcharged with nettings, fights, and small ordnance"—not only because she was more fit to examine the creeks, but because he had heard of some taunt among the men that he was afraid of the sea.

After that, woe on woe. Seven days after they left Cape Raz, their largest ship, the *Delight*, after she had "most part of the night" continued in sounding of trumpets, drums, and fifes, like the swan that singeth before her death, struck the next day upon unknown shoals. There were lost all but fourteen, and among them Frank's philosopher friend, poor Budaeus. Those who escaped, after all horrors of cold and famine, were cast on shore in Newfoundland. Worn out with hunger and want of clothes, the crews of the two remaining ships persuaded Sir Humphrey to sail toward England on the 31st of August. On "that very instant, even in winding about," they beheld close alongside "a very lion in shape, hair, and color, not swimming, but sliding on the water, with his whole body; who passed along, turning his head to and fro, yawning and gaping wide, with ugly demonstration of long teeth and glaring eyes; and to bid us farewell (coming right against the Hind) he sent forth a horrible voice, roaring or bellowing as doth a lion."

"And the devil it was, doubtless," said Adrian, "the roaring lion who goes about seeking whom he may devour."

"He has not got your brother, at least," quoth Amyas.

"No," rejoined Mrs. Hawkins, a smile not on her lips but in her eyes, for those were days in which men believed in the devil; "he roared for joy to think how many poor souls would be left still in heathen darkness by Sir Humphrey's death. God be with that good knight, and send all mariners where he is now!"

Then Amyas told the last scene. When they were off the Azores, the storms came on heavier than ever, with "terrible seas, breaking short and pyramid-wise," till, on the 9th of September, the tiny *Squirrel* nearly foundered and yet recovered. "And the general, sitting abaft with a book in his hand, cried out to us in the Hind so oft as we did approach within hearing, 'We are as near heaven by sea as by land,' reiterating the same speech, well beseeming a soldier resolute in Jesus Christ, as I can testify he was."

"The same Monday, about twelve of the clock, or not long after, the frigate (the *Squirrel*) being ahead of us in the Golden Hind, suddenly her lights were out; and withal our watch cried, the general was cast away, which was true; for in that moment the frigate was devoured and swallowed up of the sea."

And so ended Amyas Leigh's story.

"Oh, my brother! My brother!" moaned poor Adrian. "The glory of his house, the glory of Devon!"

"Ah! What will the Queen say?" asked Mrs. Hawkins through her tears.

"Tell me," asked Adrian, "had he the jewel on when he died?"

"The

"Ignorance and evil, even in full flight, deal terrible backhanded strokes at their pursuers."

It was no safe matter in those days to travel from Plymouth northward through Devon. Unless one wished to circle many miles, the road led straight through the domain of a foreign potentate, a man who had ravaged the realm and defeated the forces of Queen Elizabeth. Behind his back, at least, he was known as the King of the Gubbings. As Fuller noted, they were secured by distance, for those of more valor dared not face them openly for fear of their fury. The name itself was a mark of shame; "gubbings" were the shavings of fish, worthless and despised, and the inhabitants of this Scythia within England seemed to sense the disgrace attached to their title. They lived in cots that were more holes than houses, multiplying without marriage, their language a dross of vulgar Devonian that the more learned found hardest to understand. They were a peculiar breed, exempt from all authority, living by theft and outrunning horses, holding together like bees to revenge any wrong done to one of their own. Yet, rumors circulated that they were beginning to be civilized, offering their children for baptism.

Amyas Leigh, clad in his full suit of armor and armed with lance, sword, and two great dags, rode out of Plymouth with Salvation Yeo and a handful of north Devon men returning from Ireland. They pushed on as fast as they could, aiming to reach Lydford before nightfall, but delays with their horses meant they arrived at the frontier just as the sun dipped below the wild heath. The landscape was a dreary tableland banked by the crags of Dartmoor and sloping toward the great cone of Brent-Tor, an extinct volcano crowned by a tiny church vowed by a distressed merchant. From the waste slopes, tiny threads of blue smoke rose from the dens of the Gubbings, and the travelers halted frequently, mistaking distant furze-bushes and ponies for the patrols of an advancing army.

Their destination was a wayside inn, a desolate lump of lichen-spotted granite with paper-patched windows and rotting thatch. At the door stood the innkeeper, a brawny, sodden-visaged brute holding up his hose for lack of points, his head adorned with feathers that testified to a night spent sheep-stealing. He spotted Amyas's party, counted them, and seeing the armor, let out a sleepy howl. His wife, Mary, a strapping lass in a green bodice and red petticoat, emerged with a fishing rod, complaining that the "dun fly" had been eaten. The host, enraged that his catch was gone, struck her, and she returned the blow with good effect before he shuffled off to summon his accomplice, Patrico.

Inside, the inn was a "mucksy sort of a place," but the travelers preferred it to bivouacking near the enemy. The host's missing catch was roasted alongside "black Dartmoor mutton," which, under the pressure of the gridiron, confessed itself to be red-deer venison. Amyas secured his horses in a shed lacking lock or key, while Yeo posted himself at the door. Soon, Yeo returned in high excitement, reporting that a gentleman had entered the back way. When Amyas asked him to invite the stranger, Yeo explained that the man had slipped away, only to return moments later.

"He's gone in back ways, by the court," Yeo reported.
"Well, he has an odd taste," Amyas replied.

Yeo returned once more, breathless. "Come out, sir! I've got him. Safe as a rat in a trap!"
"Who?"
"A Jesuit, sir."
"Nonsense, man!"
"I tell you truth, sir. I saw him turn in his toes, put down his feet so carefully as if afraid of offending God at every step. He called 'Mary!' quite still, and she flew out ready to eat him. He gave her a queer cuffin'—that's a justice in these canters' thieves' Latin—and she kissed it, crossed herself, and put it in her bosom. Then he slipped to the stable, peeped in, and went in when he saw no one. I shut the door and backed a cart against it."

"Fool's trick, man! How do you know he is not some honest gentleman?"
"Honest gentlemen don't give maidens Agnuses," Yeo insisted. "I've put him in. If you want him let out, you must do it yourself, for my conscience is against it. 'If any let one of them go, his life shall be for the life of him.'"

Amyas hurried out, apologizing to his cousin Eustace, who sat disconsolately in the stable. Eustace, with a countenance half foolish, half venomous, looked like a fox trapped by terriers. After a tense exchange where Eustace claimed he was bound to know no prudence save for the Lord's work, and Amyas accused him of hunting down Rose Salterne as an outlaw, the two cousins found common ground over supper. They spoke guardedly at first, but Amyas's straightforward kindness thawed Eustace's frozen heart, and they drifted into talk of old haunts and boyhood memories.

Yet, beneath the surface, the tension remained. Eustace asked why Amyas was going to Bideford while Frank and his mother were in London. Amyas admitted he could not rest until he heard the whole story about Rose Salterne. Eustace shrieked, realizing he had betrayed himself, and paced the room in despair, clutching at empty air. He knew of the Brotherhood of the Rose, and when Amyas pressed him about meeting a Spaniard at Lundy, Eustace refused to answer, warning that Amyas was within a few miles of those who would obey him.

Amyas, though angry, restrained himself, knowing that capturing Eustace would bring certain death upon him. He dismissed his cousin, telling him to go to the devil, and turned his attention to the men. They settled for the night, but at midnight, Amyas was awakened by the sound of flying dragons—terror-stricken geese—and the roar of a fight in the courtyard.

Yeo was holding his own against a dozen men, sword and buckler in hand, while the entire household screamed in chaos. The ruffians fled over a low hedge, leaving two wounded behind. One was a huge man pierced through the body; the other lay dead in the straw. Yeo's story revealed that he had expected a night-attack on the horses, suspecting Eustace's involvement, and had taken lodging in the stable. The attackers had been led by the innkeeper, who had slipped away to call neighboring outlaws to the spoil.

Before they could secure the scene, a fresh hubbub arose from the kitchen. Mr. Evan Morgans, alias Father Parsons, stood foaming with rage, captured by sturdy sailors, with Eustace Leigh standing behind him. Parsons claimed he was preparing his "wounded lamb" for the account to which Yeo's cruelty had sent him. The dying man, Ballard, the leader of the Gubbings, lay on the floor, moaning for Patrico. Parsons, kneeling beside him, listened to the thief's gibberish confession, while Eustace interpreted the thieves' dialect into Latin.

"It's a false peace," Yeo protested, watching the soul perish before his eyes.
"At all events, he is confessing his sins," Amyas argued, holding Yeo back. "If that's not good for him, what is?"

But the dark catalogue of brutal deeds had been gasped out, and ere the words of absolution could follow, the head fell back, and all was over. Parsons declared that confession in extremis was sufficient, while Yeo insisted the man's soul was lost. Amyas, furious, ordered Parsons to accompany them, threatening to shoot him if he delayed their journey. Reluctantly, Parsons agreed, and Eustace volunteered to carry the message to the remaining Gubbings.

They marched through the mire toward Okehampton, with Parsons tied to Amyas's saddle. Yeo walked alongside, mocking the Jesuit with tales of bogs and martyrdom, his laughter echoing under the moon as they approached Lydford Castle. Once across the threshold, Yeo and Simon Evans stopped, claiming they had saved the Jesuit's fine clothes. They had hove the man over the bank into a furze-bush, leaving him to his fate, for a Jesuit, Yeo coolly remarked, had as many lives as a cat.

Leaving Lydford and its ill-omened castle behind, Amyas and his party trudged on toward Okehampton. The voice of the Torridge stream recalled the nymph's words to him, and his mind wandered to Rose Salterne. He had never seen her since that day six years prior, and the chain of petty accidents that kept them apart seemed cruel. He wondered if his old oath meant anything more than a tavern frolic, but as he looked at it, he felt it was a sacred duty. He had nothing but his sword, yet he vowed to find the man who had ruined her, for if there was no law on earth, there was law in heaven.

He rode into the ugly, dirty town of Okehampton, detained by Justice Shallow, who refused to take depositions without a brother justice present. The wrangling nearly exposed the presence of Jesuits in the Moorland Utopia, but Shallow, citing the proverb "leave ill alone," dismissed the matter. Amyas continued his weary journey until he saw the broad shining river and the white houses of Bideford.

When he arrived at Sir Richard's door, he found the knight still in Ireland and Lady Grenville at Stow. Disappointed, he returned to the Ship Tavern, where the host chattered about the emptiness of the town and the strange doings among the ladies. Amyas, disturbed, asked about the old man, Mr. Salterne. The host described him as a changed man, sitting up all night in Rose's room, turning over her garments, and refusing to drink.

Amyas went to see Salterne himself. The old merchant opened the door with stern courtesy, welcoming Amyas into his lonely house. He led him to the parlor, where he ordered the apprentices to bring food and wine, his sudden hilarity contrasting sharply with the host's earlier description. He fed Amyas like a fighting-cock, boasting of his own past voyages and his ambition to have married his daughter to an earl.

Then, Salterne led Amyas upstairs to a room untouched since Rose's departure. The bed was made, the books arranged, the dressing-table set with pins and rings, and the dolls lined up in a cupboard. Salterne opened a chest filled with necklaces, jewels, furs, and cloth of gold, treasures gathered over twenty years for her wedding day.

"And whom do you think I kept all these for?" Salterne whispered, his voice trembling. "For her wedding-day. For your wedding-day, if you'd been minded, sir! Yes, yours, sir! And yet, I believe, I was so ambitious that I would not have let her marry under an earl... Ah, well! There was my idol, sir. I made her mad, I pampered her up with gewgaws and vanity; and then, because my idol was just what I had made her, I turned again and rent her."

He pointed to the open chest and the empty bed. "That was what I meant; and that was what God meant. Never mind. Come downstairs and finish your wine. I see you don't care about it all. Why should you! you are not her father, and you may thank God you are not. Go, and be merry while you can, young sir! . . . And yet, all this might have been yours. And--but I don't suppose you are one to be won by money--but all this may be yours still, and twenty thousand pounds to boot."

"I want no money, sir, but what I can earn with my own sword."
"Earn my money, then!" Salterne demanded, clutching Amyas's arm. "To keep your oath."
"My oath! How did you know that I had one?"
"Ah! you were well ashamed of it, I suppose, next day! A drunken frolic all about a poor merchant's daughter! But there is nothing hidden that shall not be revealed."

Salterne revealed that he had brought John Brimblecombe to him, showing him the room and the treasure, and that the clown had wept and prayed until Salterne fell to his knees. "Bring me home that man's head, and take ship, prizes--all! Keep the gain, sir, and give me the revenge!"

Amyas was silent, the weight of his mother's permission pressing on him. "I dare not go without her leave," he said.
"I dare not, sir; I must obey my parent, whatever else I do," Salterne replied, his voice heavy with sorrow.

Amyas departed to the inn, where he found Cary waiting for him, ready to share details that would be told in another chapter. For now, the night deepened, and the path ahead remained uncertain, but the vow was sealed.

"The King of Spain is a foul pagan,
And lives upon Mahound;
And pity it were that lady fair
Should marry a heathen hound."

—*King Estmere*

Six weeks after the duel, the miller of Stow arrived at the great house in deep distress, requesting the loan of the bloodhounds. Rose Salterne had vanished into the night, her whereabouts unknown to all. Sir Richard was away in Bideford, yet the old steward, Anthony, took it upon himself to summon the keepers. Down they went to the mill, accompanied by the idle lads of the parish, who viewed the maiden-hunt as excellent sport and approached the case with a bias heavily favoring Rose.

They reviled the miller and his wife as hard-hearted heathens, convinced that the poor maid had been driven to throw herself from a cliff or drown in the sea. Conversely, the women of Stow held a unanimous opinion that the hussy had "gone off" with some disreputable fellow, predicting that pride would inevitably meet its fall.

The facts of the matter were stark: all of Rose's trinkets remained behind, suggesting she had departed honestly, save for some missing linen, which Old Anthony hinted might be found in another's box. The only tangible clue was a small footprint beneath her bedroom window. Upon this, the bloodhound was placed (naturally, on a leash). After a premonitory whimper, he lifted his mighty voice and started, bell-mouthed, through the garden gate and up the lane, towing the panting keeper behind him until they reached the downs above. There, they headed straight for Marslandmouth, where the entire posse comitatus pulled up, breathless, at the door of Lucy Passmore.

Lucy, as I should have noted earlier, was now a widow and found her widowhood not entirely contrary to her interests. Her augury regarding her old man had been fulfilled; he had never returned since the night he set sail with Eustace and the Jesuits.

"Some natural tears she shed, but dried them soon," as many of them did, at least those not required for business; and then, determined to prevent suspicion by a bold move, she set off for Stow. She told Lady Grenville a most pathetic tale: how her husband had gone out pollock fishing and never returned, yet how she had heard horsemen galloping past her window in the dead of night. She was certain they were Jesuits who had carried off her old man by main force, likely killing and salting him down for provisions on their voyage back to the Pope in Rome. She concluded by entreating protection against those "Popish skulkers up to Chapel," sworn to do her mischief, and appealing to Lady Grenville's sense of justice regarding whether the Queen ought not to grant her a pension for having had her heart's love turned into a sainted martyr by the hands of idolatrous traitors.

Lady Grenville, who held Lucy in high regard for her medical skills and always summoned her when a child suffered a "housty" (sore throat), went forth and pleaded the case before Sir Richard with such effect that Lucy was, for the next two or three years, better off than ever. But now—what did she have to do with Rose's disappearance? And where was she herself? Her door was fastened, and around it her flock of goats stood, crying in vain for her to milk them; while from the downs above, her donkeys, wandering at their own sweet will, answered the bloodhound's bay with a burst of harmony.

"They're laughing at us, keeper, they neddies; sure enough, we've lost our labor here."

But the bloodhound, after pacing the door for a while, turned down the glen and never stopped until he reached the sea's edge.

"They've taken water. Let's go back and ransack the old witch's house."

"'Tis just like that old Lucy to lock a poor maid into shame."

Returning, they attacked the cottage. By a general plebiscite, they ransacked the little dwelling, partly in indignation and partly, if the truth be told, in the hope of plunder. Yet there was none. Lucy had decamped with all her movable wealth, saving only the huge black cat among the embers. At the sight of the bloodhound, the cat vanished up the chimney (some said with a strong smell of brimstone) and, viewed from outside, was chased into the woods, where she doubtless lived many happy years as a scourge to all the rabbits of the glen.

The goats and donkeys were driven off to Stow, and the mob returned, slightly ashamed of their brief wrath and a little afraid of what Sir Richard might say. He, upon his return, sold the donkeys and goats, giving the money to the poor, promising to refund it if Lucy returned and surrendered to justice. But Lucy did not return. Her cottage, from which the neighbors shrank as from a haunted place, remained as she left it, crumbling slowly into four fern-covered walls. Through these, the little stream murmured on from pool to pool—the only voice, for many years to come, breaking the silence of that lonely glen.

A few days later, Sir Richard, on his way from Bideford to Stow, stopped at Clovelly Court. He mentioned, with a casual "by the by," news that made Will Cary leap from his seat almost to the ceiling. What it was we know already.

"And there is no clue?" asked old Cary, for his son was speechless.

"Only this," replied Sir Richard. "I hear that a fellow prowling the cliffs that night saw a pinnace running for Lundy."

Will rose and hurried out of the room. Within half an hour, he and three or four armed servants were on a trawling skiff, heading for Lundy. He did not return for three days, bringing news that an elderly man, seemingly a foreigner, had lodged for months in a part of the ruined Moresco Castle tenanted by John Braund. A few weeks prior, a younger foreigner had joined him from a ship—a Flushinger or Easterling of some sort. The ship came and went more than once, carrying the young man. A few days ago, a lady and her maid, a stout woman, had arrived with him, spoken to the elder man in secret, stayed all night, and then all three sailed in the morning. Fishermen on the beach had heard the young man call the other "father." He was a very still man, much like a mass-priest might be. They knew no more, or chose not to.

Upon this, old Cary and Sir Richard sent Will on a second trip with the parish constable of Hartland (a huge parish wherein, for its sins, lies the Isle of Lundy, ten miles out at sea). He returned with the body of the hapless John Braund, farmer, fisherman, and smuggler. This worthy, after much fruitless examination (during which the examinee was afflicted with extreme deafness and loss of memory), was sent to Exeter Gaol on a charge of "harboring priests, Jesuits, gypsies, and other suspect and traitorous persons."

Poor John Braund, whose motive for entertaining these ugly customers was probably not treason but a wife, seven children, and arrears of rent, did not thrive under the change from the pure air of Lundy to the pestiferous air of Exeter Gaol. This prison, made infamous two years later (if I recollect right) by a "black assizes" nearly as fatal as the notorious one at Oxford, saw judge, jury, counsel, and bystanders—including many members of Devon's best families—sicken in court and die miserably within days, whether by the stench of prisoners or a stream of foul air.

John Braund took the gaol-fever in a week and died raving in that noisome den. His secret, if he had one, perished with him, leaving nothing but vague suspicion regarding Rose Salterne's fate. That she had gone off with the Spaniard was doubted by few; but whither, and in what capacity? On that last subject, no mercy was shown to her by many a Bideford dame who had hated the poor girl simply for her beauty, nor by many a country lady who had "always expected that the girl would be brought to ruin by the absurd notice, beyond what her station had a right to, which was taken of her," while every young maiden aspired to fill the throne Rose had abdicated. Thus, on the whole, Bideford considered itself going on as well without poor Rose as it had done with her, or even better. Though she lingered in some hearts as a fair dream, the business and bustle of each day soon swept that dream away, and her place knew her no more.

And Will Cary?

For a while, he was like a man distracted. He heaped himself with superfluous reproaches for having, as he claimed, first brought Rose into disgrace and then driven her into the arms of the Spaniard. St. Leger, a sensible man, tried in vain to persuade him that the fault was not his; that the two must have been attached long before the quarrel; that it must have ended so, sooner or later; that Old Salterne's harshness, rather than Cary's wrath, had hastened the catastrophe; and finally, that Rose and her fortunes, now that she had eloped with a Spaniard, were not worth troubling their heads about. Poor Will would not be so comforted. He wrote to Frank at Whitehall, telling him the whole truth, calling himself all fools and villains, and entreating Frank's forgiveness.

He received an answer in which Frank said Will had no reason to accuse himself; that these strange attachments were due to a *synastria*, or sympathy of the stars, ruling the destinies of each person, to fight against which was to fight against the heavens themselves. That he, as Rose's brother, was bound to believe, nay, to assert at the sword's point if need be, that the incomparable Rose of Torridge could make none but a worthy and virtuous choice; and that to the man whom she had honored by her affection was due, Spaniard and Papist though he might be, all friendship, worship, and loyal faith forevermore.

And honest Will took it all for gospel, little dreaming what agony of despair, what fearful suspicions, what bitter prayers, this letter had cost the gentle heart of Francis Leigh.

He showed the letter triumphantly to St. Leger, who was quite wise enough to gainsay no word of it aloud, yet quite wise enough to believe in secret that Frank viewed the matter in quite a different light. However, he contented himself with saying, "The man is an angel as his mother is!" and the matter dropped for a few days.

Then came forward one who had no mind to let it drop: Jack Brimblecombe, now curate of Hartland town and "passing rich on forty pounds a year."

"I hope no offense, Mr. William; but when are you and the rest going after... after her?" The name stuck in his throat.

Cary was taken aback.

"What's that to thee, Catiline the blood-drinker?" he asked, trying to laugh it off.

"What? Don't laugh at me, sir, for it's no laughing matter. I drank that night naught worse, I expect, than red wine. Whatever it was, we swore our oaths, Mr. Cary; and oaths are oaths, say I."

"Of course, Jack, of course; but to go to look for her—and when we've found her, cut her lover's throat. Absurd, Jack, even if she were worth looking for, or his throat worth cutting. Tut, tut, tut—"

But Jack looked steadfastly in his face. After some silence:

"How far is it to the Caracas, then, sir?"

"What is that to thee, man?"

"Why, he was made governor thereof, I hear; so that would be the place to find her?"

"You don't mean to go thither to seek her?" shouted Cary, forcing a laugh.

"That depends on whether I can go, sir; but if I can scrape the money together, or get a berth on board some ship, why, God's will must be done."

Will looked at him to see if he had been drinking or gone mad, but the little pig's eyes were both sane and sober.

Will knew no answer. To laugh at the poor fellow was easy; to deny that he was right, that he was a hero and cavalier outdoing romance itself in faithfulness, was not so easy. Cary, in the first impulse, wished him at the bottom of the bay for shaming him. Of course, his own plan of letting ill alone was the rational, prudent, irreproachable plan, just what any gentleman in his senses would do; but here was a vulgar, fat curate, out of his senses, determined not to let ill alone, but to do something, as Cary felt in his heart, of a far diviner stamp.

"Well," said Jack, in his stupid steadfast way, "it's a very bad look-out; but mother's pretty well off if father dies, and the maidens are stout wenches enough and will make tidy servants, please the Lord. And you'll see that they come to no harm, Mr. William, for old acquaintance' sake, if I never come back."

Cary was silent with amazement.

"And, Mr. William, you know me for an honest man, I hope. Will you lend me five pounds, and take my books in pawn for them, just to help me out?"

"Are you mad, or in a dream? You will never find her!"

"That's no reason why I shouldn't do my duty in looking for her, Mr. William."

"But, my good fellow, even if you get to the Indies, you will be clapped into the Inquisition and burnt alive, as sure as your name is Jack."

"I know that," he said in a doleful tone; "and a sore struggle of the flesh I have had about it; for I am a great coward, Mr. William, a

"Brass within, and steel without,
With beams on his topcastle strong;
And eighteen pieces of ordinance
He carries on either side along."

Thus sang Sir Andrew Barton of the Pelican, a vessel now resting like an ancient warrior on the sedgy banks of the Thames at Deptford. Drawn high and dry, she serves as a monument to the globe-circling voyages of Sir Francis Drake, upon whose mainmast hang epigrams celebrating her triumphs. It was here, amidst the revelry of a great feast hosted by the Lord Mayor, that Elizabeth I had once knighthooded Drake, though the bridge of planks leading to her ship had collapsed under the weight of the crowd, falling harmlessly upon the throng—a sign, the locals whispered, of a lucky planet guiding the vessel.

Today, however, the atmosphere aboard the Pelican had shifted from jubilation to somber reflection. The Lord Mayor, Sir Edward Osborne, a man whose own chivalry had been forged in the desperate leap from London Bridge to save an infant, presided over a table of England's greatest minds. Beside him sat the Earl of Cumberland and Walter Raleigh, the latter debating the mysteries of the North-West Passage with the quiet scholar Richard Hakluyt and the seasoned traveler Anthony Jenkinson. Across the table, Philip Sidney, having relinquished his seat of honor to allow the virtuosi to converse freely, lamented his confinement to court politics while envying the adventurous spirit of Martin Frobisher.

The conversation turned to the perilous icebergs and the frozen steppes of Siberia, a stark contrast to the wine-filled banquets of the present. Yet, the mood fractured when a waiter announced the arrival of a gentleman seeking Sir Walter Raleigh. Amyas Leigh entered, hesitating in the doorway, his presence commanding silence. "Captain Leigh!" cried the assembly. Osborne, recognizing the delay, asked why he had not simply walked in, but Amyas offered a look to Raleigh that spoke volumes. Pale with shock, Raleigh rose and followed Amyas into a private cabin.

Five minutes later, Amyas emerged alone. In brief, devastating words, he delivered the news: Sir Humphrey Gilbert was dead. The feast dissolved instantly. Osborne, recalling the Egyptian custom of placing a corpse among guests to remind them of mortality, declared the gathering over. "None here can drink with the loss of so great a man still ringing in his ears," he said. The guests departed hurriedly, each whispering, "What will the Queen say?"

Raleigh, Sidney, and Cumberland left in one boat, leaving Amyas and his brother Frank to walk back to Whitehall. The journey was heavy with unspoken grief. Upon reaching their mother's lodgings, they found her absent, a relief that allowed them to retreat to a bay-window overlooking the river. The fading light illuminated the stark contrast between the two brothers. Frank, the courtier, appeared slender, bloodless, and fragile, his fingers delicate as a woman's. Amyas, conversely, was a statue of hardened experience; his locks cropped short, his face bronzed by sun and storm, a scar crossing his temple, and his hand massive and smith-like.

"You look aged," Amyas observed, noting the pallor and sharpness of his brother's features.
"And you," Frank replied with a wry smile, "look like a man who has seen too much of the world."

The conversation drifted to the nature of their lives and the pull of the West. Frank spoke of the ancient poets' belief that Elysium lay beneath the setting sun, a divine instinct driving men to flee eastward toward peace. "I should rather have my face turned to the West when I die," he confessed, admitting his longing to join the Argonauts subduing the earth for God and the Queen.

Amyas, realizing the depth of Frank's resolve, finally voiced the purpose of his journey to London. "I came not merely to see you and my mother, but to ask your advice and her permission to take you with me."
Frank's reaction was immediate and fierce. "Dearest man, a month of it would kill you!" he cried, holding his brother at arm's length, feeling the terrifying thinness of his own frame. Yet, he countered with the philosophy of Thales, claiming the ocean was the mother of all life, and he felt no repugnance in returning to her bosom.

"My mother?" Amyas protested.
"My mother knows all," Frank insisted, citing the prophecies of Doctor Dee. The astrologer had warned that Frank, born under a cold, watery planet, needed the vivifying heat of the sun to survive. He had been told to spend no more winters in the East but to return to the sea-breezes to warm his frozen lungs. Furthermore, stories of men renewed in youth by traveling to Italy and Spain had softened his mother's heart. "She believes," Frank said, his voice trembling. "And I must go. It is not merely love, Amyas—tyrannous and uncontrollable—but honor. My oath."

As Frank paused, gasping for breath from a violent cough, a gentle voice interrupted. "Fools, fools that we were—to take that fantastical vow!" Amyas exclaimed, but the voice belonged to their mother. She had entered silently. "No, my sons," she corrected, her eyes shining with a fierce, maternal resolve. "You vowed for the sake of peace on earth... Blessed are the peacemakers."

Amyas, horrified, begged her not to hate him for taking her first-born away. "God takes him, and not you," she replied, laying her head upon Amyas's chest. She spoke of exceeding honors awaiting them in the West, comparing their destinies to Esau and Jacob. Then, with a winning smile, she proposed joining them. "I could nurse the sick," she said, "or perhaps win back that poor girl you love. I could mend and wash for you. Is it not as easy to play the good housewife afloat as on shore?"

Amyas looked from one to the other, realizing the gravity of her offer. "Mother! You know not what you ask. This is a sterner matter than either of you fancy; one that must be worked out with sharp shot and cold steel."
"How?" they cried in unison, aghast.
"I must pay my men," Amyas explained, his voice hardening. "I must pay them with Spanish gold. And as a loyal subject, I cannot go to the Spanish Main with a clear conscience unless I do all the harm my hand finds to do to her enemies."

Mrs. Leigh shuddered at the thought of Frank becoming "a man of blood," but her gentle heart eventually yielded to the logic of duty. "Frank must at least ask the Queen's leave," she conceded.

The days that followed were a blur of anxiety for Frank. Terrified of the Queen's frown, he spent his time courting Leicester, Hatton, and Sidney, acting as if he were suing for a reprieve from the scaffold. Finally, he secured an audience. He returned pale and exhausted, recounting the Queen's initial anger and her accusations of disloyalty. But when he threw himself at her mercy, pleading that exile from her presence was already a sufficient punishment, she was moved. With angelic pity, she dismissed him, having granted his request.

News of this triumph reached the family through Edmund Spenser, who arrived with Mrs. Leigh. He revealed that the Queen had informed Frank that by undertaking this quest, he had voluntarily forfeited the highest earthly honor available: knighthood. "She told him this day," Spenser said, "that by going on this quest he deprived himself of that highest earthly honor."

Frank smiled, unfazed. "I have lost nothing after all. There is still an order of knighthood in which I may win my spurs, even though her majesty refuse me the accolade."
"What, then? You will not take it from a foreign prince?"
Frank laughed softly. "Have you never read of that knighthood which is eternal in the heavens? Of those true cavaliers whom John saw in Patmos, riding on white horses, clothed in fine linen, knights-errant in the everlasting war against the False Prophet and the Beast? Let me but become worthy of their ranks hereafter, what matter whether I be called Sir Frank on earth?"

With the Queen's permission secured and Spenser's poetic encouragement given, the preparations for the voyage began in earnest. Frank mortgaged his farm; Will Cary did the same. Old Salterne, grumbling at the expense, forced the adventurers to accept his two-hundred-ton ship and five hundred pounds toward outfitting her. Mrs. Leigh worked day and night, sewing clothes and preparing comforts, while Amyas contributed his time and intellect, overseeing every rope and nail with the old merchant.

Recruitment was led by Cary and the enthusiastic John Brimblecombe, who preached a crusade against the Spaniards through the local towns. Amyas, wary of haste after previous disasters, selected only the finest men. Among them was a peculiar character named Parracombe, a former schoolfellow who had drunk his inheritance and now sought redemption at sea. His presence brought a tale of confusion involving an "Egyptian" fortune-teller and a suspicious Popish ostler who had vanished from the Mariners' Rest inn, hinting at deeper conspiracies against the Queen, though nothing could be proven.

Also joining was Salvation Yeo, nearly wild with anticipation to fight the Spaniards once more. He brought with him a letter from Sir Francis Drake, detailing his own projects to drain the silt from Plymouth harbor, and a list of practical naval hints from Captain John Hawkins regarding ordnance and the prevention of scurvy. These notes proved invaluable to Amyas.

By November 15th, 1583, the tall ship *Rose* dropped anchor at Appledore Pool. She was a formidable vessel, carrying a hundred men, ample provisions, and a heavy armament of culverins, swivels, muskets, and pikes. The following Sunday, the entire crew received communion at Northam Church amidst a massive crowd. As the ship hove anchor and sailed out over the Bar before a soft east wind, accompanied by the music of sacbut, fife, and drum, and the discharge of all ordnance, the cliffs and quays erupted in cheers and tearful blessings.

Mrs. Leigh, having kissed her sons one last time at the altar steps, climbed to a rocky knoll outside the churchyard. She watched the *Rose* glide out between the yellow denes, shrinking hour by hour until her hull sank below the dim horizon and her white sails faded into the gray Atlantic mist. Bowing her head in worship, she returned home to loneliness and prayer, leaving her sons to their chivalrous adventure in the boundless West.

"The sun's rim dips; the stars rush out;
At one stride comes the dark."

Land! Land! Land! Yes, there it lay, far to the south and west, a long, blue ribbon separating the crimson sea from the golden sky. It was land at last—a promise of fresh streams, cooling fruits, and space for limbs cramped by scurvy and weakness. Perhaps, too, it held gold and gems, the hidden wealth of India. Who could say? The old world of hard facts lay thousands of miles behind them; before them stretched a realm of wonder, fable, and boundless hope. Men who had crawled from their stifling hammocks now rose; strong men fell to their knees in gratitude. Every eye and hand reached toward the fading blue cloud, which seemed to rise higher as the ship surged forward, driven by the trade-wind that whispered affectionately against brow and sail: "I am the faithful friend of those who dare!"

"Blow freshly, blow yet more strongly, thou good trade-wind," Frank murmured to himself, his straining eyes fixed on that first outpost of the New World, which held his entire existence. His cheeks were hollow, wasted, and the feverish flush on each burned crimson in the dying light. Soon, the western rainbows—emerald, topaz, amethyst, ruby—faded into silver-gray. Above, through the deepening sapphire, the Moon and Venus reigned over the darkening sea.

"That must be Barbados, your worship," said Drew, the master. "Unless my reckoning is hopelessly wrong, which Heaven knows it has no right to be after such a voyage, and God be praised."

"Barbados? I've never heard of it."

"Likely enough, sir. Yeo and I were here with Captain Drake, and I returned afterward with poor Captain Barlow. I recall good harborage to the south and west."

"And free from Spaniards, cannibals, or other evil beasts," added Yeo. "A true garden of the Lord, hidden in the seas as an inheritance for those who love Him. I heard Captain Drake speak of planting it, should the chance ever arise."

"I remember now," said Amyas, "some talk between him and poor Sir Humphrey regarding an island here. God grant he had gone there instead of to Newfoundland!"

"Nay, then," replied Yeo. "He is in bliss now with the Lord; and you would not have kept him from that, sir?"

"He would have waited as willingly as he went, if he could have served his Queen thereby. But what say you, my masters? Is it not better to spend a few days here, to restore our sick, before we strike for the Main and begin our work?"

All approved the counsel except Frank, who remained silent.

"Come, fellow-adventurer," urged Cary. "We must have your voice too."

"To my impatience, Will," Frank replied softly, "there is but one place on earth, and I long daily for wings to fly thither. Yet the counsel is sound. I approve it."

The verdict was announced and met with a hearty cheer from the crew. Before morning, they had sailed along the southern shore and were feeling their way into the bay where Bridgetown now stands. All eyes were fixed on the low, wooded hills sleeping in the moonlight, spangled by fireflies like a million dancing stars. All nostrils drank greedily the fragrant air sweeping from the land, heavy with the scent of a thousand flowers; all ears welcomed the hum of insects, the snore of tree-toads, and the plaintive notes of shore-fowl, a noisy life that filled the tropic night.

At last, the ship stopped. The cable rattled through the hawsehole. Then, careless of lurking Spaniards or Caribs, an instinctive cheer burst from every throat. Poor fellows! Amyas had much ado to prevent them landing immediately in the darkness, reminding them that daylight was but two hours away.

"Never were two such long hours," complained a young lad, pacing restlessly.

"You never were in the Inquisition," retorted Yeo dryly, "or you'd know better how slow time can run. Stand still and give thanks you are where you are."

"I say, Gunner, is there gold on that island?"

"Never heard of any; and so much the better for it," Yeo replied.

"But, Gunner," asked a scurvy-stricken cripple, licking his lips, "are there oranges and lemons there?"

"Not that I've seen; but plenty of good fruit down to the beach, thank the Lord. Here comes the dawn at last."

The rose flushed upward, the sun rushed forth, and its level rays glittered on the smooth stems of the palms, throwing rainbows across the foam of the coral reefs and gilding lonely uplands far away. Long lines of pelicans clanged out to sea; the insect hum hushed, and a thousand birds burst into jubilant song. A thin blue mist crept upward toward the inner downs and vanished, leaving them to quiver in the burning glare. The land-breeze, which had blown fresh out to sea all night, died away into glassy calm, and the tropic day began.

The sick were lifted over the side and landed boat-load after boat-load on the beach, stretching themselves in the shade of the palms. Within half an hour, the whole crew was scattered on the shore, save for a dozen men who had volunteered to keep watch aboard until noon.

Now, the first instinctive cry of nature was for fruit! Fruit! Fruit! The poor, lame wretches crawled from place to place, plucking greedily the violet grapes of the creeping shore vine, staining their mouths and blistering their lips with prickly pears despite Yeo's entreaties. Some of the healthy began hewing down cocoa-nut trees, achieving little but blunting their hatchets. Till Yeo and Drew, having gathered half a dozen reasonable men, went inland and returned in an hour laden with the dainties of that primeval orchard—acid juniper-apples, luscious guavas, and crowned ananas, queen of all fruits, found by hundreds on the broiling ledges of the low tufa-cliffs. Then, sitting on the sandy turf, defiant of galliwasps and jackspaniards, they partook of an equal banquet. Old blue land-crabs sat in their house-doors, brandishing their fists in defiance, while solemn cranes stood in the shallow water with heads on one side, meditating on how long it had been since they had seen featherless bipeds breaking the solitude of their isle.

Frank wandered up and down, silent, more in wonder than sadness, while great Amyas walked after him, mouth full of juniper-apples, enacting the part of a showman with a patronizing air, as one who had seen the wonders already and was above being astonished.

"New, new; everything new!" Frank said meditatively. "Oh, awful feeling! All things changed around us, even to the tiniest fly and flower; yet we the same, the same forever!"

Amyas, to whom such utterances were altogether sibylline and unintelligible, answered:

"Look, Frank, that's a colibri. Have you heard of colibris?"

Frank looked at the living gem hanging, loud-humming, over some fantastic bloom, then dashed away, seemingly to call its mate, whirring and dancing round and round the flower-starred bushes, flashing fresh rainbows at every shift of light.

Frank watched solemnly for a while, then:

"Qualis Natura formatrix, si talis formata? Oh my God, how fair must be Thy real world, if even Thy phantoms are so fair!"

"Phantoms?" asked Amyas uneasily. "That's no ghost, Frank, but a jolly little honey-sucker, with a wee wife and children no bigger than peas, but yet solid, greedy little fellows enough, I'll warrant."

"Not phantoms in thy sense, good fellow, but in the sense of those who know the worthlessness of all below."

"I'll tell you what, brother Frank, you are a great deal wiser than me, I know; but I can't abide to see you turn up your nose as it were at God's good earth. See now, God made all these things; and never a man, perhaps, set eyes on them till fifty years ago; and yet they were as pretty as they are now, ever since the making of the world. And why do you think God could have put them here, then, but to please Himself"—Amyas took off his hat—"with the sight of them? Now, I say, brother Frank, what's good enough to please God is good enough to please you and me."

"Your rebuke is just, dear old simple-hearted fellow; and God forgive me, if with all my learning, which has brought me no profit, and my longings, which have brought me no peace, I presume at moments, sinner that I am, to be more dainty than the Lord Himself. He walked in Paradise among the trees of the garden, Amyas; and so will we, and be content with what He sends. Why should we long for the next world before we are fit even for this one?"

"And in the meanwhile," said Amyas, "this earth's quite good enough, at least here in Barbados."

"Do you believe," asked Frank, trying to turn his own thoughts, "in those tales of the Spaniards, that the Sirens and Tritons are heard singing in these seas?"

"I can't tell. There's more fish in the water than ever came out of it, and more wonders in the world, I'll warrant, than we ever dreamt of; but I was never in these parts before. In the South Sea, I must say, I never came across any, though Yeo says he has heard fair music at night up in the Gulf, far away from land."

"The Spaniards report that at certain seasons choirs of these nymphs assemble in the sea, and with ravishing music sing their watery loves. It may be so. For Nature, which has peopled the land with rational souls, may not have left the sea altogether barren of them; above all, when we remember that the ocean is as it were the very fount of all fertility, and its slime (as the most learned hold with Thales of Miletus) that *prima materia* out of which all things were one by one concocted. Therefore, the ancients feigned wisely that Venus, the mother of all living things, whereby they designed the plastic force of nature, was born of the sea-foam, and rising from the deep, floated ashore upon the isles of Greece."

"I don't know what plastic force is; but I wish I had had the luck to be by when the pretty poppet came up. However, the nearest thing I ever saw to that was maidens swimming alongside of us when we were in the South Seas, and would have come aboard, too; but Drake sent them all off again for a lot of naughty packs, and I verily believe they were no better. Look at the butterflies now! Don't you wish you were a boy again, and not too proud to go catching them in your cap?"

And so the two wandered on together through the glorious tropic woods, then returned to the beach to find the sick already grown cheerful, and many who that morning could not stir from their hammocks pacing up and down, gaining strength with every step.

"Well done, lads!" cried Amyas. "Keep a cheerful mind. We will have the music ashore after dinner, for want of mermaids to sing to us, and those that can dance may."

Thus those four days were spent. The men, like schoolboys on a holiday, gave themselves up to simple merriment, not forgetting, however, to wash clothes, take in fresh water, and store up a good supply of such fruit as seemed likely to keep. Until, tired with fruitless rambles after gold, which they expected to find in every bush despite Yeo's warnings that none had been heard of on the island, they were fain to lounge about, full-grown babies, picking up shells and sea-fans to take home to their sweethearts, smoking agoutis out of hollow trees with shout and laughter, and tormenting every living thing they could come near, till not a land-crab dared look out of his hole, or an armadillo unroll itself, till they were safe out of the bay and off again to the westward, unconscious pioneers of all the wealth, commerce, beauty, and science which has in later centuries made that lovely isle the richest gem of all the tropic seas.

The vessel slipped silently past the southern tip of Grenada under the cover of darkness, finally entering the archipelago known as the "Fairy Ring." To the seasoned eyes of the crew, these waters were a paradox: a realm where nature had concentrated her most breathtaking beauty, yet humanity had concentrated its greatest sins. While the newcomers might have viewed Barbados with a sense of strange grandeur, the seas surrounding these islands offered a different kind of splendor—calm, smiling waters untouched by the hurricanes that roared miles to the north. The sky, sea, and land merged into a single, vast rainbow, a spectacle that left even the pragmatic sailors, driven by thoughts of Spanish gold, momentarily awestruck.

Amyas, however, saw beyond the scenery. Accustomed to the tropics, his mind was fixed on a singular, dangerous objective: the extirpation of the Spanish presence and the annexation of the West Indies to Queen Elizabeth's dominions. Yet, even his unpoetic gaze could not fail to register the awe-inspiring nature of their surroundings. These were lands famous yet entirely new to Englishmen, territories where failure meant not merely death, but the tortures of the Inquisition. Few English ships had dared these waters before; those who had, under Captain John Hawkins, had learned the hard way that Spanish jealousy required English steel to overcome. As the old mariners of the *Pelican* and the *Minion* recounted tales of every isle and bird, Frank stood silent, his soul drinking in the glory of the approaching horizon.

He murmured to himself, "This is the gate of heaven," watching the world dissolve into a blazing phantom. Islands and capes hung suspended in the air, mirrored below in the shimmering heat haze. Giant cacti twisted like candelabra against glaring slopes, and the sun itself seemed distorted into strange, pear-shaped figures by beds of crimson mist. But while Frank marveled, Yeo rejoiced. To the south of the setting sun, a cluster of tall peaks rose from the sea—the mountains of Macanao at the western end of Margarita, the Isle of Pearls. It was a place renowned across the Mediterranean and German fairs, second only to the pearl-rich island in the Gulf of Panama, which had claimed the life of John Oxenham fifteen years prior.

The following day, they ran along the northern coast, passing undiscovered the fortress guarding the eastern pearl fisheries. Eventually, they opened into a deep, wooded bight where a Spanish caravel and three small boats lay at anchor. Instantly, the deck erupted with activity. Opinions flew regarding the approach; some advocated sailing directly into the roadstead with the afternoon breeze, while others, fearing hidden reefs, favored a quiet approach via the pinnace. Yeo suggested flying Spanish colors to avoid alarm, but Amyas firmly rejected the deception, deeming it a mean act unless necessary for extreme danger or statecraft.

They held their course until the next point blocked their view, then launched the assault. Cary took the largest boat with twenty men, while Amyas commanded the smaller vessel with fifteen, including John Brimblecombe. The priest arrived in his cassock and bands, clutching an old sword belonging to his uncle, which he treasured deeply.

Approaching the bight's mouth, they encountered a formidable barrier of coral rocks. Forced to navigate the reef's edge, the crew gazed in wonder at the subaquatic gardens of Nereus, where living flowers swayed beneath the clear water. Suddenly, a "school of sharks" emerged from the depths. Some were nearly as long as the boat, their wicked eyes fixed upward with a hungry intensity.

"Jack," Amyas whispered, pointing to the largest predator, "look how that fellow eyes thee. He has taken a fancy to that plump hide and thinks thou wouldst taste as tender as a sucking porker."

Jack turned pale but remained silent. Just then, the massive shark spotted a parrot-fish emerging from a coral cleft. Driven by instinct, the fish leaped into the air, nearly aboard the boat, while three or four great brown snouts clashed together within two yards of Jack, displaying rows of saw-like teeth before sinking back into the water. Overwhelmed by the sight, Jack softly muttered, "In manus tuas, Domine!" and turned his eyes away from the sea.

Once through the reef, they entered the bight with a fair breeze. Cary's boat closed the distance before the Spaniards could mount their defenses. Standing in the stern-sheets, Cary shouted for surrender, noting their numerical superiority. When the Spanish captain demanded to know in whose name they acted, Cary retorted, "In the name of common sense, ye dogs! Do you not see that you are but fifty strong to our twenty?" Before the captain could fire, Cary scrambled up the side, knocking him over with a blow intended to spare bloodshed. The crew surrendered immediately, some kneeling, others leaping into the water.

Meanwhile, Amyas secured the second boat, which yielded without resistance, while the negroes swam ashore. The third boat, however, attempted to flee. Seizing an opportunity to distinguish himself, John Brimblecombe grabbed a boat-hook and clawed onto the fleeing vessel's stern, shouting, "Stay, ye Papists! Stay, Spanish dogs!" Expecting ten to one, he was instantly pulled overboard.

Seized by a panic born of his imagination, which filled the sea with the sharks he had just witnessed, Jack began to roar like a bull. In his confusion, he struck out after the Spanish boat, seemingly in a madness of valor or a desperate hope to grab the trailing hook. Dressed in his cassock, he floated like a great black monk-fish, howling and puffing with salt water in his mouth. "Stay, ye Spanish dogs! Help, all good fellows! See you not that I am a dead man? They are nuzzling already at my toes! He hath hold of my leg! My right thigh is bitten clean off! Oh that I were preaching in Hartland pulpit!"

His cries grew more frantic than any seasickness Panurge ever endured. The English, expecting him to be snapped up by sharks or struck by oars, fired a volley into the fugitives. The Spaniards, terrified, leaped overboard. Jack scrambled into the boat, drew his sword with one hand, and wiped water from his eyes with the other, slashing at the empty air and crying, "Yield, idolaters!" Realizing there was no one left to fight, he sat panting in the sternsheets and began stripping off his hose.

Amyas, assuming his friend had gone mad from the sun or heatstroke, ordered the boat alongside and demanded to know what he was doing with his "nether tackle." Amidst uproarious laughter, Jack vowed that his right thigh had been bitten through to the bone and that his hose was full of blood. He would have swooned from imaginary blood loss had not his friends, through argument and anger, convinced him he was whole and sound.

They then overhauled their prize, finding it stocked with hides and salt-pork, but also containing crates of brown pearls. The Spaniards had damaged the color by opening the shells with fire rather than allowing gradual decay. After a malicious prankster set the captured ship ablaze—a fitting end for a vessel laden with hides—they departed, though Amyas chided the wanton destruction. Cary joked that as "Lutheran devils," they were bound to vanish with an evil savor.

Once aboard, Amyas confronted Brimblecombe, advising him to play the man more seriously and keep his breath for rowing rather than roaring before injury. Frank joined the rebuke, questioning Jack's logic. "Tell me, thou recreant Jack," Frank challenged, "is not a priest more than a layman? If a priest shows himself no man, he shows himself all the more no priest."

Jack, realizing the trap, stammered that sharks did not understand logic. Frank pressed further, asking if it was the part of a man to howl like a pig because he feared what was not there. Jack fell silent. Frank concluded that Jack's behavior was not cowardice but Manicheanism—blaming an evil creator for sharks made to devour useless carcasses. As a Brother of the Rose, Jack should have leaped joyfully into the shark's mouth.

"You are a cause of scandal," Frank declared, "and a rotten branch to be excised." He stripped Jack of his office as chaplain, warning that unless he performed deeds equal to the Seven Champions within seven days, he would be allowed to carry no iron but a pen. Amyas added his weight, criticizing the clergy for being soft-handed men who could not dig or beg, and whose failure to stand firm in the hour of need unpreached their entire lives. Together, they boarded Jack with heavy sarcasm until he covered his ears and fled, leaving Frank laughing so heartily that Amyas found relief in the smile it brought to his own steady face.

The next day, Sunday, they held divine service, though Amyas read the articles of their agreement rather than letting Jack preach, given his recent shame. Seeing a sloping beach with fresh water, they decided to land to wash clothes and replenish supplies, scarce since Barbados. Jack insisted on joining, carrying his sword and arquebuse, claiming a dream of Spanish attack that he felt compelled to fulfill, even if it meant death. He preferred dying to being mocked as an ape or a scarecrow for his cowardice.

Upon landing, they washed and stretched their legs, admiring the beauty before setting out a seine net. Among their catch was a seven-foot sea-cow, or manati, described as a pious beast that guarded corpses until they decayed, carried its young under its arm, and sang melodiously when wounded. They also secured two large black fowls, curassos, which provided a hearty meal of roasted and boiled meat.

While the crew filled casks near the river's outflow, John Brimblecombe sat apart under a great tree, reading his Bible with a shamefaced expression, his sword and arquebuse nearby. His solitude ended abruptly when a cry signaled the enemy's approach. Fifty Spanish soldiers, accompanied by negroes and an officer on horseback with a plumed hat, emerged from the woods, swords drawn.

"Stand, for your lives!" Amyas shouted. Ten minutes were lost in scrambling for formation, but when Jack saw the Spaniards, he acted as if he had expected them. Plucking a leaf to mark his page, he laid down his book, caught his arquebuse, and ran like a mad dog straight at the Spanish captain. He shot the officer through the body, killing him instantly, then threw the weapon at the next man and charged with his sword.

The Spaniards, viewing him as a fiend or Luther incarnate, retreated pell-mell, firing five or six shots at once. Whether from fear or the risk of hitting their own men, their aim was poor; Jack received only one shrewd ball in the thigh, which left him limping for days. As the Spaniards fled, nearly forty Englishmen rallied in good order. The Spaniards turned and ran, and Cary noted, "The dogs had had such a taste of the parson, that they had no mind to wait for the clerk and people."

"Come back, Jack! Are you mad?" Amyas shouted.

But Jack, silent until then, pursued them fiercely. Reaching a great blow at an arquebusier, he caught his foot in a root, fell, and struck his head against the ground, knocking out the last of his breath. When Amyas saw the Spaniards gone, he picked up the unconscious Jack. Staring about wildly, Jack cried, "Glory be! Glory be!--How many have I killed? How many have I killed?"

"Nineteen, at the least," Cary replied, "and seven with one back stroke." He showed Brimblecombe the dead captain and several wounded men.

"There!" Jack paused, blowing hard. "Will you laugh at me any more, Mr. Cary; or say that I cannot fight, because I am a poor parson's son?"

Cary took his hand, apologized for his earlier scoffing, and admitted that Jack had played the best man of them all. Jack, never bearing malice, began to laugh in return, recalling Cary's schoolboy pranks with Bideford. They returned to the boats, grateful for their deliverance. As the crew rejoiced over Jack, he grew faint from unseen bleeding, making as little of his real wound as he had of his imaginary one the day before.

That evening, Frank asked how Jack had shown such cool valor after his previous panic. Jack explained that he had been reading the eleventh chapter of Hebrews to fortify his spirits, specifically the verse, "out of weakness were made strong, waxed valiant in fight." Hearing the cry of the Spaniards, he believed he fought in a just cause with equal faith, granting him a strange assurance of victory. "There is no credit due to me," he modestly stated. "There was no valor in me whatsoever, but only a certainty of safety; and any coward would fight if he knew that he were to have all the killing and none of the scratches."

The next Sunday, Jack repeated this sermon, which edified all, even Salvation Yeo, who acknowledged him as a godly preacher and a valiant comrade despite his simple ways.

They took the Spanish officer's sword and a heavy gold chain as prizes. Jack accepted the sword but steadfastly refused the chain, urging Amyas to put it into the common stock. When Amyas refused, Jack cut the chain into links and distributed them among the crew members who had helped him, winning their enduring goodwill.

The chronicler noted that he had never seen such a lack of covetousness in the worthy man, from their school days until his peaceful burial in Hartland. Though ugly, lowly, and simple in understanding, Brimblecombe possessed a spirit so loving and cheerful, lifted from base purposes to the worship of duty, that he seemed to believe it more blessed to give than to receive. All the wealth he gained in war he dispersed among his sisters and the poor of his parish. Living unmarried until his death like a true lover and constant mourner, he left hardly enough to bring his body to the grave. Those who once laughed at him would now envy him, desiring to

The cry rose high, the horses neighed, and the beacons flared against the night sky, a desperate signal to save the treasure they had so narrowly secured. Yet, Amyas Preston, having "fleshed his dogs" in the recent skirmish, refused to linger on the islands of Margarita and Cubagua. With the alarm raised, he steered his ship southwest, cutting across the great bay that stretched from the Peninsula of Paria to Cape Codera. To their right lay the jagged silhouette of Tortuga; to their left, the Piritoos islands rose like twin green ridges barely kissing the tideless sea.

Yeo and Drew, veterans of this coast who had once traded with Hawkins, navigated the waters with practiced ease. Before them, the majestic range of the Caracas mountains pierced the heavens, towering so high that the hills familiar to most of the crew seemed mere mounds in comparison. Even Frank, who knew the Alps, and Amyas, who had traversed the Andes, found themselves dwarfed by this colossal barrier. Cary, whose horizon was limited to the rugged peaks of M'Gillicuddy's Reeks, spent a day gripping the rigging, his chin raised unnaturally high, staring upward until his neck stiffened, all to the amusement of the others.

As dawn approached, the sea grew choppy despite a gentle breeze. The massive wall of Cape Codera loomed a thousand feet to their left, beyond which lay endless banks of mountains bathed in the pale yellow moonlight. By morning, a large ship appeared on the horizon, having passed them during the night, now ten miles to the eastward. Yeo immediately proposed turning back to intercept her, arguing that it was the Lord's work to be thorough. But Amyas and Frank hesitated.

"Why, Yeo," Frank asked, "you said one more day would bring us to La Guayra."

"All the more reason," Yeo replied solemnly, "to do the Lord's work thoroughly when He has brought us safely this far."

"She can pass well enough, and no loss," Amyas countered.

"Ah, sirs," Yeo insisted, "she is delivered into your hands, and you must give an account of her."

"My good Yeo," Frank interjected, "I trust we shall give good account of many a tall Spaniard before we return. But you know La Guayra, and the salvation of the lady we seek, was our first object."

Yeo shook his head sadly. "A lady brought Captain Oxenham to ruin."

"You dare not compare her with this one?" Frank and Cary exclaimed in unison.

"God forbid," Yeo said. "But no adventure prospers without a single eye to the Lord's work: to cripple the Spaniard and exalt Her Majesty. I believed that was dearer to Captain Leigh's heart than anything."

Amyas stood torn. His duty to the Queen demanded he pursue the Spanish vessel; his vow to the lady demanded he press on to La Guayra. It was a practical dilemma, not merely theoretical. Ultimately, Frank's counsel prevailed, and they sailed eastward. Had they not, the story would have ended differently.

Around midday, a canoe staggered toward them under a triangular sail, carrying two Indians.

"Metal floats in these seas," Cary remarked. "A fresh marvel for you, Frank."

"Expound," Frank said, ready to swallow any wonder.

"How else would those bronze statues dare to sea in such a cockleshell?" Cary asked. "Have I given you the *dor* now, master courtier?"

"I am past *dors*, Will. But what noble creatures! How fearlessly they approach! Can they know we are English, the avengers of their kind?"

"They likely take us for Spaniards wishing to buy coconuts. See, the canoe is laden with vegetables."

"Hail them, Yeo!" Amyas commanded. "You speak the best Spanish; I need to speak with one of them."

Yeo hailed them. The canoe pulled alongside, lowering its sail as a splendid Indian scrambled aboard with the agility of a cat. He stood six feet tall, bold and graceful, mirroring the bearing of Frank or Amyas himself. He smiled broadly, revealing white teeth, but his expression shifted instantly. Springing to the side, he shouted to his companion in Spanish, "Treachery! No Spaniard!" and attempted to leap overboard. A dozen strong men caught him before he could escape.

It took effort to subdue him; his naked limbs were slippery and his strength considerable. Amyas alternately pleaded with the men not to harm the Indian and urged the stranger to be quiet, promising no injury. After five minutes of confusion, the Indian surrendered sulkily.

"Don't bind him," Amyas ordered. "Let him loose, form a ring around him. Now, my man, here is a dollar."

The Indian's eyes gleamed as he took the coin.

"All I ask," Amyas said, "is to tell me what ships are in La Guayra, and then to guide me to the governor's house and the customs house."

The Indian placed the coin on the deck, crossed himself, and stared Amyas in the face.

"No, senor! I am a freeman, a cavalier, a Christian Guayqueria. My forefathers swore fealty to the King of Spain, and he calls them his most loyal subjects to this day. God forbid I should aid his enemies, who are my enemies too."

A growl rose from the men who understood him, with several hinting that a cord around the head or a match between the fingers would yield the truth quickly.

"God forbid!" Amyas declared. "He is a brave and loyal man, and as such I will treat him. Tell me, my brave fellow, how do you know we are His Catholic Majesty's enemies?"

With a shrewd smile, the Indian pointed to various objects on the ship, declaring each "Not Spanish."

"Well, and what of that?" Amyas asked.

"None but Spaniards and free Guayquerias have the right to sail these seas."

Amyas laughed. "Thou art a valiant piece of copper. Pick up thy dollar and go in peace. Make room for him, men. We can learn what we want without his help."

The Indian paused, astonished. "Overboard with you!" Amyas commanded. "Don't you know when you are well off?"

"Most illustrious senor," the Indian began in a drawling, sententious tone, "I have been deceived. I heard you heretics roasted and ate all true Catholics, and that your priests had tails."

"Plague on you, sirrah!" squeaked Jack Brimblecombe. "Have I a tail? Look here!"

"Quien sabe? Who knows?" the Indian retorted through his nose.

"How do you know we are heretics?" Amyas asked.

"Humph! But in repayment for your kindness, I warn you, illustrious senor, not to go to La Guayra. Ships of war await you there. Moreover, Governor Don Guzman sailed eastward yesterday to hunt for you. I wonder you did not meet him."

"To hunt for us! On the watch for us!" Cary cried. "Impossible; lies! Amyas, this is some trick to frighten us away."

"Don Guzman came out but yesterday?" Amyas pressed. "Are you sure?"

"As I live, senor, he and another ship, for which I took yours."

Amyas stamped on the deck. That was the ship they had passed!

"Fool that I was to stay close to my enemy and let my opportunity slip!" he lamented. "If I had done my duty, all would have gone right!"

But it was too late to regret. The Indian's story was likely false.

"Off with you!" Amyas commanded. The Indian bounded over the side into his canoe, leaving the crew marveling at the stateliness of this bold sea-cavalier.

Thus, Westward-ho they ran beneath the mighty northern wall, the highest cliff on earth—seven thousand feet of rock separated from the sea by a narrow strip of bright green lowland. Patches of sugar-cane and coconut trees near the water reminded them of the tropics, while above, the landscape was savage, rough, and bare as an Alpine precipice. Deep clefts allowed the southern sun to blaze down upon the sea, revealing strange, stately trees lining the glens and a veil of perpetual mist shrouding the inner summits. White, fleecy clouds hung motionless in the burning air, enhancing the sense of vastness and solemn rest.

"Within those mountains, three thousand feet above our heads," said Drew, the master, "lies Saint Yago de Leon, the great city founded by the Spaniards fifteen years ago."

"Is it a rich place?" Cary asked.

"Very, they say."

"Is it a strong place?" Amyas asked.

"No forts at all, they say. The Spaniards boast that Heaven has made such good walls that man need make none."

"I don't know," Amyas said. "Lads, could you climb those hills?"

"Higher than Harty Point, sir: but it depends on what lies behind them."

Now the last point was rounded, and La Guayra lay in full view, the object of their four-thousand-mile voyage. A low black cliff crowned by a wall, batteries at either end. Within, narrow streets of white houses ran parallel to the sea along a strip of flat land no wider than two hundred yards, backed by the mountain wall in deepest shade. How one ascended that wall remained a puzzle, but Drew pointed out a narrow path winding upward through a glen, seemingly sheer. That was the road to the capital, if any dared try it. Despite the mountain's shadow, the place wore a dusty, glaring look. The air from the land was stifling; La Guayra, heated by the vast fire-brick of rock, was one of the hottest spots on earth.

Where was the harbor? There was none, only an open roadstead where five vessels tossed at anchor. The two outer ones were small merchant caravels. Behind them lay two long, low, ugly-looking craft, at the sight of which Yeo let out a long whistle.

"Galleys, as I'm a sinful saint!" Yeo exclaimed. "And what's that big one inside? She has more than hawseholes in her idolatrous black sides."

"We shall open her astern of the galleys," Amyas said. "Look out, Cary, your eyes are better than mine."

"Six round portholes on the main deck," Will noted.

"And I see the brass *patararoes* glittering on her poop," Amyas added. "Will, we're in for it."

"In for it we are, captain," Yeo replied.

"Farewell, farewell, my parents dear. I never shall see you more, I fear."

"Let's go in, nevertheless, and pound the Don's ribs, my old lad of Smerwick. Eh? Three to one is fair odds."

"Not underneath those fort guns, I beg leave to say," Yeo warned. "If the Philistines come out, we will make them like unto Zeba and Zalmunna."

"Quite true," said Amyas. "Game cocks are game cocks, but reason is reason."

"If the Philistines do not come out, they will send a messenger," Cary predicted. "Look out, all thin skulls!"

As he spoke, a puff of white smoke rolled from the eastern fort, and a heavy ball plunged into the water between the ship and the shore.

"I don't altogether like this," Amyas muttered. "What do they mean by firing without warning? And what are these ships of war doing here? Drew, you told me the armadas never lay here."

"No more, I believe, they do, sir, because the anchorage is so bad," Drew replied. "I'm mortal afeared that rascal's story was true, and the Dons have got wind of our coming."
