"Run up a white flag, at all events. If they expect us, they must have known some time since."

"True, sir. They must have come from Santa Marta or Cartagena. That would take a month."

Amyas suddenly recalled Eustace's threat at the wayside inn. Could he have betrayed their purpose? Impossible!

"Let us hold a council of war, Frank."

Frank was, however, absorbed in a different matter. Half a mile eastward, two hundred feet up the steep mountain side, stood a large, low white house embosomed in trees and gardens. No other house of similar size existed nearby. Was not that the royal flag of Spain flaunting before it? That must be the governor's house; that must be the abode of the Rose of Torridge! Frank stood devouring it with wild eyes, until he convinced himself he saw a woman's figure walking on the terrace, none other than the one he sought. Amyas could hardly tear him away to the council of war, a sad and peevish affair.

The three adventurers, with Brimblecombe, Yeo, and Drew, retired to the poop. Each looked at the others. What was to be done? Months of plans and hopes were brought to naught in an hour.

"It is impossible," Amyas said finally, "to surprise the town by land while these ships are here; for if we land our men, we leave our ship undefended."

"As impossible as to challenge Don Guzman while he is not here," Cary added.

"I wonder why the ships have not opened fire already," Drew observed.

"Perhaps they respect our flag of truce," Cary suggested. "Why not send a boat to treat?"

"For her?" Frank interrupted. "If we show we know of her existence, her name is blasted in the eyes of those jealous Spaniards."

"And as for respecting our flag of truce," Yeo said, "trust them not. They will keep the same faith as they did with Captain Hawkins at San Juan d'Ulloa, in that accursed business that began all our wars. We might have

"Full seven long hours in all men's sight
This fight endured sore,
Until our men so feeble grew,
That they could fight no more.
And then upon dead horses
Full savorly they fed,
And drank the puddle water,
They could no better get.

When they had fed so freely
They kneeled on the ground,
And gave God thanks devoutly for
The favor they had found;
Then beating up their colors,
The fight they did renew;
And turning to the Spaniards,
A thousand more they slew."

The Brave Lord Willoughby. 1586.

When the sun leaped up the next morning, and the tropic light flashed suddenly into the tropic day, Amyas paced the deck with dishevelled hair and torn clothes, his eyes red with rage and weeping, his heart full of a grief that defies description. Picture it to yourselves, you who have ever lost a brother; and you who have not, thank God that you know nothing of his agony. Full of impossible projects, he strode and staggered up and down as the ship thrashed close-hauled through the rolling seas. He would go back and burn the villa. He would take Guayra, and have the life of every man in it in return for his brother's. "We can do it, lads!" he shouted. "If Drake took Nombre de Dios, we can take La Guayra." And every voice shouted, "Yes."

"We will have it, Amyas, and have Frank too, yet," cried Cary; but Amyas shook his head. He knew, and knew not why he knew, that all the ports in New Spain would never restore to him that one beloved face.

"Yes, he shall be well avenged. And look there! There is the first crop of our vengeance." He pointed toward the shore, where between them and the now distant peaks of the Silla, three sails appeared, not five miles to windward.

"There are the Spanish bloodhounds on our heels, the same ships which we saw yesterday off Guayra. Back, lads, and welcome them, if they were a dozen."

There was a murmur of applause from all around; and if any young heart sank for a moment at the prospect of fighting three ships at once, it was awed into silence by the cheer which rose from all the older men, and by Salvation Yeo's stentorian voice.

"If there were a dozen, the Lord is with us, who has said, 'One of you shall chase a thousand.' Clear away, lads, and see the glory of the Lord this day."

"Amen!" cried Cary; and the ship was kept still closer to the wind.

Amyas had revived at the sight of battle. He no longer felt his wounds, or his great sorrow; even Frank's last angel's look grew dimmer every moment as he bustled about the deck; and ere a quarter of an hour had passed, his voice cried firmly and cheerfully as of old—

"Now, my masters, let us serve God, and then to breakfast, and after that clear for action."

Jack Brimblecombe read the daily prayers, and the prayers before a fight at sea, and his honest voice trembled, as, in the Prayer for all Conditions of Men (in spite of Amyas's despair), he added, "and especially for our dear brother Mr. Francis Leigh, perhaps captive among the idolaters;" and so they rose.

"Now, then," said Amyas, "to breakfast. A Frenchman fights best fasting, a Dutchman drunk, an Englishman full, and a Spaniard when the devil is in him, and that's always."

"And good beef and the good cause are a match for the devil," said Cary. "Come down, captain; you must eat too."

Amyas shook his head, took the tiller from the steersman, and bade him go below and fill himself. Will Cary went down, and returned in five minutes, with a plate of bread and beef, and a great jack of ale, coaxed them down Amyas's throat, as a nurse does with a child, and then scuttled below again with tears hopping down his face.

Amyas stood still steering. His face was grown seven years older in the last night. A terrible set calm was on him. Woe to the man who came across him that day!

"There are three of them, you see, my masters," said he, as the crew came on deck again. "A big ship forward, and two galleys astern of her. The big ship may keep; she is a race ship, and if we can but recover the wind of her, we will see whether our height is not a match for her length. We must give her the slip, and take the galleys first."

"I thank the Lord," said Yeo, "who has given so wise a heart to so young a general; a very David and Daniel, saving his presence, lads; and if any dare not follow him, let him be as the men of Meroz and of Succoth. Amen! Silas Staveley, smite me that boy over the head, the young monkey; why is he not down at the powder-room door?"

And Yeo went about his gunnery, as one who knew how to do it, and had the most terrible mind to do it thoroughly, and the most terrible faith that it was God's work.

So all fell to; and though there was comparatively little to be done, the ship having been kept as far as could be in fighting order all night, yet there was "clearing of decks, lacing of nettings, making of bulwarks, fitting of waist-cloths, arming of tops, tallowing of pikes, sling-ing of yards, doubling of sheets and tacks," enough to satisfy even the pedantical soul of Richard Hawkins himself. Amyas took charge of the poop, Cary of the forecastle, and Yeo, as gunner, of the main-deck, while Drew, as master, settled himself in the waist; and all was ready, and more than ready, before the great ship was within two miles of them.

And now while the mastiffs of England and the bloodhounds of Spain are nearing and nearing over the rolling surges, thirsting for each other's blood, let us spend a few minutes at least in looking at them both, and considering the causes which in those days enabled the English to face and conquer armaments immensely superior in size and number of ships, and to boast that in the whole Spanish war but one queen's ship, the Revenge, and (if I recollect right) but one private man-of-war, Sir Richard Hawkins's Dainty, had ever struck their colors to the enemy.

What was it which enabled Sir Richard Grenville's Revenge, in his last fearful fight off the Azores, to endure, for twelve hours before she struck, the attack of eight Spanish armadas, of which two (three times her own burden) sank at her side; and after all her masts were gone, and she had been boarded three times without success, to defy to the last the whole fleet of fifty-four sail, which lay around her, waiting for her to sink, "like dogs around the dying forest king"?

What enabled young Richard Hawkins's Dainty, though half her guns were useless through the carelessness or treachery of the gunner, to maintain for three days a running fight with two Spaniards of equal size with her, double the weight of metal, and ten times the number of men?

What enabled Sir George Cary's illustrious ship, the Content, to fight, single-handed, from seven in the morning till eleven at night, with four great armadas and two galleys, though her heaviest gun was but one nine-pounder, and for many hours she had but thirteen men fit for service?

What enabled, in the very year of which I write, those two "valiant Turkey Merchantmen of London, the Merchant Royal and the Tobie," with their three small consorts, to cripple, off Pantellaria in the Mediterranean, the whole fleet of Spanish galleys sent to intercept them, and return triumphant through the Straits of Gibraltar?

And lastly, what in the fight of 1588, whereof more hereafter, enabled the English fleet to capture, destroy, and scatter that Great Armada, with the loss (but not the capture) of one pinnace, and one gentleman of note?

There were more causes than one: the first seems to have lain in the build of the English ships; the second in their superior gunnery and weight of metal; the third (without which the first would have been useless) in the hearts of the English men.

The English ship was much shorter than the Spanish; and this (with the rig of those days) gave them an ease in manoeuvring, which utterly confounded their Spanish foes. "The English ships in the fight of 1588," says Camden, "charged the enemy with marvellous agility, and having discharged their broadsides, flew forth presently into the deep, and levelled their shot directly, without missing, at those great ships of the Spaniards, which were altogether heavy and unwieldy." Moreover, the Spanish fashion, in the West Indies at least, though not in the ships of the Great Armada, was, for the sake of carrying merchandise, to build their men-of-war flush-decked, or as it was called "race" (razes), which left those on deck exposed and open; while the English fashion was to heighten the ship as much as possible at stem and stern, both by the sweep of her lines, and also by stockades ("close fights and cage-works") on the poop and forecastle, thus giving to the men a shelter, which was further increased by strong bulkheads ("cobridgeheads") across the main-deck below, dividing the ship thus into a number of separate forts, fitted with swivels ("bases, fowlers, and murderers") and loopholed for musketry and arrows.

But the great source of superiority was, after all, in the men themselves. The English sailor was then, as now, a quite amphibious and all-cunning animal, capable of turning his hand to everything, from needlework and carpentry to gunnery or hand-to-hand blows; and he was, moreover, one of a nation, every citizen of which was not merely permitted to carry arms, but compelled by law to practise from childhood the use of the bow, and accustomed to consider sword-play and quarter-staff as a necessary part and parcel of education, and the pastime of every leisure hour. The "fiercest nation upon earth," as they were then called, and the freest also, each man of them fought for himself with the self-help and self-respect of a Yankee ranger, and once bidden to do his work, was trusted to carry it out by his own wit as best he could. In one word, he was a free man.

The English officers, too, as now, lived on terms of sympathy with their men unknown to the Spaniards, who raised between the commander and the commanded absurd barriers of rank and blood, which forbade to his pride any labor but that of fighting. The English officers, on the other hand, brought up to the same athletic sports, the same martial exercises, as their men, were not ashamed to care for them, to win their friendship, even on emergency to consult their judgment; and used their rank, not to differ from their men, but to outvie them; not merely to command and be obeyed, but, like Homer's heroes, or the old Norse Vikings, to lead and be followed. Drake touched the true mainspring of English success when he once (in his voyage round the world) indignantly rebuked some coxcomb gentlemen-adventurers with—"I should like to see the gentleman that will refuse to set his hand to a rope. I must have the gentlemen to hale and draw with the mariners." But those were days in which her majesty's service was as little overridden by absurd rules of seniority, as by that etiquette which is at once the counterfeit and the ruin of true discipline. Under Elizabeth and her ministers, a brave and a shrewd man was certain of promotion, let his rank or his age be what they might; the true honor of knighthood covered once and for all any lowliness of birth; and the merchant service (in which all the best sea-captains, even those of noble blood, were more or less engaged) was then a nursery, not only for seamen, but for warriors, in days when Spanish and Portuguese traders (whenever they had a chance) got rid of English competition by salvos of cannon-shot.

Hence, as I have said, that strong fellow-feeling between officers and men; and hence mutinies (as Sir Richard Hawkins tells us) were all but unknown in the English ships, while in the Spanish they broke out on every slight occasion. For the Spaniards, by some suicidal pedantry, had allowed their navy to be crippled by the same despotism, etiquette, and official routine, by which the whole nation was gradually frozen to death in the course of the next century or two; forgetting that, fifty years before, Cortez, Pizarro, and the early Conquistadores of America had achieved their miraculous triumphs on the exactly opposite method by that very fellow-feeling between commander and commanded by which the English were now conquering them in their turn.

Their navy was organized on a plan complete enough; but on one which was, as the event proved, utterly fatal to their prowess and unanimity, and which made even their courage and honor useless against the assaults of free men. "They do, in their armadas at sea, divide themselves into three bodies; to wit, soldiers, mariners, and gunners. The soldiers and officers watch and ward as if on shore; and this is the only duty they undergo, except cleaning their arms, wherein they are not over curious. The gunners are exempted from all labor and care, except about the artillery; and these are either Almaines, Flemings, or strangers; for the Spaniards are but indifferently practised in this art. The mariners are but as slaves to the rest, to moil and

"Follow thee? Follow thee? Wha wad na follow thee? Lang hast thou looed and trusted us fairly."

Amyas would have certainly succumbed to the yellow fever, but for one overriding reason he explained to Cary: he had no time to be sick while his men were sick. It was a valid and sufficient cause, as many a noble soul in the Crimea knew too well, so long as the excitement of work remained present. Yet, it was too apt to fail the hero the moment his labor ceased, allowing him to sink into the pit he had so often leaped over.

The following morning, he called a council of war—or, more accurately, a sanitary commission—for he was at his wits' end. The men were panic-stricken, ready to mutiny. Amyas told them that killing him offered no possible good, nor did being killed by him; he then retired below to consult with his officers. The doctor spoke of humors, complexions, and animal spirits in a language of pure science or nonscience. Jack Brimblecombe preached pulpit rhetoric about it being God's visitation. Cary expressed mere despair, though he masked it with a jesting smile. Yeo adopted a stoic fatalism, quoting Scripture to support his resignation. Drew, the master, remained silent, stating his business was to sail the ship, not to cure calentures.

Whereupon Amyas clutched his locks, according to custom, and finally broke forth. "Doctor! A fig for your humors and complexions! Can you cure a man's humors, or change his complexion? Can an Ethiopian change his skin, or a leopard his spots? Do not shove your ignorance onto God, sir. I ask you the reason of this sickness, and you do not know. Jack Brimblecombe, do not talk to me of God's visitation; this looks much more like the devil's visitation to my mind. We are doing God's work, Sir John, and He is not likely to hinder us. So down with the devil, say I. Cary, laughter kills the cat, but it will not cure a Christian. Yeo, when an angel tells me that it is God's will that we all die like dogs in a ditch, I will call this God's will; but not before. Drew, you say your business is to sail the ship; then sail her out of this infernal poison-trap this very morning, if you can, which you cannot. The mischief is in the air, and nowhere else. I felt it run through me coming down last night, and smelled it like any sewer: and if it was not in the air, why was my boat's crew taken first? Tell me that!"

There was no answer.

"Then I will tell you why they were taken first: because the mist, when we came through it, only rose five or six feet above the stream, and we were in it, while you on board were above it. And those taken on board this morning, every one of them, slept on the main-deck, and every one of them, too, was in fear of the fever. By this I judge two things: Keep as high as you can, and fear nothing but God, and we are all safe yet."

"But the fog was up to our round-tops at sunrise this morning," said Cary.

"I know it: but we who were on the half-deck were not in it so long as those below, and that may have made the difference, let alone our having free air. Besides, I suspect the heat in the evening draws the poison out more, and that when it gets cold toward morning, the venom of it goes off somehow."

How it went off Amyas could not tell (right in his facts as he was), for nobody on earth knew, I suppose, at that day; and it was not till nearly two centuries of fatal experience that the settlers in America discovered the simple laws of these epidemics which now every child knows, or ought to know. But common sense was on his side; and Yeo rose and spoke.

"As I have said before, many a time, the Lord has sent us a very young Daniel for judge. I remember now to have heard the Spaniards say, how these calentures lay always in the low ground, and never came more than a few hundred feet above the sea."

"Let us go up those few hundred feet, then."

Every man looked at Amyas, and then at his neighbor.

"Gentlemen, 'Look the devil straight in the face, if you would hit him in the right place.' We cannot get the ship to sea as she is; and if we could, we cannot go home empty-handed; and we surely cannot stay here to die of fever. We must leave the ship and go inland."

"Inland?" answered every voice but Yeo's.

"Up those hundred feet which Yeo talks of. Up to the mountains; stockade a camp, and get our sick and provisions thither."

"And what next?"

"And when we are recruited, march over the mountains, and surprise St. Jago de Leon."

Cary swore a great oath. "Amyas! You are a daring fellow!"

"Not a bit. It's the plain path of prudence."

"So it is, sir," said old Yeo, "and I follow you in it."

"And so do I," squeaked Jack Brimblecombe.

"Mr. Drew?"

"At your service, sir, to live or die. I know naught about stockading; but Sir Francis would have given the same counsel, I verily believe, if he had been in your place."

"Then tell the men that we start in an hour's time. Win over the Pelicans, Yeo and Drew; and the rest must follow, like sheep over a hedge."

The Pelicans and the liberated galley-slaves joined the project at once, but the rest gave Amyas a stormy hour. The great question was, where were the hills? In that dense mangrove thicket, they could not see fifty yards before them.

"The hills are not three miles to the south-west of you at this moment," said Amyas. "I marked every shoulder of them as we ran in."

"I suppose you meant to take us there?"

The question set a light to a train—and angry suspicions were blazing up one after another, but Amyas silenced them with a countermine. "Fools! If I had not wit enough to look ahead a little farther than you do, where would you be? Are you mad as well as reckless, to rise against your own captain because he has two strings to his bow? Go my way, I say, or, as I live, I'll blow up the ship and every soul on board, and save you the pain of rotting here by inches."

The men knew that Amyas never said what he did not intend to do; not that Amyas intended to do this, because he knew that the threat would be enough. So they agreed to go, and were reassured by seeing that the old Pelican's men turned to the work heartily and cheerfully.

There is no use keeping the reader for five or six weary hours under a broiling (or rather stewing) sun, stumbling over mangrove roots, hewing his way through thorny thickets, dragging sick men and provisions up mountain steeps, amid disappointment, fatigue, murmurs, curses, snakes, mosquitoes, false alarms of Spaniards, and every misery, save cold, which flesh is heir to. Suffice it that by sunset that evening they had gained a level spot, a full thousand feet above the sea, backed by an inaccessible cliff which formed the upper shoulder of a mighty mountain, defended below by steep wooded slopes, and needing but the felling of a few trees to make it impregnable.

Amyas settled the sick under the arched roots of an enormous cottonwood tree and made a second journey to the ship to bring up hammocks and blankets for them, while Yeo's wisdom and courage were of inestimable value. As pioneer, he had found the little brook up which they forced their way; he had encouraged them to climb the cliffs over which it fell, arguing rightly that on its course they were sure to find some ground fit for encampment within the reach of water; he had supported Amyas, when again and again the weary crew entreated to be dragged no farther, and had gone back again a dozen times to cheer them upward. Cary, who brought up the rear, bullied and cheered on the stragglers who sat down and refused to move, drove back at the sword's point more than one who was beating a retreat, carried their burdens for them, and sang them songs on the halt; in all things approving himself the gallant and hopeful soul which he had always been. Till Amyas, beside himself with joy at finding that the two men on whom he had counted most were utterly worthy of his trust, went so far as to whisper to them both, in confidence, that very night—

"Cortez burnt his ships when he landed. Why should not we?"

Yeo leapt upright; and then sat down again, and whispered—

"Do you say that, captain? 'Tis from above, then, that's certain; for it's been hanging on my mind too all day."

"There's no hurry," quoth Amyas; "we must clear her out first, you know," while Cary sat silent and musing. Amyas had evidently more schemes in his head than he chose to tell.

The men were too tired that evening to do much, but ere the sun rose next morning Amyas had them hard at work fortifying their position. It was, as I said, strong enough by nature; for though it was commanded by high cliffs on three sides, yet there was no chance of an enemy coming over the enormous mountain-range behind them, and still less chance that, if he came, he would discover them through the dense mass of trees which crowned the cliff, and clothed the hills for a thousand feet above. The attack, if it took place, would come from below; and against that Amyas guarded by felling the smaller trees, and laying them with their boughs outward over the crest of the slope, thus forming an abatis (as every one who has shot in thick cover knows to his cost) warranted to bring up in two steps, horse, dog, or man. The trunks were sawn into logs, laid lengthwise, and steadied by stakes and mould; and three or four hours' hard work finished a stockade which would defy anything but artillery. The work done, Amyas scrambled up into the boughs of the enormous ceiba-tree, and there sat inspecting his own handiwork, looking out far and wide over the forest-covered plains and the blue sea beyond, and thinking, in his simple straightforward way, of what was to be done next.

To stay there long was impossible; to avenge himself upon La Guayra was impossible; to go until he had found out whether Frank was alive or dead seemed at first equally impossible. But were Brimblecombe, Cary, and those eighty men to be sacrificed a second time to his private interest? Amyas wept with rage, and then wept again with earnest, honest prayer, before he could make up his mind. But he made it up. There were a hundred chances to one that Frank was dead; and if not, he was equally past their help; for he was—Amyas knew that too well—by this time in the hands of the Inquisition. Who could lift him from that pit? Not Amyas, at least! And crying aloud in his agony, "God help him! for I cannot!" Amyas made up his mind to move. But whither? Many an hour he thought and thought alone, there in his airy nest; and at last he went down, calm and cheerful, and drew Cary and Yeo aside. They could not, he said, refit the ship without dying of fever during the process; an assertion which neither of his hearers was bold enough to deny. Even if they refitted her, they would be pretty certain to have to fight the Spaniards again; for it was impossible to doubt the Indian's story, that they had been forewarned of the Rose's coming, or to doubt, either, that Eustace had been the traitor.

"Let us try St. Jago, then; sack it, come down on La Guayra in the rear, take a ship there, and so get home."

"Nay, Will. If they have strengthened themselves against us at La Guayra, where they had little to lose, surely they have done so at St. Jago, where they have much. I hear the town is large, though new; and besides, how can we get over these mountains without a guide?"

"Or with one?" said Cary, with a sigh, looking up at the vast walls of wood and rock which rose range on range for miles. "But it is strange to find you, at least, throwing cold water on a daring plot."

"What if I had a still more daring one? Did you ever hear of the golden city of Manoa?"

Yeo laughed a grim but joyful laugh. "I have, sir; and so have the old hands from the Pelican and the Jesus of Lubec, I doubt not."

"So much the better;" and Amyas began to tell Cary all which he had learned from the Spaniard, while Yeo capped every word thereof with rumors and traditions of his own gathering. Cary sat half aghast as the huge phantasmagoria unfolded itself before his dazzled eyes; and at last—

"So that was why you wanted to burn the ship! Well, after all, nobody needs me at home, and one less at table won't be missed. So you want to play Cortez, eh?"

"We shall never need to play Cortez (who was not such a bad fellow after all, Will), because we shall have no such cannibal fiends' tyranny to rid the earth of, as he had. And I trust we shall fear God enough not to play Pizarro."

So the conversation dropped for the time, but none of them forgot it.

In that mountain-nook the party spent some ten days and more. Several of the sick men died, some from the fever superadded to their wounds; some, probably, from having been bled by the

The narrative turns darker here, a necessary descent into the shadowed corners of history so that the true nature of our enemies may be understood. Three weeks have elapsed, and the setting shifts to a subterranean labyrinth of cells within Cartagena. A single door stands ajar, revealing a dim corridor where two cloaked figures wait. One is Eustace Leigh; the other, a confidant of the Holy Office.

In Eustace's hand burns a tallow lamp, its feeble light illuminating a straw mattress and the slumbering form of a man. We recognize the high, pale brow and delicate features—this is Frank. Rescued barely alive from the savagery of African captors, he is now reserved for the refined cruelty of civilized men. He underwent the rack this very afternoon, and now his betrayer, Eustace, stands before him. Whether to persuade or to trap, Eustace himself is uncertain, though his heart bleeds for his cousin.

Yet, Eustace has long ceased to value his own life. He acts solely out of a twisted sense of duty. He must wake the sleeper, coax, threaten, and force a recitation of faith while the victim's heart remains tender from the torture.

But look how peacefully Frank sleeps. Is it merely the play of lamplight, or does a faint smile grace his lips? As Eustace leans closer, he hears a whisper drifting from the dreamer's throat—a mother's name, followed by something far holier. The resolve crumbles instantly. Eustace cannot bring himself to rouse him.

"Let him rest," Eustace whispers to his companion. "I fear my words will be of little use."

"I fear so too, sir. Never did I witness such an obdurate heretic. He scoffed openly at their Holinesses."

"Ah!" Eustace replies, his voice tight. "Great is the depravity of the human heart, and the power of Satan! Let us depart for the present."

"Where is she?" the inquisitor asks.

"The elder sorceress, or the younger?"

"The younger—the—"

"Señora de Soto? Ah, poor soul! One might pity her, were she not a heretic." The man watches Eustace keenly, then adds softly, "She is currently with the notary, for the betterment of her soul, I trust."

Eustace halts, a shudder rattling through him. He struggles to form an "Amen!"

"Listen here," the inquisitor gestures to a nearby door. "We may eavesdrop a moment, provided you do not betray me, señor."

Eustace knows the man watches for any sign of compunction, ready to report the slightest flicker of sympathy to his superiors. Yet, a terrible curiosity overrides his terror. He pauses before the fatal door. His face burns, his knees knock together, and his heart hammers against his ribs as he presses himself against the wall, shielding his convulsed countenance.

Inside, a man's voice is clear and low. The notary repeats the ancient accusation of witchcraft, a charge the Inquisition uses to justify their conscience or whitewash their villainy for the mob. Then, Eustace's soul seems to sink as a woman's voice answers, sharpened by indignation and agony.

"Witchcraft against Don Guzman? What need of that, oh God! What need?"

"You deny it then, Señora? We are sorry for you; but—"

A confused, choking murmur follows, words that mean nothing or everything.

"She has confessed!" Eustace whispers, tears streaming down his face. "Saints, I thank you! She—"

A wail erupts, piercing through Eustace's ears, brain, and heart. He would tear the door open, but his companion drags him away. Wail after wail follows, the wretched man hurrying off, stopping his ears in vain against those piercing cries that haunt him like avenging angels through the dreadful vaults.

He escapes into the fragrant open air, under the golden moonlight of the tropics, into a garden that could serve as a model for Eden. Yet, man's hell follows him into God's heaven; the wails still ring in his mind.

"Oh, misery, misery, misery!" he murmurs through grinding teeth. "And I have brought her to this! I had to bring her to it! What else could I? Who dares blame me? And yet, what devilish sin have I committed that requires such punishment? Was there no one else? No one? And yet, it may save her soul. It may bring her to repentance!"

"It may, indeed; for she is delicate and cannot endure much. You ought to know as well as I, señor, the merciful disposition of the Holy Office."

"I know it, I know it," Eustace interrupts, trembling now for himself. "All in love—all in love. A paternal chastisement—"

"And the proofs of heresy are patent, alongside the strong suspicion of enchantment and the notorious character of the elder sorceress. You yourself admitted she was a witch in England before she brought her here as an attendant."

"Of course she was. Of course. Yes; there was no other course open. And though my flesh is weak, sir, none can prove better to the Holy Office how willing my spirit is!"

Thus, Eustace departs. Before another sun sets, he seeks out the principal of the Jesuits, pouring out his heart—or as much of it as he dares—and begging permission to finish his novitiate and enter the order, with the understanding that he be sent immediately back to Europe or elsewhere. "Otherwise," he states frankly, "I shall go mad, even if I am not mad already."

The Jesuit, a kindly man, approaches the Holy Office and settles the matter, recounting Eustace's past valiant services to the Church to place him beyond suspicion. His testimony is no longer required. That very night, Eustace leaves Cartagena for Nombre, sailing the following week to a destination unknown.

I say, I know not whither. Eustace Leigh vanishes from these pages. He may have risen as General of his Order, or worn out his years in some tropical forest, "conquering the souls"—and inevitably the bodies—of the Indians. Perhaps he returned to England and became the very Ballard hanged and quartered three years later for his part in Babington's conspiracy. I do not know.

This book is a chronicle of men—their virtues and sins, victories and defeats. But Eustace is no longer a man. He has become a thing, a tool, a Jesuit who moves only where sent, doing good or evil indifferently as commanded. By an act of moral suicide, he has lost his soul in the desperate hope of saving it; stripped of will, conscience, and responsibility to God or man, he answers only to "The Society." In a word, as Eustace himself admits, he is "dead." Twice dead, I fear. Let the dead bury their dead. We have no further concern with Eustace Leigh.

"My mariners,
Souls that have toil'd, and wrought, and thought with
me—Death closes all: but something ere the end,
Some work of noble note, may yet be done,
Not unbecoming men that strove with gods!"

TENNYSON'S Ulysses.

Nearly three years had slipped away since that small band knelt at evensong beneath the giant tree of Guayra. Years of apparent blankness, traceable only by scattered notes and misspelled names. Through untrodden hills and forests, spanning some eight hundred miles in length by four hundred in breadth, they had hunted for the Golden City, seeking in vain. They had searched along the wooded banks of the Orinoco, beyond the roaring foam of Maypures, and up the upper waters of the mighty Amazon. They had ascended streams into Peru itself, trampling the cinchona groves of Loxa, ignorant as the world then was of their healing virtues. They had witnessed the virgin snows of Chimborazo towering white above thunderclouds and the giant cone of Cotopaxi blackening in sullen wrath before fiery streams rolled down its sides. Foiled at the back of the Andes, they had turned eastward, plunging from alpine cliffs into "the green and misty ocean of the Montana." Slowly and painfully, they had worked their way northward again, along the eastern foot of the inland Cordillera, and now they bivouacked upon one of the many feeders of the Meta, flowing down from the Suma Paz into the forest-covered plains.

There they sat, their watch-fires glittering on the stream beneath enormous trees. Amyas and Cary, Brimblecombe, Yeo, and the Indian lad who had followed them through all their wanderings were alive and well; yet as far as ever from Manoa, its fairy lake, golden palaces, and the wonders of the Indian's tale. Again and again in their wanderings they had heard faint rumors of its existence, starting off in fresh directions only to meet fresh disappointment, a hope deferred that maketh sick the heart.

There they sat at last—forty-four men out of the eighty-four who left the tree of Guayra. Where were the rest?

"Their bones are scatter'd far and wide,
By mount, by stream, and sea."

Drew, the master, lay on the banks of the Rio Negro, five brave fellows by him, slain in fight by poisoned arrows in a vain attempt to penetrate the mountain-gorges of the Parima. Two more lay amid the valleys of the Andes, frozen to death by fierce slaty hail sweeping down from the condor's eyrie; four more were drowned at the rapids of the Orinoco; five or six wounded men remained behind at another rapid among friendly Indians, to be recovered perhaps never. Fever, snakes, jaguars, alligators, cannibal fish, electric eels, had thinned their ranks month by month. Of their march through the primeval wilderness no track remained except those lonely graves.

And there the survivors sat beside the silent stream beneath the tropic moon. Sun-dried and lean, yet strong and bold as ever, with the quiet fire of English courage burning undimmed in every eye and the genial smile of English mirth fresh on every lip. They made a jest of danger and a sport of toil, as cheerily as when they sailed over the bar of Bideford in days belonging to some antenatal life. Their beards had grown down upon their breasts; their long hair was knotted on their heads like women's to keep off the burning sunshine; their leggings were of the skin of the delicate Guazu-puti deer; their shirts were patched with Indian cotton web. The spoils of jaguar, puma, and ape hung from their shoulders. Their ammunition was long since spent; their muskets, spoiled by the perpetual vapor-bath of the steaming woods, were left behind as useless in a cave by some cataract of the Orinoco. But their swords were bright and terrible as ever, and they carried bows of a strength which no Indian arm could bend, arrows pointed with the remnants of their armor. Many were armed with the pocuna or blowgun of the Indians—more deadly, because more silent, than the firearms they had abandoned. Thus they had wandered, and thus they would wander still, the lords of the forest and its beasts; terrible to all hostile Indians, but kindly, just, and generous to all who dealt faithfully with them. Many a smooth-chinned Carib and Ature, Solimo and Guahiba, recounted with wonder and admiration the righteousness of the bearded heroes, who proclaimed themselves the deadly foes of the faithless and murderous Spaniard and spoke of the great and good queen beyond the seas, who would send her warriors to deliver and avenge the oppressed Indian.

The men slept among the trees, some on the ground, some in grass-hammocks slung between stems. All was silent save the heavy plunge of the tapir in the river as he tore up water-weeds for his night's repast. Sometimes the jaguar, climbing from tree-top to tree-top after his prey, awakened the monkeys clustered on the boughs, which again aroused the birds. Ten minutes of unearthly roars, howls, shrieks, and cacklings made the forest ring as if all pandemonium had broken loose; but that soon died away again. Even while it lasted, it was too common a matter to awaken the sleepers, much less to interrupt the council of war going on beside the watch-fire between the three adventurers and the faithful Yeo. A hundred times they had held such councils in vain, and for aught they knew, this one would be as fruitless as those gone before. Nevertheless, it was a more solemn one than usual, for the two years during which they had agreed to search for Manoa were long past, and some new place must be determined on unless they intended to spend the rest of their lives in that green wilderness.

"Well," said Will Cary, taking his cigar from his mouth, "at least we have got something out of those last Indians. It is a comfort to have a puff at tobacco once more, after three weeks' fasting."

"For me," said Jack Brimblecombe, "Heaven forgive me! but when I get the magical leaf between my teeth again, I feel tempted to sit as still as a chimney and smoke till my dying day without stirring hand or foot."

"Then I shall forbid you tobacco, Master Parson," said Amyas. "For we must be up and away again to-morrow. We have been idling here three mortal days, and nothing done."

"Shall we ever do anything? I think the gold of Manoa is like the gold which lies where the rainbow touches the ground, always a field beyond you."

Amyas was silent awhile, and so were the rest. There was no denying that their hopes were all but gone. In the immense circuit they had made, they had met with nothing but disappointment.

"There is but one more chance," said he at length. "That is the mountains to the east of the Orinoco, where we failed the first time. The Incas may have moved on to them when they escaped."

"Why not?" said Cary. "They would so put all the forests, beside the Llanos and half-a-dozen great rivers, between them and those dogs of Spaniards."

"Shall we try it once more?" said Amyas. "This river ought to run into the Orinoco; and once there, we are again at the very foot of the mountains. What say you, Yeo?"

"I cannot but mind, your worship, that when we came up the Orinoco, the Indians told us terrible stories of those mountains, how far they stretched, and how difficult they were to cross by reason of the cliffs aloft and the thick forests in the valleys. And have we not lost five good men there already?"

"What care we? No forests can be thicker than those we have bored through already. Why, if one had but a tail, like a monkey, for an extra warp, one might have gone a hundred miles on end along the tree-tops and found it far pleasanter walking than tripping in withes and being eaten up with creeping things from morn till night."

"But remember, too," said Jack, "how they told us to beware of the Amazons."

"What, Jack, afraid of a parcel of women?"

"Why not?" said Jack. "I wouldn't run from a man, as you know; but a woman—it's not natural, like. They must be witches or devils. See how the Caribs feared them. And there were men there without necks and with their eyes in their breasts, they said. Now how could a Christian tackle such customers as them?"

"He couldn't cut off their heads, that's certain; but I suppose a poke in the ribs will do as much for them as for their neighbors."

"Well," said Jack, "if I fight, let me fight honest flesh and blood, that's all, and none of these outlandish monsters. How do you know but that they are invulnerable by art-magic?"

"How do you know that they are? And as for the Amazons," said Cary, "woman's woman, all the world over. I'll bet that you may wheedle them round with a compliment or two, just as if they were so many burghers' wives. Pity I have not a court-suit and a Spanish hat. I would have taken an orange in one hand and a handkerchief in the other, gone all alone to them as ambassador, and been in a week as great with Queen Blackfacealinda as ever Raleigh is at Whitehall."

"Gentlemen!" said Yeo. "Where you go, I go; and not only I, but every man of us, I doubt not. But we have lost now half our company and spent our ammunition, so we are no better men, were it not for our swords, than these naked heathens round us. Now it was, as you all know, by the wonder and noise of their ordnance (let alone their horses, which is a break-neck beast I put no faith in) that both Cortez and Pizarro, those imps of Satan, made their golden conquests. With which if we could have astounded the people of Manoa—"

"Having first found the said people," laughed Amyas. "It is like the old fable. Every craftsman thinks his own trade the one pillar of the commonweal."

"Well! your worship," quoth Yeo, "it may be that being a gunner I overprize guns. But it don't need slate and pencil to do this sum—are forty men without shot as good as eighty with?"

"Thou art right, old fellow, right enough, and I was only jesting for very sorrow and must needs laugh about it lest I weep about it. Our chance is over, I believe, though I dare not confess as much to the men."

"Sir," said Yeo, "I have a feeling on me that the Lord's hand is against us in this matter. Whether He means to keep this wealth for worthier men than us, or whether it is His will to hide this great city in the secret place of His presence from the strife of tongues, and so to spare them from sinful man's covetousness, and England from that sin and luxury which I have seen gold beget among the Spaniards, I know not, sir; for who knoweth the counsels of the Lord? But I have long had a voice within which saith, 'Salvation Yeo, thou shalt never behold the Golden City which is on earth, where heathens worship sun and moon and the hosts of heaven; be content, therefore, to see that Golden City which is above, where is neither sun nor moon, but the Lord God and the Lamb are the light thereof.'"

There was a simple majesty about old Yeo when he broke forth in utterances like these, which made his comrades, and even Amyas and Cary, look on him as Mussulmans look on madmen, as possessed of mysterious knowledge and flashes of inspiration. Brimblecombe, whose pious soul looked up to the old hero with a reverence which had overcome all his Churchman's prejudices against Anabaptists, answered gently, "Amen! amen! my masters all: and it has been on my mind, too, this long time, that there is a providence against our going east; for see how this two years past, whenever we have pushed eastward, we have fallen into trouble and lost good men; and whenever we went Westward-ho, we have prospered; and do prosper to this day."

"And what is more, gentlemen," said Yeo, "if, as Scripture says, dreams are from the Lord, I verily believe mine last night came from Him. For as I lay by the fire, sirs, I heard my little maid's voice calling of me, as plain as ever I heard in my life; and the very same words, sirs, which she learned from me and my good comrade William Penberthy to say, 'Westward-ho! jolly mariners all!' a bit of an ungodly song, my masters, which we sang in our wild days; but she stood and called it as plain as ever mortal ears heard, and called again till I answered, 'Coming! my maid, coming!' and after that the dear chuck called no more—God grant I find her yet!--and so I woke."

Cary had long since given up laughing at Yeo about the "little maid"; and Amyas answered, "So let it be, Yeo, if the rest agree: but what shall we do to the westward?"

"Do?" said Cary. "There's plenty to do; for there's plenty of gold, and plenty of Spaniards, too, they say, on the other side of these mountains: so that our swords will not rust for lack of adventures, my gay knights-errant all."

So they chatted on; and before night was half through a plan was matured, desperate enough—but what cared those brave hearts for that? They would cross the Cordillera to Santa Fe de Bogota, of the wealth whereof both Yeo and Amyas had often

"Let us alone. What pleasure can we have
To war with evil? Is there any peace
In always climbing up the climbing wave?
All things have rest, and ripen toward the grave
In silence; ripen, fall, and cease:
Give us long rest or death, dark death, or dreamful ease."

Alexander von Humboldt, in his travels, once observed a group of Indians squatting around their fires, smeared with grease and paint, eating ants and clay. He naively remarked that without the comforting doctrine of science—which claims such behavior is merely the crude starting point of humanity—he might have viewed them as the final, degraded remnants of a fallen race. It is a pity the great traveler did not succumb to that temptation, choosing instead to cling to the dogmas of modern anthropology rather than the older, more honest biblical truth: that man in a state of nature is a fallen being, destined for death. It is far more hopeful to believe these poor souls were once nobler and have declined, than to believe they have always been blockheads incapable of improvement.

Our voyagers, however, cared little for such philosophical debates. Accepting the Bible as their guide, they sided with Humboldt's common sense rather than his scientific theories. To them, these new hosts were immortal souls, "captivated by the devil at his will," lost in the pathless forests and likely to remain lost forever. And indeed, the facts seemed to support their fears. While these Indians had not sunk quite as low as the Guahibas they had encountered lower down the river, the signs of the evil spirit were unmistakable.

Upon landing, they found a scattered village of palm-leaf sheds with hammocks strung between trees. Patches of cassava and indigo plants dotted the forest floor, suggesting a level of neatness superior to the average settlement. Yet, immediately flanking the landing place were four or five stout fellows wielding tall drums and long earthen trumpets. The moment the strangers stepped ashore, a deafening cacophony of howls, groans, and thumps erupted, fully justifying Yeo's remark, "They are calling upon their devil, sir." Cary, with a touch of reason, added that the Indians were unlikely to be disappointed, for "none but Sir Urian would ever come to listen to such a noise."

"And you mark, sirs," said Yeo, "there's some feast or sacrifice toward. I'm not overconfident of them yet."

"Nonsense!" said Amyas. "We could kill every soul of them in half an hour, and they know that as well as we."

Yet a great demonstration was clearly underway. The forest children were arrayed in two lines, men in front and women behind, bedizened with arnotto, indigo, and feathers. Suddenly, a figure leapt into the center of the clearing—a personage who could not have complained if anyone had mistaken him for the devil himself. Dressed in a jaguar-skin with a long tail, grinning teeth, horns, a plume of black and yellow feathers, and a huge rattle, he produced a hideous yell.

"Here's the Piache, the rascal," said Amyas.

"Ay," said Yeo, "in Satan's livery, and I've no doubt his works are according, trust him for it."

"Don't be frightened, Jack," said Cary, stepping back to support Brimblecombe. "It's your business to tackle him, you know. At him boldly, and he'll run."

The men laughed, and the Piache, realizing his solemn impression had failed, recovered himself. He smote a musician over the head with his rattle to procure silence and began a harangue, to which Amyas listened patiently, cigar in mouth.

"What's it all about, boy?"

"He wants to know whether you have seen Amalivaca on the other shore of the great water?"

Amyas, accustomed to this inquiry regarding the mythic civilizer who carved the inland cliffs before vanishing beyond the ocean, answered by praising Queen Elizabeth. The Piache replied that she must be one of Amalivaca's seven daughters, some of whom he had taken back while breaking the legs of the rest to prevent escape. Amyas corrected him, stating that his queen's legs were certainly not broken, for she was a model of grace and the best dancer in her dominions. More importantly, he asked if the tribe would provide cassava bread and allow them to rest on the island before fighting the Spaniards across the mountains.

The Piache, capering and howling, beckoned Amyas and his party to follow him. Seeing the Indians were unarmed and in high spirits, they complied. The Piache led them to a carefully closed hut, crawling up to it on all fours in an abject fashion to consult the "Daughter of the Sun."

"Ask what he is about, boy."

The lad asked the old cacique, who replied that the Piache was consulting the Daughter of the Sun.

"Here is our mare's nest at last," quoth Cary, as the Piache rose from whines to screams, gesticulating violently until he foamed at the mouth and collapsed, exhausted.

"As good as a stage play."

"The devil has played his part," said Jack. "And now, by the rules of all plays, Vice should come on."

"And a very fair Vice it will be, I suspect; a right sweet Iniquity, my Jack! Listen."

From within the hut rose a low, sweet song. The simple Indians bowed their heads in reverence, and the English were hushed in astonishment. The voice was not shrill or guttural like an Indian's, but round, clear, and rich, like a European's. As it swelled, displaying a compass and power extraordinary for any singer, the wild wanderers were rapt in new delight. The notes leaped and ran, perfect in melody like a bird on a bough, causing the Indians to welcome them as messengers from a higher world. Finally, one triumphant burst rang out, followed by dead silence. The Piache, suddenly restored, jumped upright and recommenced his preaching.

"Tell the howling villain to make short work of it, lad! His tune won't do after that last one."

The lad grinned, informing Amyas that the Piache signified their acceptance as friends by the Daughter of the Sun. Whereupon the Indians screamed with delight, and Amyas, rolling another tobacco leaf, asked, "Then let her give us some cassava."

The door opened, and the Indians prostrated themselves as a fair apparition emerged—the same maiden they had seen on the island, now decked in feather-robes of every imaginable hue. She walked slowly and stately to Amyas, glancing proudly at her prostrate adorers. Pointing gracefully to the trees, gardens, and huts, she conveyed by expressive signs that all was at his service. Taking his hand, she lifted it gently to her forehead.

At this sign of submission, a shout of rapture rose from the crowd. As the mysterious maiden retired, the English were pressed by adoring Indians who marveled at their swords, bows, and beast trophies. Women hastened to bring fruit, flowers, cassava, and calabashes of intoxicating drink. The English sat beneath the trees, feasting merrily while drums made hideous music and lithe young girls performed uncouth dances that scandalized Brimblecombe and Yeo. Persuaded to beat an early retreat, Amyas returned to the island-fortress, racking his brains to identify the maiden.

Amyas settled in his mind that she was one of the lost Inca race, perhaps a descendant of the fair girl, wife of the Inca Manco, whom Pizarro had tortured and killed forty years prior. That evening, after their customary psalm and catch, twenty mellow voices rang out above the roar of the cataract. Once or twice they thought they heard an echo, but it was only until Cary returned from a walk that they realized the truth.

"The sweet Iniquity is mimicking us, lad."

They went to the river's brink and heard plainly the same voice repeating snatches of the airs they had sung. The effect was strange and solemn: the men's deep voices answered out of the dark forest by those sweet treble notes. They stood long listening to the eddies swirling golden in the moonlight, seeing nothing beyond the black wall of trees, before returning to dream of Incas and nightingales.

Over the next week, the maiden appeared rarely, keeping her distance as haughtily as a queen. When Amyas questioned the cacique about her, the old man made an owl's face and intimated she was a very strange personage, a "child of the Sun," and that the less said about her the better. Cary, however, threatened to blight all the cassava plants unless the cacique explained that they were also children of the Sun, sent to inquire how the Indians had treated their step-sister.

The cacique, taking them in his canoe a mile down the stream, told them in a rhythmic chant how his tribe had once been a mighty nation in Papamene until the Spaniards drove them forth. Wandering northward beneath the cone of Cotopaxi, they had found this fair creature, about the size of a seven-year-old, wandering in the forest. Wondering at her white skin and delicate beauty, they worshipped her as a god and led her home. Realizing she was human, their wonder scarcely lessened; how could such a tender being survive the forests and escape the jaguar? She must be under Divine protection, a daughter of the Sun, one of the mighty Inca race whose fall had reached even these lonely wildernesses. They believed she would rise again to power when bearded white men came to restore them to their ancient throne.

As she grew, she was tended with royal honors by the tribe's conjuror to propitiate the Sun. She became a prophetess as well as an object of fetish-worship, more prudent in council, valiant in war, and cunning in the chase than all the elders. Her strange, sweet songs were full of mysterious wisdom about birds, animals, and flowers, taught her by the Sun and the Good Spirit. She remained unmarried, despising Indian youths and forbidden by the conjuror to mingle with the race of the Sun.

Cary remarked that the conjuror made a very good thing of it, but Amyas was silent, full of dreams about Manoa. Perhaps the Incas had moved eastward to escape the Spaniards, leaving this girl behind. He sighed at the hopelessness of searching with his diminished band, yet he hoped to learn the truth from the maiden herself. If he could get home safely, many gallant gentlemen, including Raleigh, would join him in a fresh search for the Golden City of Guiana.

The cacique entreated them to pray to the Sun for his descendant and begged them not to take her away, lest the Sun forget the poor Omaguas. Amyas had no wish to stay longer than necessary to retrieve the sick men from the Orinoco, a journey likely to take months.

Cary volunteered to undertake the adventure with half a dozen men and Indian help, but obtaining the latter was difficult. The tribe feared the fierce Guahibas, and every Indian tribe viewed others of different languages as natural enemies. Amyas and his party attributed this strange fact to the delusion of the devil, the divider and accuser.

Whether pride or shyness kept the maiden aloof, she eventually conquered it. She gave the English to understand that none of them was to be her companion but Amyas. Ere a month was past, she was often hunting with him in the forest, accompanied by a train of chosen nymphs who had persuaded their peers to spurn the dusky suitors. This fashion, common among Indian tribes where women escape male tyranny, convinced the English they were near the land of the famous Amazons. Amyas had no doubt that, as a descendant of the Incas, the maiden preserved the tradition of the Virgins of the Sun and their austere monastic rule.

A harmless friendship sprang up between Amyas and the girl, which soon turned to good account. Upon hearing he needed a crew for Cary's expedition, she consulted the Piache, assembled the tribe, and retired to her hut to commence a song. Unless the Piache lied, this song was a command to furnish young men for the expedition under penalty of the sovereign displeasure of an evil spirit with an unpronounceable name. The argument succeeded on the spot, and the canoe departed on its perilous errand.

John Brimblecombe doubted whether a venture started by the direct patronage of the fiend would succeed. Amyas, disliking the humbug, told Ayacanora it would be better to claim the deed pleased the Good Spirit.

"Ah!" said she, naively, "they know better than that. The Good Spirit is big and lazy; he smiles and takes no trouble. But the little bad spirit is so busy—here, and there, and everywhere. He is the useful one to have for a friend!"

The Piache much approved this sentiment. He told Brimblecombe sharply that if the Indians began asking the Good Spirit for what they wanted, who would bring him cassava and coca for keeping the bad spirit quiet? This argument, though forcible, did not stop Jack's preaching or the morning and evening services in the island camp. The Indians, attracted by the singing, attended in such numbers that the Piache found his occupation gone and vowed to put an end to Jack's Gospel with a poisoned arrow.

Blinded by his master, Satan, the Piache attempted to impart this plan to Ayacanora, expecting her partnership in tithes and offerings. He was exceedingly astonished to receive a box on the ear and a storm of abuse. Afterward, Ayacanora went to Amyas and proposed that the Piache be thrown to the alligators and Jack installed in his place, declaring that whatever the bearded men said must be true and whoever plotted against them should die.

Jack, however, magnanimously forgave his foe and preached on with fresh zeal, though not with much success. The conjuror, though his main treasure had gone to the enemy, retained a reserve in a

God will relent, and quit thee all thy debt,
Who ever more approves, and more accepts
Him who imploring mercy sues for life,
Than who self-rigorous chooses death as due,
Which argues over-just, and self-displeased
For self-offence, more than for God offended.

A fortnight of severe toil had passed since they bade farewell to the green ocean of the eastern plains. They had crossed the Cordillera, casting a longing glance at Santa Fe, the wealthy city nestled in gardens upon its lofty plateau, only to realize it was far too vast for their ambitions. Yet, their time was not wasted. Their Indian lad had discovered a gold-train descending from Santa Fe toward the Magdalena, and they waited for it beside a miserable rut that served as a road. Encamped in a forest of oaks that might have reminded them of Europe were it not for the tree-ferns forming the undergrowth and the deep gorges opening at their feet, they found themselves in a strange limbo. Above them blew the cool breezes of a temperate zone, while far below, dim through an everlasting vapor-bath of rank hot steam, lay the mighty forms and gorgeous colors of the tropic forest.

They had pitched their camp among the tree-ferns, overlooking a steep hillside where a road once existed, perhaps when Cundinamarca was a civilized kingdom. Now, Spanish misrule had reduced it to a few steps slipping from their places at the bottom of a narrow mud ditch. Like the aqueducts, bridges, and post-houses of that strange empire, all traces of civilization had decayed in the mad search for gold, save architecture alone, which survived only in the splendid cathedrals rising upon the ruins of the temples of the Sun. Rapid tropic vegetation had reclaimed its old domains, leaving Amyas and his crew utterly alone, within miles of an important Spanish settlement, as isolated as they would be in the solitudes of the Orinoco or the Amazon.

Attempts to find sulphur and nitre had failed, forcing them to rely on swords and arrows—a method that disgusted Yeo, though Amyas reminded him that Drake had taken Nombre de Dios with no better weapons. Blocking the road above by felling a large tree across it, the men sat among the flowers chewing coca, meditating on a mysterious roar that had followed them nightly since leaving the banks of the Meta. It was neither the snap of a branch nor the call of a bird, yet they dismissed it, for the gold train was far more important than any noise.

Suddenly, a sharp crack and a loud cry echoed from beneath. "That was a whip's crack," said Yeo, "and a woman's wail. They are close here, lads!" "A woman's?" Amyas asked. "Do they drive women in their gangs?" "Why not, the brutes?" Yeo replied, pointing out the glittering basnets of the approaching column.

Amyas ordered silence, instructing them to wait for his signal before shooting, then to use arrows first, followed by swords. Up came the troop, slowly, and hearts beat loud at their coming. First, about twenty soldiers, half on foot and half borne in chairs on the backs of single Indians, while the marchers had consigned their heavy armor and arquebuses to attendant slaves who were pricked on at will by the pikes of the soldiers behind them. "The men are mad to let their ordnance out of their hands," observed someone. "An Indian will pray to an arquebus not to shoot him; he sure their artillery is safe enough," said Yeo.

Following the soldiers came a procession that made them forget all else: a sad and hideous sight, yet one too common in those remoter districts where humane edicts were disregarded. A line of Indians, Negroes, and Zambos, naked, emaciated, scarred with whips and fetters, and chained together by their left wrists, toiled upwards, panting under the burden of baskets held by straps across their foreheads. Among them were old men, youths, women, and slender young girls with children at their knees. A low murmur of indignation rose from the ambushed Englishmen, worthy of the free and righteous hearts of those days, when Raleigh could appeal to man and God on the ground of common humanity, ere the seventeenth century had brutalized them with the fatal gift of negro-slaves.

The first forty bore a burden that made all but Amyas and Yeo forget the wretches carrying it. Each basket contained a square package of carefully corded hide. "What's in them, captain?" "Gold!" And at that magic word, all eyes strained greedily forward. Amyas had to whisper, "Be men, be men, or you will spoil all yet!" The last twenty bore larger baskets, seemingly with food, followed by twenty more soldiers and the officer in charge, who smiled away in his chair, twirling his huge mustachios, unaware of the English arrows itching to be through his ribs.

Amyas felt a shrinking from drawing his bow in cool blood on men so utterly unsuspicious and defenseless, even though they were committing devilish cruelty. Drivers armed with whips lingered up and down the slowly staggering file, avenging every lagging or stumble with a blow of the cruel manati-hide that cracked like a pistol-shot against the naked limbs of the silent victims.

Suddenly, the *casus belli* arose of its own accord. The last but one of the chained line was an old gray-headed man, followed by a slender, graceful girl of about eighteen. Amyas's heart yearned over them as they came up. Just as they passed, the foremost of the file rounded the corner above; there was a bustle, and a voice shouted, "Halt, senors! there is a tree across the path!"

"A tree across the path?" bellowed the officer, with a variety of passionate addresses to the Mother of Heaven and various saints, while the trembling Indians surged up and down upon the ruinous steps until the poor old man fell groveling on his face. The officer leaped down and hurried upward. Finding the old man, he pricked the prostrate wretch with the point of his sword. "Sin peccado concebida! Grandfather of Beelzebub, is this a place to lie worshipping your fiends?" The old man tried to rise, but the weight on his head was too much; he fell again, motionless. The driver applied the manati-hide across his loins with fearful force, declaring him "used up."

"What does the intendant mean by sending me out with worn-out cattle like these? Forward there!" shouted the officer. "Clear away the tree, senors, and I'll soon clear the chain. Hold it up, Pedrillo!" The driver held up the chain fastened to the old man's wrist. The officer stepped back and flourished a Toledo blade, whose beauty made Amyas break the Tenth Commandment on the spot. The man was tall, handsome, and broad-shouldered. Amyas thought he was going to display the strength of his arm in severing the chain at one stroke.

Even he was not prepared for the recondite fancies of a Spanish adventurer. The blade gleamed in the air, once, twice, and fell—not on the chain, but on the wrist which it fettered. There was a shriek, a crimson flash, and the chain and its prisoner were parted indeed. One moment more, and Amyas's arrow would have been through the throat of the murderer, who paused, regarding his workmanship with a satisfied smile; but vengeance was not to come from him.

Quick and fierce as a tiger-cat, the girl sprang on the ruffian, clasped him in her arms with the intense strength of passion, and leaped with him from the narrow ledge into the abyss below. There was a rush, a shout; all faces were bent over the precipice. The girl hung by her chained wrist; the officer was gone. There was a moment's awful silence; and then Amyas heard his body crashing through the tree-tops far below.

"Haul her up! Hew her in pieces! Burn the witch!" shouted the driver, pulling at the chain while all sprang from their chairs to stoop over the brink. Now was the time for Amyas! Heaven had delivered them into his hands. Swift and sure, at ten yards off, his arrow rushed through the body of the driver, and then, with a roar as of the leaping lion, he sprang like an avenging angel into the midst of the astonished ruffians.

His first thought was for the girl. In a moment, by sheer strength, he had jerked her safely up into the road, while the Spaniards recoiled right and left, fancying him for the moment some mountain giant or supernatural foe. His hurrah undeceived them in an instant, and a cry of "English! Lutheran dogs!" arose, but too late. The men of Devon had followed their captain's lead: a storm of arrows left five Spaniards dead and a dozen more wounded, and down leapt Salvation Yeo, his white hair streaming behind him, with twenty good swords more, and the work of death began.

The Spaniards fought like lions, but they had no time to fix their arquebuses on the crutches, nor room in that narrow path to use their pikes. The English had the wall of them, and to have the wall there was to have the foe's life at their mercy. Five desperate minutes, and not a living Spaniard stood upon those steps; certainly no living one lay in the green abyss below. Two only, who were behind the rest and in full armor, escaped without mortal wound and fled down the hill again.

"After them! Michael Evans and Simon Heard; and catch them, if they run a league." The two long and lean Clovelly men, active as deer from forest training, ran two feet for the Spaniard's one; and in ten minutes returned, having done their work. Amyas and his men hurried past the Indians to help Cary and the party forward, where shouts and musket shots announced a sharp affray. Their arrival settled the matter. All the Spaniards fell but three or four, who scrambled down the crannies of the cliff.

"Let not one of them escape! Slay them as Israel slew Amalek!" cried Yeo, and ere the wretches could reach a place of shelter, an arrow was quivering in each body as it rolled lifeless down the rocks. "Now then! Loose the Indians!" They found armorer's tools on one of the dead bodies, and it was done.

"We are your friends," said Amyas. "All we ask is, that you shall help us to carry this gold down to the Magdalena, and then you are free." Some few of the younger groveled at his knees, kissing his feet and hailing him as the child of the Sun; but the most part kept a stolid indifference, sitting quietly where they stood, staring into vacancy. The iron had entered too deeply into their souls. They seemed past hope, enjoyment, even understanding.

But the young girl, who was last of all in the line, as soon as she was loosed, sprang to her father's body. Speaking no word, she lifted it in her thin arms, laid it across her knees, kissed the fallen lips, and stroked the furrowed cheeks, murmuring inarticulate sounds like the cooing of a woodland dove, of which none knew the meaning but she. Suddenly the truth flashed on her; silent as ever, she drew one long heaving breath, rose erect with the body in her arms, and leaped into the abyss.

They watched her dark and slender limbs, twined closely round the old man's corpse, turn over and over until a crash among the leaves and a scream among the birds told that she had reached the trees; and the green roof hid her from their view. "Brave lass!" shouted a sailor. "The Lord forgive her!" said Yeo. "But, your worship, we must have these rascals' ordnance." "And their clothes too, Yeo, if we wish to get down the Magdalena unchallenged."

Amyas reminded them to remember Mr. Oxenham and his crew, warning them not to make God's gift their ruin by faithlessness, greediness, or mutinous haste. "You shall find none in us!" cried several men. "We know your worship. We can trust our general." "Thank God!" said Amyas. "Now then, it will be no shame or sin to make the Indians carry it, saving the women, whom God forbid we should burden. But we must pass through the very heart of the Spanish settlements, and by the town of Saint Martha itself. So the clothes and weapons of these Spaniards we must have, let it cost us what labor it may."

"How many lie in the road?" "Thirteen here, and about ten up above," said Cary. "Then there are near twenty missing. Who will volunteer to go down over cliff, and bring up the spoil of them?" "I, and I, and I;" and a dozen stepped out, for they knew that Amyas meant to help at the doing of it himself.

"Very well, then, follow me. Sir John, take the Indian lad for your interpreter, and try and comfort the souls of these poor heathens. Tell them that they shall all be free." "Why, who is that comes up the road?" All eyes turned in the direction he spoke. And, wonder of wonders! up came none other than Ayacanora herself, blow-gun in hand, bow on back, and bedecked in all her feather garments, which were rather the worse for a fortnight's woodland travel.

All stood mute with astonishment as, seeing Amyas, she uttered a cry of joy, quickened her pace into a run, and at last fell panting and exhausted at his feet. "I have found you!" she said; "you ran away from me, but you could not escape me!" And she fawned round Amyas, like a dog who has found his master, and then sat down on the bank, bursting into wild sobs. "God help us!" said Amyas, clutching his hair as he looked down upon the beautiful weeper. "What am I to do with her, over and above all these poor heathens?" But there was no

The golden tropic sea stretched out before them, a shimmering expanse of living flame under the evening sky. On the shore of New Granada, the bay of Santa Marta rippled in the land-breeze, while the mighty forests sparkled with the light of myriad fireflies. High above, nineteen thousand feet aloft, the peak of Horqueta cleaved the dark-blue vault of heaven, its rose-red cone fading to a dull leaden hue as the stars began to flash out one by one. Venus, like a second moon, tinged the eastern snows with gold, casting a long, rippling line of light across the water. Everywhere was glory and richness; the heavens, hills, and sea formed a sparkling garland of jewels, leading one to wonder if the soil beneath was equally jewelled with emeralds, rubies, and grains of gold.

Inside the state cabin of the great galleon, *The City of the True Cross*, the Bishop of Cartagena sat in a state of holy calm. His stout figure rested on an easy-chair, his ankles propped on another, beside a table laden with the fruits of the Indies—oranges, limes, guavas, and pineapples. An Indian girl, bedizened with scarfs and gold chains, kept the flies at bay with a feather fan, while flasks of virtuous wine from Alicant stood in a pail of ice brought down from the Horqueta. The bishop was not alone; Don Pedro, the colonel; Don Alverez, the intendant; Don Paul, the captain of mariners; and two friars had gathered to comfort him regarding the unsatisfactory state of his cathedral town, which had just been sacked and burnt by an old friend of theirs, Sir Francis Drake.

"We have been great sufferers, señores," the bishop snuffled, quoting Scripture with a glibness that bordered on irreverence, his texts often travestied by his grumbling at the mosquitoes. "Great sufferers, truly; but there shall be a remnant... Ah! Gold? Yes, I trust Our Lady's mercies are not shut up. Look, señores!" He pointed majestically out the window. "It looks gold! It smells of gold. Yea, the very waves sing of gold!"

"It is a great privilege," said the intendant, "to have comfort so gracefully administered by a churchman and a scholar."

"A poet, too," added Don Pedro. "You have no notion what sweet sonnets—"

"Hush, Don Pedro," the bishop interrupted. "If I, a mateless bird, have spent an idle hour teaching lovers how to sing, what of that? I am a churchman, señores; but I am a man and I can feel. I can sympathize, palliate, excuse. Who knows better than I how much human nature lurks in us, fallen sons of Adam?" He turned to the trembling Indian girl. "Tita!"

"Um?" she grunted.

"Fill his excellency the intendant's glass. Does much more treasure come down, illustrious señor? May the poor of Mary hope for a few more crumbs?"

"Not a pezo, I fear," the bishop replied, pointing to the snow-capped Horqueta. "The big white cow up there has been milked dry for this year."

"Ah!" he looked up at the magnificent peak. "Only good to cool wine with, eh? And as safe for the time being as Solomon's birds."

"Solomon's birds? Explain your recondite allusion, my lord," the intendant requested.

The bishop launched into a tale of hoopoes who once wore golden crowns. He recounted how, at the court of King Solomon, the birds begged for crowns of feathers instead, for mankind hunted and trapped them so ruthlessly for the sake of gold that life had become a burden. Solomon granted their wish, and ever since, they lived merrily as crickets in an oven, bearing the name of the King.

"Then, friend," replied the other friar, a Dominican of darker and sterner complexion, "corrupt human nature would within a week discover some fresh bauble, for which to kill and be killed in vain."

"I merely remarked," the first friar, Fray Gerundio, interjected, "that it were well for the world if all mankind were to put up the same prayer as the hoopoes."

"World, sir? What do you know about the world?" the bishop scoffed, turning to the Dons. "Convert your Indians, sir, if you please, and leave affairs of state to your superiors. You will excuse him, señores. A very worthy and pious man, but a poor peasant's son; and besides—a little wrong here; too much fasting and watching, I fear, good man." He touched his forehead knowingly, signaling that Fray Gerundio's wits were in an unsatisfactory state.

The friar heard and saw with a quiet smile. He was one of those excellent men whom the cruelties of his countrymen had stirred up to deeds of love and pious daring. True Protestants in their own way, they protested against the evil nearest them; true liberals, they called the dark-skinned heathen their brothers; true martyrs, they bore witness of Christ, often receiving slander and contempt in return. Such an one was Fray Gerundio, a clumsy-tongued peasant's son who could never put three sentences together save when he waxed eloquent, crucifix in hand, amid groups of Indians or negroes. He was accustomed to such rebuffs as the bishop's; he took them for what they were worth and sipped his wine in silence.

"They say," observed the commandant, "that a very small Plate-fleet will go to Spain this year."

"What else?" said the intendant. "What have we to send, in the name of all saints, since these accursed English Lutherans have swept us out clean?"

"And if we had anything to send," said the sea-captain, "what have we to send it in? That fiend incarnate, Drake—"

"Ah!" said his holiness. "Spare my ears! Don Pedro, you will oblige my weakness by not mentioning that man; his name is Tartarean, unfit for polite lips. Draco—a dragon—the emblem of Diabolus himself! And the guardian of the golden apples of the West, who would fain devour our new Hercules, his most Catholic majesty. Deceived Eve, too, with one of those same apples—a very evil name, señores—a Tartarean name—Tita!"

"Um!"

"Fill my glass."

"Nay," cried the colonel, with a great oath, "this English fellow is of another breed of serpent from that, I warrant."

"Your reason, señor; your reason?"

"Because this one would have seen Eve at the bottom of the sea, before he let her, or any one but himself, taste aught which looked like gold."

The conversation drifted to the devastation of Cartagena, the loss of the blockhouse, and the shame of being beaten behind their own barricades. The bishop lamented the villainous trick of the ransom, recalling how Drake had dined with him, offered to convert him, and then demanded fresh ransom for the priory and the fort.
