The evening of February 15, 1587, found Mrs. Leigh pacing the terrace-walk at Burrough House. For three weary years, she had performed this ritual every night, regardless of the weather, gazing out over the winding river, the hazy sandhills, and the vast, western sea. Three years had passed since Frank and Amyas sailed away with their gallant ship and its brave crew, vanishing into the infinite unknown much like John Oxenham before them. Yet, no news had arrived. The loving eyes of Bideford, Appledore, Clovelly, and Ilfracombe had grown hollow from watching and weeping for those lost to the West.

There had been a fleeting flush of hope when Sir Richard, unbeknownst to Mrs. Leigh, sent a horseman to Plymouth upon hearing that Drake, Frobisher, and Carlisle had returned from the Spanish Main. The rider brought glorious tidings of the sackings of Cartagena and San Domingo, and the relief of Raleigh's Virginian Colony, but no word of the *Rose* or its passengers. Mrs. Leigh had bowed her head, whispering, "The Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord!"

Her hair was now gray, her cheeks wan, and her step feeble. She rarely left home save for church or the neighboring cottages. She never spoke her sons' names, allowing not a single word to escape her lips that might betray her thoughts of them. Yet, daily, when the tide rose high and the red flag on the sandhills signaled water over the bar, she paced the terrace, devouring the sea with greedy eyes in search of a sail that never came. The stately ships entered and exited as of old; white sails hung off the bar for hours, days, months, and years. But an instinct within her told her that none were the vessel she sought. She knew that ship, every line and cut of cloth; she could have identified it from miles away among a whole fleet, but it never appeared. Mrs. Leigh bowed her head in worship, moving among the poor who regarded her as an awe-inspiring figure, one whom God had drawn near to Himself in that mysterious heaven of sorrow. Lone women and bedridden men looked into her steadfast eyes, finding strength there, for they knew—though she never spoke of her own grief—that she had descended into the fiercest depths of the fiery furnace and walked there unhurt by the side of One whose form was as of the Son of God. All the while, she blamed herself for her "earthly" longings, confessing nightly to Heaven the weakness that drew her feet to the terrace beneath the wind-clipped trees.

That evening, however, Northam was in a stir. The pebble ridge thundered far below, but Northam was noisy enough without the rolling surge. The tower rocked with pealing bells; people filled the streets shouting and singing around bonfires. They burned the Pope in effigy, drank to the Queen's health, and cried, "So perish all her enemies!" The hills glowed red with bonfires in every village. Far away, the bells of Bideford answered those of Northam as they had seven years prior when Amyas returned from circumnavigating the globe. Today, news had arrived that Mary, Queen of Scots, had been beheaded at Fotheringay. All England, like a dreamer shaking off a hideous nightmare, leapt up in a tremendous shout of jubilation, lifting the terror and danger of seventeen anxious years from its heart forever.

She was gone to answer at a higher tribunal for the noble English blood poured out for her, and the noble English hearts she had tempted into treachery, rebellion, and murder. Elizabeth's words had finally been fulfilled:

"The daughter of debate,
That discord aye doth sow,
Hath reap'd no gain where former rule
Hath taught still peace to grow."

Now she could do evil no more. Murder, adultery, the unforgiving heart, and the tongue that could not speak truth even for its own interest had passed, perhaps atoned for. Her fair face remained a pitiful dream in the memory of those who knew that either she or England must perish.

"Nothing is left of her
Now, but pure womanly."

Even Mrs. Leigh, a Protestant, breathed a prayer for mercy on that soul, "clear as diamond, and as hard," as she had once described herself. She pondered the final scene before the fatal block; it could not have been mere acting. Mrs. Leigh had learned many lessons in the last seven years; might not Mary Stuart have learned something in seventeen? She had been a courtier and knew, as far as a chaste Englishwoman could know, of the godless profligacy of the French court in which poor Mary had been trained among the Medicis, the Guises, and Cardinal Lorraine. She shuddered and sighed to herself: "To whom little is given, of them shall little be required!" Yet the bells continued to peal.

What answered them from the fast-darkening twilight? A flash, followed by the thunder of a gun at sea.

Mrs. Leigh stopped. The flash was right outside the bar. It could not be a ship in distress; the wind was light and westerly, and it was a high spring tide. What could it be? Another flash, another gun. The noisy folk of Northam hushed instantly and hurried into the churchyard overlooking the broad flats and the river.

There was a gallant ship outside the bar, running in with all sails set. It was a large vessel, nearly a thousand tons, but not of English rig. What was its meaning? A Spanish cruiser preparing reprisals for Drake's raid along the Cadiz shore? Unlikely. The Don favored scientific warfare with admirals, vice-admirals, and transports, not such dare-devil tactics.

Easily, on the flowing tide and fair western wind, she slipped up the channel between the two lines of sandhills. She was almost off Appledore. She was no enemy; if she were a foreigner, she was a daring one, for she had never veiled her topsails. Every foreign ship must veil its topsails within sight of an English port, or risk war, as the Spanish admiral discovered many years prior when he sailed into Plymouth Sound without veiling his topsails or lowering the flag of Spain. John Hawkins, the port admiral, had rushed out in his famous *Jesus of Lübeck*, sent a shot between the admiral's masts, and when that failed, ran alongside and fired a shot that pierced the admiral through and through. The offending flag came down, apologies were made, but not accepted for a long time by the stout guardian of her Majesty's honor. If John Hawkins acted thus for a Spanish fleet in peacetime, there were many old sea-dogs in Appledore who would do the same for a single ship in wartime if they could find even an iron pot to burn powder with.

The strange sail passed behind the hill of Appledore, and then a cheer rose from a hundred throats in the quiet evening air. Mrs. Leigh stood still, listening. Another gun thundered among the hills, followed by another cheer.

It took twenty minutes for the vessel to hove in sight again around the dark rocks of the Hubbastone as she turned up the Bideford river. Mrs. Leigh had stood perfectly motionless during that time, a pale, scarcely breathing statue, her eyes fixed upon the Viking's rock.

Round the Hubbastone she came at last. Music blared on board—drums, fifes, shawms, and trumpets—waking ringing echoes from every knoll of wood and slab of slate. As she opened fully onto Burrough House, another cheer burst from her crew, rolling up to the hills from the silver waters far below.

Mrs. Leigh walked quickly toward the house and called her maid, "Grace, bring me my hood. Master Amyas is come home!"

"No, surely? O joyful sound! Praised and blessed be the Lord! But, madam, how did you know?"

"I heard his voice on the river; but I did not hear Mr. Frank's with him, Grace!"

"Oh, be sure, madam, where the one is, the other is. They'd never part company. Both come home or neither, I'll warrant. Here's your hood, madam."

Mrs. Leigh, with Grace behind her, started with rapid steps toward Bideford. Was it true? Was it a dream? Had the divine instinct of the mother enabled her to recognize her child's voice among all the rest, or was her brain turning from the long effort of her supernatural calm?

Grace asked herself the same question many times between Burrough and Bideford. When they arrived on the quay, the question answered itself.

As they came down Bridgeland Street—which would later house tobacco warehouses for the Virginia trade, but then was merely a row of rope-walks and sailmakers' shops—they could see the strange ship already at anchor. Just reaching the lower end of the street, a great mob swept round the corner: sailors, women, apprentices, hurrahing, questioning, weeping, laughing. Mrs. Leigh stopped; the mob stopped also.

"Here she is!" shouted someone. "Here's his mother!"

"His mother? Not their mother!" Mrs. Leigh said to herself, turning very pale, though her heart was long past breaking.

The next moment, the giant head and shoulders of Amyas swept round the corner, far above the crowd.

"Make a way! Make room for Madam Leigh!" Amyas fell on his knees at her feet.

She threw her arms round his neck, bending her fair head over his, while sailors, apprentices, and coarse harbor-women were hushed into holy silence, forming a ring round the mother and son.

Mrs. Leigh asked no questions. She saw that Amyas was alone.

At last, he whispered, "I would have died to save him, mother, if I could."

"You need not tell me that, Amyas Leigh, my son."

Another silence.

"How did he die?" whispered Mrs. Leigh.

"He is a martyr. He died in the—"

Amyas could say no more.

"The Inquisition?"

"Yes."

A strong shudder passed through Mrs. Leigh's frame, then she lifted her head.

"Come home, Amyas. I little expected such an honor—such an honor—and such a fair young martyr, too; a very St. Stephen! God, have mercy on me; and let me not go mad before these folk when I ought to be thanking Thee for Thy great mercies! Amyas, who is that?"

She pointed to Ayacanora, standing close behind Amyas, watching keenly.

"She is a poor wild Indian girl—my daughter, I call her. I will tell you her story hereafter."

"Your daughter? My grand-daughter, then. Come hither, maiden, and be my grand-daughter."

Ayacanora came obediently and knelt, having seen Amyas kneel.

"God forbid, child! Kneel not to me. Come home, and let me know whether I am sane or mazed, alive or dead."

Drawing her hood over her face, she turned to go back, holding Amyas tight by one hand and Ayacanora by the other.

The crowd let them depart some twenty yards in respectful silence before bursting into a cheer that made the old town ring.

Mrs. Leigh stopped suddenly.

"I had forgotten, Amyas. You must not let me stand in the way of your duty. Where are your men?"

"Kissed to death by this time; all of them, that is, who are left."

"Left?"

"We went out a hundred, mother, and we came home forty-four—if we are at home. Is it a dream, mother? Is this you? And this old Bridgeland Street again? As I live, there stands Evans the smith, at his door, tankard in hand, as he did when I was a boy!"

The brawny smith crossed the street but stopped when he saw Amyas, yet no Frank.

"Better one than neither, madam!" he said, offering rough comfort. Amyas shook his hand as he passed, but Mrs. Leigh neither heard nor saw him nor anyone else.

"Mother," said Amyas as they passed the causeway, "we are rich for life."

"Yes; a martyr's death was the fittest for him."

"I have brought home treasure untold."

"What, my boy?"

"Treasure untold. Cary has promised to see to it tonight."

"Very well. I would that he had slept at our house. He was a kindly lad and loved Frank. When did he?"

"Three years ago, and more. Within two months of our sailing."

"Ah! Yes, he told me so."

"Told you so?"

"Yes; the dear lad has often come to see me in my sleep; but you never came. I guessed how it was—as it should be."

"But I loved you none the less, mother!"

"I know that, too: but you were busy with the men, you know, sweet; so your spirit could not come roving home like his, which was free. Yes—all as it should be. My maid, and do you not find it cold here in England, after those hot regions?"

"Ayacanora's heart is warm; she does not think about cold."

"Warm? Perhaps you will warm my heart for me, then."

"Would God I could do it, mother!" said Amyas, half reproachfully.

Mrs. Leigh looked up in his face and burst into a violent flood of tears.

"Sinful! Sinful that I am!"

"Blessed creature!" cried Amyas. "If you speak so, I shall go mad. Mother, mother, I have been dreading this meeting for months. It has been a nightmare hanging over me like a horrible black thunder-cloud; a great cliff miles high, with its top hid in the clouds, which I had to climb and dared not. I have longed to leap overboard and flee from it like a coward into the depths of the sea. The

"The daughter of debate,
That discord still doth sow,
Shall reap no gain where former rule
Hath taught still peace to grow.
No foreign banish'd wight
Shall anker in this port
Our realm it brooks no stranger's force;
Let them elsewhere resort."

Amyas had returned to the quietude of home, and for a year, the chronicles of his life remained largely unmarked by event. Yeo, the old sailor, had settled into the role of major domo, trailing Amyas like a grey wolfhound and spending his evenings at the hearth, his Bible resting on his knee while his hands crafted endless trifles for the household, particularly for Ayacanora. She received these offerings with a pout, chided by Mrs. Leigh, before accepting them to store away unseen. Her heart was set on becoming an English maid; she followed Mrs. Leigh through the kitchen and still-room, eager to learn the arts of sewing and housekeeping. Having grown accustomed to the savage's boundless hospitality, she now sought to understand the English concept of giving to those who could offer nothing in return. She watched Mrs. Leigh minister to the poor and saw Amyas distribute wealth to build almshouses for weary mariners, interpreting these acts as thank-offerings to God. Though her initial understanding of sacrifice leaned toward the bloody rituals of the Aztecs, Mrs. Leigh gently explained that giving to the poor was giving to the Great Spirit Himself, who dwelt within them all. Ayacanora absorbed this lesson, working tirelessly for the elderly women of Northam, her beauty radiant and beloved, though she often dressed in Spanish finery that dazzled the congregation. When the vicar protested the disturbance, she defiantly declared the garments belonged to the "Piache" (the conjuror) and insisted he wear them, eventually securing a magnificent mantle for the altar-cloth, a blaze of emerald and crimson that had once adorned an Aztec prince.

While Amyas watched her with a mixture of amusement and pride, Ayacanora's behavior toward the local ladies proved equally challenging. They came to congratulate Mrs. Leigh and glimpse the "fair savage," only to be snubbed by the girl, who refused to be a spectacle. Tears flowed when Mrs. Leigh questioned her conduct, for Ayacanora clung to her, declaring she would rather be a kitchen slave than return to the "bad people" of the forest. Yet, as months passed, the storms of her temper subsided into a rational, if unhappy, calm.

Clues to her origins began to surface. She recognized houses and red cattle, puzzled by the smallness of English sheep compared to the great camel-like llamas of Peru. Her greatest delight was a horse; after failing to steal one from Amyas, she successfully bartered her finery with the vicar for a pony, though the vicar's honesty thwarted the deal until Amyas purchased a jennet for her. One evening at Stow, after a day of riding, she awoke in a nightmare of the "Old ape of Panama," recounting a vision of being dragged into a courtyard, mounted on a horse, and transported by an Indian over mountains into a dark wood where she reverted to a little savage child. Mrs. Leigh accepted this as a divine revelation of her identity: a child exposed by a jealous stepfather, likely Don Xararte, somewhere in the Andes.

Further confirmation arrived through the story of an Indian named Raleigh, brought home by Sir Richard Grenville from Virginia. Baptized in Bideford as the first "Red man" on British soil, he was later listed in the parish registers as "Rawly, a Winganditoian" in 1589, though his entry noted he had died. The records suggested a tragic fate for the indigenous people, arriving at civilization too late to be saved, yet too late to prevent their decay.

While Amyas remained idle, England did not. The year 1588 approached, a climactic turning point foretold by astronomers. Tensions rose as Spain, emboldened by the Pope and Jesuit propaganda, prepared a massive armada to crush the "heretical" English and restore Catholic rule. The Jesuits preached the imminent doom of England, citing the sorrows of Mary, Queen of Scots, and the supposed wrongs of the Virgin Mary, urging a crusade against Elizabeth. Yet, the English people, driven by a fierce patriotism and a pragmatic refusal to sacrifice their ancestral lands, rejected the call for invasion. Instead, they resolved that Mary, Queen of Scots, must die, or the commonweal of England perish.

Drake wrote to Amyas, urging him to join the fleet to "singe the king of Spain's beard," promising hard blows and hard money. Amyas replied, bound by his mother's command to wait, but expressed his desire to hunt down Don Guzman, a Spanish commander rumored to be on one of the galleons. Though he obeyed his mother, his mood darkened with sullen malice against the Spaniard, a shadow that Salvation Yeo's fanaticism only deepened.

Finally, in January 1588, Sir Richard Grenville arrived in Bideford, bringing news of the Queen's fierce resolve. He revealed that the council had decided on a naval defense rather than land fortifications, arguing that England's true rampart was its fleet. Raleigh, now a key figure in the council, was to oversee the training of the militia. Amyas and Grenville spent the following months preparing seven ships in Bideford river, with Amyas serving as admiral. However, this fleet was destined not for Virginia, but for the defense of England itself.

On a long June evening in 1588, Mrs. Leigh sat by the window, stitching, while Ayacanora struggled with a book of heroes. Amyas paced the garden, his brow furrowed in calculation, ignoring the admiring glances of the children and Ayacanora. Mrs. Leigh spoke of heroism, pausing before a portrait of a lost loved one, and Ayacanora asked if she could ever be an English girl. Amyas entered, teasing her about sailing to the West Indies and seeing Indians again. His jests touched a nerve; Ayacanora burst into tears, hiding her face in Mrs. Leigh's lap. Amyas, realizing the depth of her pain, offered a gift, only for her to tear it apart, declaring she hated him and the memory of his past. Mrs. Leigh pleaded with her son to stop tormenting the child, revealing that Ayacanora loved him with a woman's devotion, a love born of gratitude and shared trauma. Amyas, blinded by prejudice and the taint of her Spanish blood, dismissed her feelings, fearing the inheritance of her family's cruelty. He confessed that he could not bear the thought of raising children with such bloodlines, recalling the look in her eyes as she stood over the captain of the galleon with a smoking knife.

Suddenly, a servant rushed in with a letter from Sir Richard. Amyas opened it and laughed aloud. "The Armada is coming!" he cried, his wish fulfilled. The letter confirmed the Spanish fleet had sailed from Lisbon. Amyas threw his arms around his mother, apologizing for his earlier despair. With renewed purpose, he dashed to Bideford, finding Sir Richard rallying the local gentry. The merchants and captains, inspired by Amyas's spirit, pledged their ships and cargo, replacing Virginian goods with powder and shot. Within a week, the seven ships were ready. On June 21st, the squadron sailed from Appledore, led by Amyas Leigh, to join the main fleet at the Sound. As Mrs. Leigh watched from the shore, Ayacanora stood beside her, tears streaming down her face. "And he never bade me good-bye," she sobbed. Mrs. Leigh comforted her, and in that shared sorrow, the savage faded, and the true woman rose anew, united in the sacred fire of grief and hope.

"Oh, where be these gay Spaniards,
Which make so great a boast O?
Oh, they shall eat the gray-goose feather,
And we shall eat the roast O!"

Cornish Song.

Imagine the spectators who, last summer, stood with swelling pride before the noble port of Plymouth. They beheld the vast breakwater spanning the Sound, the arsenals and docks, the twin estuaries teeming with gallant ships, and watched the great screw-liners turning within their own length by invisible force, threading crowded fleets with the ease of a toy boat. Now, imagine if, by some magical reversal, the nineteenth century—with all its magnificence of wealth and science—vanished as surely as it may vanish hereafter. Suppose they found themselves cast back three hundred years into the pleasant summer days of 1588.

Mount Edgecombe remains, beautiful as ever. But where are the docks? Where is Devonport? No vast dry-dock roofs rise at the water's edge. Drake's Island carries but a paltry battery, freshly raised by the man whose name it bears; Mount Wise is a lone gentleman's house amidst the fields; the citadel is a pop-gun fort that a third-class steamer would shell into rubble for an afternoon's amusement. And the shipping? The floating castles of the Hamoaze have dwindled to a few crawling lime-hoys. The Catwater is packed, not as it is now with merchant craft, but with the ships that will, tomorrow, begin the greatest sea-fight the world has ever seen.

To modern eyes, they are a paltry squadron. The largest vessel is not equal in size to a thirty-six-gun frigate, carries less weight of metal than one of our new gun-boats, and can employ that weight at no more than a quarter of our modern range. Would our modern spectators, arriving by rail for a few hours to witness the cavalry embark and returning in time for dinner, look down upon that petty port and fleet with a contemptuous smile? Would they begin some flippant speech about the progress of intellect and the triumphs of science, regarding our benighted ancestors as a raree-show to feed their silly wonder? Doubtless, they would, if they belonged to the many who gaze on such triumphs without thankfulness or understanding, like an ox eating clover thrust into his rack without knowing how it grew.

But if any of them were of the class by whom those very triumphs have been achieved—the thinkers and workers who labor themselves, knowing by hard experience the struggles, self-restraints, and disappointments by which the discoverer reaches his prize—then their smile would not be one of pity, but of filial love. For they would see in those outwardly paltry armaments the potential germ of the mightier power that now loads the Black Sea waves. They would be aware that to produce it, with the materials and knowledge of that time, demanded an intellect, an energy, and a spirit of progress equal, if not superior, to those of which we now so loudly boast.

Yet, if he had been a student of men rather than of machinery, he would have found few nobler companies to exercise his discernment than those gathered on the little terrace bowling-green behind the Pelican Inn on the afternoon of July nineteenth. Chatting in groups or lounging over the low wall commanding a view of the Sound and the shipping far below were almost every notable man of the Plymouth fleet, the entire *posse comitatus* of "England's forgotten worthies." The Armada had been scattered by a storm; Lord Howard had sought it as far as the Spanish coast, but the wind had shifted south, and fearing the Dons might pass him, he had returned to Plymouth, uncertain if the Armada would come at all. Slip on for a while the drawer's apron, enter through the rose-clad door with a tray of long-necked Dutch glasses and a silver tankard of wine, and look around at the gallant captains waiting for the Spanish Armada, like lions in their lair waiting for the passing herd of deer.

See those five talking earnestly in the center of a ring that longs to overhear yet is too respectful to approach. Those soft, long eyes and pointed chin are Walter Raleigh's. The fair young man in the flame-colored doublet, whose arm is round Raleigh's neck, is Lord Sheffield. Opposite them stands, beside Sir Richard Grenville, a man as stately even as he, Lord Sheffield's uncle, Lord Charles Howard of Effingham, Lord High Admiral of England; next to him is his son-in-law, Sir Robert Southwell, captain of the *Elizabeth Jonas*. But who is that short, sturdy, plainly dressed man standing with legs apart and hands behind his back, looking up with keen gray eyes into the face of each speaker? His cap is in his hands, revealing a bullet-shaped head of crisp brown hair and a wrinkled forehead, high cheekbones, a short square face, broad temples, and thick lips firm as granite. A coarse plebeian stamp of man, yet the whole figure and attitude radiate boundless determination, self-possession, and energy. When he finally speaks a few blunt words, all eyes turn respectfully upon him—for his name is Francis Drake.

A burly, grizzled elder in greasy, sea-stained garments, contrasting oddly with the huge gold chain about his neck, waddles up as if born and living ever since in a gale at sea. The upper half of his sharp, dogged visage seems of brick-red leather, the lower of badger's fur. As he claps Drake on the back and, with a broad Devon twang, shouts, "Be you a coming to drink your wine, Francis Drake, or be you not?—saving your presence, my lord," the Lord High Admiral only laughs and bids Drake go and drink his wine. For John Hawkins, Admiral of the Port, is the patriarch of Plymouth seamen; if Drake is their hero, Hawkins is their grandfather, and he says and does pretty much what he likes in any company on earth. Not to mention that today's prospect of an Armageddon fight has shaken him altogether out of his usual crabbed reserve, making him overflow with loquacious good-humor, even toward his rival Drake.

So they push through the crowd, wherein is many another man whom one would gladly have spoken with face to face on earth. Martin Frobisher and John Davis sit on that bench smoking tobacco from long silver pipes; by them are Fenton and Withrington, who have both tried to follow Drake's path round the world and failed, though by no fault of their own. The man who pledges them better luck next time is George Fenner, known to "the seven Portugals," Leicester's pet, and captain of the galleon which Elizabeth bought of him. That short, prim man in the huge yellow ruff, with sharp chin, minute imperial, and self-satisfied smile, is Richard Hawkins, the Complete Seaman, Admiral John's hereafter famous and hapless son. The elder talking with him is his good uncle William, whose monument still stands—or should stand—in Deptford Church; for Admiral John set it up there but one year after this time, recording how he was "a worshipper of the true religion, an especial benefactor of poor sailors, a most just arbiter in most difficult causes, and of a singular faith, piety, and prudence." That, and the fact that he got creditably through some sharp work at Porto Rico, is all I know of William Hawkins: but if you or I, reader, can have as much or half as much said of us when we have to follow him, we shall have no reason to complain.

There is John Drake, Sir Francis' brother, ancestor of the present stock of Drakes; and there is George, his nephew, a man not overwise who has been round the world with Amyas. And there is Amyas himself, talking to one who answers him with fierce, curt sentences, Captain Barker of Bristol, brother of the hapless Andrew Barker who found John Oxenham's guns and, owing to a mutiny among his men, perished by the Spaniards in Honduras twelve years ago. Barker is now captain of the *Victory*, one of the Queen's best ships, and he has his accounts to settle with the Dons, as Amyas has; so they are both growling together in a corner while all the rest are as merry as flies upon the vine above their heads.

But who is the aged man sitting upon a bench against the sunny south wall of the tavern, his long white beard flowing almost to his waist, his hands upon his knees, his palsied head moving slowly from side to side to catch the scraps of discourse of the passing captains? His great-grandchild, a little maid of six, has laid her curly head upon his knees, and his granddaughter, a buxom black-eyed dame of thirty, stands by him, tending him half as nurse and half as showman, for he seems an object of curiosity to all the captains. His fair nurse has to entreat again and again, "Bless you, sir, please now, don't give him no liquor, poor old soul, the doctor says." It is old Martin Cockrem, father of the ancient host, aged himself beyond the years of man, who can recollect the bells of Plymouth ringing for the coronation of Henry the Eighth, and who was perhaps the first Englishman ever to set foot on the soil of the New World. There he sits, like an old Druid Tor of primeval granite amid the tall wheat and rich clover crops of a modern farm. He has seen the death of old Europe and the birth-throes of the new.

Go to him and question him, for his senses are quick as ever. Just now, the old man seems uneasy. He peers with rheumy eyes through the groups, listening for a well-known voice.

"There 'a be again! Why don't 'a come, then?"

"Quiet, gramfer, and don't trouble his worship."

"Here an hour, and never speak to poor old Martin! I say, sir"—and the old man feebly plucks Amyas's cloak as he passes—"I say, captain, do 'e tell young master old Martin's looking for him."

"Marcy, gramfer, where's your manners? Don't be vexed, sir, he'm a'most a babe, and tejous at times, mortal."

"Young master who?" says Amyas, bending down to the old man and smiling to the dame to let him have his way.

"Master Hawkins; he'm never been a-near me all day."

Off goes Amyas; of course, he lays hold of the sleeve of young Richard Hawkins. But as he is in act to speak, the dame lays hold of his, laughing and blushing.

"No, sir, not Mr. Richard, sir; Admiral John, sir, his father; he always calls him young master, poor old soul!" and she points to the grizzled beard and the face scarred and tanned with fifty years of fight and storm.

Amyas goes to the Admiral and gives his message.

"Mercy on me! Where be my wits? Iss, I'm a-coming," says the old hero in his broadest Devon, waddling off to the old man and beginning to lug at a pocket. "Here, Martin, I've got mun, I've got mun, man alive; but his Lordship keept me so. Lookee here, then! Why, I do get so lusty of late, Martin, I can't get to my pockets!"

And out struggle a piece of tarred string, a bundle of papers, a thimble, a piece of pudding-tobacco, and last of all, a little paper of Muscovado sugar—then as great a delicacy as any French bonbons would be now—which he thrusts into the old man's eager and trembling hand.

Old Martin begins dipping his finger into it, rubbing it on his toothless gums, smiling and nodding thanks to his young master; while the little maid at his knee, unrebuked, takes her share also.

"There, Admiral Leigh; both ends meet—gramfers and babies! You and I shall be like to that one day, young Samson!"

"We shall have slain a good many Philistines first, I hope."

"Amen! so be it; but look to mun! so fine a sailor as ever drank liquor; and now greedy after a hit of sweet trade! 'Tis piteous like; but I bring mun a hit whenever I come, and he looks for it. He's one of my own flesh like, is old Martin. He sailed with my father Captain Will, when they was both two little cracks aboard of a trawler; and my father went up, and here I am—he didn't, and there he is. We'm up now, we Hawkinses. We may be down again some day."

"Never, I trust," said Amyas.

"'Tain't no use trusting, young man: you go and do. I do hear too much of that there from my lad. Let they ministers preach till they'm black in the face, works is the trade!" with a nudge in Amyas's ribs. "Faith can't save, nor charity nether. There, you tell with him, while I go play bowls with Drake. He'll tell you a sight of stories. You ask him about good King Hal, now, just—"

And off waddled the Port Admiral.

"You have seen good King Henry, then, father?" said Amyas, interested.

The old man's eyes lighted at once, and he stopped mumbling his sugar.

"Seed mun? Iss, I reckon. I was with Captain Will when he went to meet the Frenchman there to Calais—at the Field, the Field—"

"The Field of the Cloth of Gold, gramfer," suggested the dame.

"That's it. Seed mun? Iss, fegs. Oh, he was a king! The face o' mun like a rising sun, and the back o' mun so broad as that there" (and he held out his palsied arms), "and the voice of mun! Oh, to hear mun swear if he was merry, oh, 'tas royal!--Seed mun? Iss, feg

"Britannia needs no bulwarks,
No towers along the steep,
Her march is o'er the mountain wave,
Her home is on the deep."

Thus sang Campbell of the mariners of England, and now the prophecy was being tested in a drama that would decide the spiritual fate of Europe and the destiny of the New World. For twelve days, the seas churned with a conflict worthy of Homer's thunder, pitting the Protestant cause of liberty against the Catholic yoke of despotism. While the narrative of this epic belongs to the chroniclers of the age, the events themselves unfolded with a ferocity that demanded witness.

The engagement began with a declaration of war. The English Lord High Admiral, commanding the *Defiance*, signaled his intent by firing a warning shot, followed immediately by the *Arkroyall*—also known as the *Triumph*—which closed to musket range and unleashed a storm of ordnance upon what the English admiral believed to be the Spanish flagship. In truth, it was the vessel of Alfonso de Leon. Soon, Sir Francis Drake, Sir John Hawkins, and Sir Martin Frobisher turned their guns upon the rear squadron under Recalde. The Spaniards quickly realized the terrifying nimbleness of their English opponents. Recalde's ships, realizing they were losing the exchange of fire, broke formation to join the main fleet. Admiral Medina, seeing his line scatter, rallied his ships into a defensive crescent. The great Armada pressed forward with the slow, stately dignity of a herd of buffalo ignoring wolves, unaware that these were not beasts of prey but cunning hunters, swift and deadly. Within two hours, the myth of Spanish invincibility was already cracking under the weight of English steel.

One of the four great galliasses was already riddled with shot, her chapels and pulpits ruined, the friars within thrown into chaos. The fleet was forced to circle around her to prevent Drake and Hawkins from sinking her, a maneuver that allowed the "principal galleon of Seville," carrying the blue-blooded Don Pedro de Valdez, to collide with her neighbor. Stripped of her foremast and abandoned by her crew, the proud vessel was left to drift, a testament to the fragility of Spanish chivalry. Yet, the English pressed on, driven by a courage that dismissed such losses as mere trifles compared to the glory awaiting them.

The first day ended with a resounding victory for England. Not a single English ship was seriously damaged, nor was a man lost. The prestige of Spain was shattered in the minds of the English, vindicating the strategy of Lord Howard, who, advised by Raleigh and Drake, had chosen a running fight over a static blockade. Among the prizes was the *Sta. Catharina*, a massive galleon bearing the arms of Don Guzman, the commandant of soldiers. Amyas Leigh, having been called off by the admiral's signal, retired to his hammock, dreaming of revenge.

But sleep was short-lived. Before an hour had passed, Cary roused him. Drake's lantern, the signal for pursuit, was nowhere to be seen, replaced only by the glow of a great fire on a burning Spanish ship. The fleet lay-to for the night, drifting in the dark.

The next morning found them off Torbay. A pinnace brought a letter from Drake, written with the characteristic brevity and wit of the admiral. He recounted how he had let go several captured merchantmen to lure the Spaniards, only to fall upon Valdez's galleon, capturing a host of noblemen. Drake noted that Valdez, initially proud to his last breath, had eventually surrendered, kissing Drake's fist and calling himself fortunate. Drake revealed that Don Guzman was indeed aboard the *Sta. Catharina*, a lofty ship of three tiers of ordnance, sailing in Recalde's squadron. The Armada, Drake wrote, was attempting to smoke them out of Plymouth but had failed; their rigid orders bound them like bulls in a tether, unable to match the English fleet's agility. "Time and light will kill any hare," Drake concluded, confident that the chase would lead the Spaniards far from safety.

Amyas, reading the letter, stamped in frustration. "St. Catherine! Why, that was the galleon we hammered all yesterday!" he exclaimed.

"Cunning old Drake," Cary remarked, "lining his own pockets while keeping the fleet waiting."

"He has given the Lord High Admiral the due," Amyas retorted, though he secretly worshipped Drake and despised the Papists.

The fleet reunited with Lord Howard and Lord Sheffield at nightfall. The loyalty of English Catholics was proven by the Howards' desperate courage, which led only to victory. As they passed a drifting, deserted Spanish galleon, Amyas refused to board her, insisting they had come to fight, not to plunder. The men pulled long faces as their neighbors towed off the *San Miguel d'Oquenda*, the vice-admiral's ship, which had been nearly blown up by a disgruntled Dutch gunner seeking revenge.

The second day saw the wind shift to the north, blowing cool off the white-walled downs of Weymouth Bay. The Spaniards turned to face the English, intending to wait for a change in the wind before attempting to pass the Needles. The English, however, executed a series of maneuvers, slipping close-hauled between the Armada and the land. By noon, they were coming down upon the Spaniards right before the wind, initiating a fierce and confused battle.

The fighting was furious. The English, being fewer and smaller, charged with marvelous agility, firing broadsides and then darting away to reload, targeting the great, unwieldy Spanish ships. Cock, an Englishman of Devon, died with honor in a small ship amidst the enemy. Lord Howard, seeing his captain Fenner in danger, rushed to his aid, shouting, "O George, what doest thou? Wilt thou now frustrate my hope?" Enflamed by this display of loyalty, Howard fought like a valiant captain, as did all his men.

Night fell upon a sea that looked like a floating volcano. The next morning, the fleets had passed Purbeck and Freshwater, pouring out past the Needles to join the chase. From all English havens, the chivalry flocked to the battle: the Lords Oxford, Northumberland, Cumberland, Pallavicin, Brooke, Carew, Raleigh, and Blunt. Spain had staked her noble houses; England's gentlemen measured their strength against the Cavaliers of Spain. Lord Howard had sent light craft to Portsmouth for ammunition, but the wind died, and the two fleets drifted helpless, shouting idle defiance at each other.

The sun set on a glassy sea and rose again on the twenty-fifth, St. James's Day, the patron saint of Spain. Would the saint intervene? The galleys attacked, thrashing the sea with three hundred oars apiece, but it was Lord Howard's *Triumph*, his brother's *Lion*, and the *Elizabeth Jonas*, *Bear*, *Victory*, and *Leicester* that met them with salvos of chain-shot, smashing oars and cutting rigging. Without the wind, the galliasses would have shared the fate of Valdez. Frobisher beat down the Spanish admiral's mainmast, and when attacked by Mexia and Recalde, he was rescued by Lord Howard. After that day, no galliass dared fight again.

The battle raged beneath the cliffs of Freshwater, where sea-fowl rose screaming from the chalk walls, and shepherds hurried down the slopes to gaze in trembling awe at the masts and flags piercing the veil of smoke. St. James's Day passed as Baal's did on Carmel; the only fire answering his votaries was that of English cannon. The Armada, gathering into a roundel, ceased fighting and made for Calais, hoping for French assistance from the Guises and a junction with Parma's forces in Dunkirk.

Before a fair Etesian gale, the fleets glided past Brighton, Beachy Head, Hastings, and Dungeness. Instead of fighting, Lord Howard rewarded his captains with knighthoods. On the cliffs, pike-men and musketeers marched eastward, joined by women, children, and aged folk, raising a cry of mingled faith and fear. The danger was not over. News from Newhaven indicated the Guises would not stir, and Seymour and Winter had reinforced the fleet to one hundred and forty sail—slightly larger than the Spanish force but with less tonnage and fewer men. The Spaniards were battered but unbroken.

As the Armada anchored in Calais Roads on Saturday evening, England's hour had come. The bells that would ring on Sunday morning would be either the death-knell or the triumphal peal of the Reformed faith.

That Sabbath was solemn in country and town. Cowards who had scoffed at the Armada's approach now succumbed to abject fear, while brave men knelt beside their families, hearts sinking at the thought of the Inquisition or the fanaticism of the Spanish soldiery. Memories of the Massacre of St. Bartholomew, the fires of Smithfield, and the extermination of the Moors haunted the English imagination. They knew the hazard: one false step, and the doom of Magdeburg would fall upon every town from London to Carlisle. None knew this better than the guiding spirit of the land, who had devised a daring stroke to anticipate the blow.

But where was Amyas Leigh? For days, he had searched for the *Sta. Catharina* in the thick of the press, unable to find her or hear of her. One moment he feared she had sunk; the next, that she had escaped. Moody, restless, and peevish, he talked of nothing but Don Guzman. He spent his spare moments polishing his sword, muttering to it, treating it as a confidant. This selfish obsession with vengeance consumed his mind; he forgot England's triumph, his own safety, and everything but his brother's blood. Yet, this was the day he had longed for since boyhood, the day to face the invader, Antichrist himself. He had believed he was sent into the world only to fight the Spaniard, and now he was fighting for such a stake, yet he was not content. While the fleet received Communion side by side, rejoicing in Britain's Salamis, Amyas turned away from the holy elements.

"I cannot communicate, Sir John," he told Captain Brimblecombe. "Charity with all men? I hate, if ever man hated on earth."

"You hate the Lord's foes only, Captain Leigh."

"No, Jack, I hate my own as well."

"Don't try to put me off with the same Jesuit's quibble..." Jack protested, but Amyas cut him off, declaring his hatred for a specific enemy that kept him from loving any human being. Jack departed with a sigh.

While the crew communed on deck, Amyas sat below sharpening his sword, then took a boat to Drake's ship to ask for news of the *Sta. Catharina*. He listened scowling to the Spanish chants and bells. Finally, Drake returned with a secret commission, and Amyas helped him until nightfall, returning to his own ship as Sir Amyas Leigh, Knight, to the joy of all but his own moody soul.

On the Sabbath, the two armaments lay scowling at each other, hardly out of gunshot. Messengers hurried to Bruges for Parma, who was not at Dunkirk but at St. Mary of Halle in Hainault, praying for visions of Cardinal Allen crowning him. Parma reported that his victuals were not ready, his Dutch sailors had fled, and his ships were heavily armed and stubborn. While he prayed, the English prayed and acted.

By Monday morning, Lord Henry Seymour had delivered a letter of command from Elizabeth. Eight fire-ships, besmeared with wild-fire, brimstone, pitch, and resin, stole down the wind toward the Spanish fleet, guided by two valiant Devon men, Young and Prowse. The ships were fired, and the sky turned red from Dover Cliffs to Gravelines Tower. Weary Belgian peasants inland leaped from their beds, believing the Day of Judgment had come.

A panic ensued. Shrieks, oaths, and prayers made the night hideous. Remembering Jenebelli's fire-ships at Antwerp, the Spaniards cut their cables and hoisted any sails they could. The Invincible Armada lumbered wildly out to sea, every ship foul of her neighbor. The largest galliass lost her rudder and drifted helplessly. The Duke, having weighed his anchor deliberately, ran in to fire a signal for return, but his truant sheep were deaf to his pipe. Swearing and praying, he ran up the Channel toward Gravelines, picking up stragglers, but Drake and Fenner had arrived first.

When Monday's sun rose on Gravelines, the thunder of cannon recommenced and was not hushed till night. Drake hung coolly in the rear to plunder but fought fiercely when needed. The Armada must never be allowed to reform; if it did, its left wing might hold the English at bay while its right drove off the blockading Hollanders, freeing Parma to sail to the Thames.

Drake weighed anchor and moved up the Channel as soon as the Spanish fleet appeared, with Fenner burning to redeem his honor. Before the others could join, the Devon ships had worried the Spaniards into confusion for two hours. But what was that heavy firing behind them? The great galliass lay stranded on the sands where the Calais pier now stands. Amyas Preston, the future hero of La Guayra, pounded her into submission while a fleet of hoys and drumblers looked on like jackals.

Then, on the southwest horizon, two mighty ships loomed larger, followed by sail upon sail. A shout greeted the *Triumph* and the *Bear* as the Lord High Admiral glided stately into the thickest of the fight. True, they had only twenty-three ships to cope with ninety Spaniards, but they had dash, daring, and the inspiration of utter need. Now, or never, the struggle must end. Ship after ship rushed in to smash the broadsides of the wooden castles, sometimes not a pike's length asunder, then out again to reload

"Full fathom deep thy father lies;
Of his bones are corals made;
Those are pearls which were his eyes;
Nothing of him that doth fade,
But doth suffer a sea-change
Into something rich and strange;
Fairies hourly ring his knell,
Hark! I hear them. Ding dong bell."

The Tempest.

It is finished. The great Armada lies broken and defeated. The eternal war, that struggle between light and darkness, between Michael and his angels against Satan and his fiends, has momentarily paused. For centuries, this battle raged in the spiritual realm, invisible to men, but today it has become flesh and blood. Simple men have chosen their sides without error, aiding God's cause not merely with prayer, but with sharp shot and cold steel. Judgment has fallen, dividing the sheep from the goats, and proving the devil's work to be nothing but a lie—a windy boast that collapses at the slightest pinprick. Empires rise and fall like bubbles; the Spanish Armada, that great bullying phantom, vanished in a cloud of sulphur and fear, leaving the children of Israel to sing the song of Moses and the Lamb.

From England, the Netherlands, Germany, and Geneva, even from the persecuted Vaudois saints whose bones lie scattered on the cold Alpine mountains, a shout of holy joy rose to the heavens. It was a prophetic birth-paean for the new world, for free commerce, and for the triumph of liberty. As Van Meteran recorded, the Queen rode into London in triumph, her chariot drawn through the streets lined with blue cloth and banners, while the citizens acclaimed her long life and the confusion of her foes. "It came, it saw, and it fled!" the medals proclaimed. Away to the north, like frightened deer, the Spanish fleet fled past the Orkneys and Shetlands, denied water and food by the brave descendants of the Vikings, until they were scattered by the gales of the Atlantic, few reaching the shores of Spain.

And where were Amyas and the *Vengeance* during this time?

At the fifty-seventh degree of latitude, the English fleet, finding themselves short of provisions and powder, turned southward toward home, leaving the Spaniards to the northern seas. A few pinnaces were sent to watch, but the main fleet, caught in the same storms that scattered the enemy, reached Harwich safely. Meanwhile, Parma, unable to believe the idol at Halle had played such a scurvy trick, watched in vain from the Dunkirk dunes. The Armada was gone, and Amyas was following in its wake.

When the Lord High Admiral decided to return, Amyas asked leave to pursue the Spaniard. He secured ammunition and provisions from Sir John Hawkins, lodging bills in the admiral's pocket among thimbles and tobacco, promising repayment from plunder or estate. Calling his men together, he reminded them of the Rose of Torridge and Don Guzman de Soto, then asked: "Men of Bideford, will you follow me? There will be plunder for those who love it, revenge for those who crave it, and for all of us, the honor of never leaving the chase while a Spanish flag flies in English seas." Every soul aboard replied they would follow Sir Amyas Leigh around the world.

There is no need to detail every incident of that long, weary chase—the attack on the *Sta. Catharina*, the desperate maneuvers to prevent her escape, the crippling of the great Venetian galleys, the mutinous whispers of the younger hands, the strange encounter with the Hebridean Scots of Skye who fought and then embraced them, and the grim decision to let the crews of two great Spanish wrecks on the Mull of Cantire be smuggled off to Spain to face execution elsewhere. They passed the strange island of Raghary, the "drowned magpie," and the *Sta. Catharina* herself, which nearly perished on the Isle of Man and later ran aground on Sarn David before being freed by the tide. These moving incidents I must pass over to reach the end, for the time has come.

It was the sixteenth day of the chase. The evening before, they had seen St. David's Head and the Welsh coast looming black against a thunderstorm. Through the night, lightning flashed fitfully, but the tall masts of the Spaniard remained hidden. Certain that the wind would not allow her to go far westward, and fearing she might be lying-to ahead of them, the English waited. Dawn broke on a sunless, drizzly day, roofed with low, dingy clouds, the sea a long glassy roll under an inky sky.

Amyas paced the sloppy deck, fretful and fierce. He knew the Spaniard could not escape, yet he cursed every moment that lingered between him and the revenge that blackened his soul. The men sat sulkily, whistling for a wind that would not come.

"Take care of those guns. You will have something loose next," growled Amyas.
"We will take care of the guns, if the Lord will take care of the wind," said Yeo.
"We shall have plenty before night," said Cary. "And thunder too."
"So much the better," said Amyas. "It may roar till it splits the heavens, if it does but let me get my work done."

"He's not far off, I warrant," said Cary. "One lift of the cloud, and we should see him."
"To windward of us, as likely as not," said Amyas. "The devil fights for him, I believe. To have been on his heels sixteen days and not sent this through him yet!" He shook his sword impatiently.

The morning wore on without a sign of life, not even a passing gull. The black melancholy of the heaven reflected itself in the black melancholy of Amyas. Was he to lose his prey? The thought made him shudder with rage.
"No, God!" he cried. "Let me but once feel this in his accursed heart, and then—strike me dead, if Thou wilt!"
"The Lord have mercy on us," cried John Brimblecombe. "What have you said?"
"What is that to you, sir? There, they are piping to dinner. Go down. I shall not come."

Jack went down, whispering in terror of Amyas's ominous words. Only old Yeo seemed unafraid.
"Well, Sir John," he said, "and why not? What better can the Lord do for a man than take him home when he has done his work? Our captain is wilful and spiteful, and must needs kill his man himself. I owe him no grudge. May the Lord give him repentance... but if I could but see him once safe ashore, I would say, 'Lord, now lettest Thou Thy servant depart in peace,' even if it were the lightning which was sent to fetch me."

Suddenly, Amyas's voice thundered from the deck. "Here she is!"
In an instant, all scrambled up the hatchway. Yes, there she was. The cloud had lifted, revealing a ragged bore of blue sky shining on her tall masts as she lay rolling some four or five miles to the eastward. Land was not visible.
"There she is; and here we are," said Cary. "But where is here? and where is there? How is the tide, master?"
"Running up Channel by this time, sir."
"What matters the tide?" said Amyas, devouring the ship with terrible, cold blue eyes. "Can't we get at her?"
"Not unless some one jumps out and shoves behind," said Cary. "I shall down again and finish that mackerel..."
"Don't jest, Will! I can't stand it," said Amyas, his voice quivering. His whole frame trembled like an aspen. Cary took his arm and drew him aside.
"Dear old lad," he said, "what is this? You are not yourself, and have not been these four days."
"No. I am not Amyas Leigh. I am my brother's avenger. Do not reason with me, Will: when it is over I shall be merry old Amyas again." He passed his hand over his brow. "Do you believe," he asked after a moment, "that men can be possessed by devils?"
"The Bible says so."
"If my cause were not a just one, I should fancy I had a devil in me. My throat and heart are as hot as the pit. Would to God it were done, for done it must be! Now go."

Cary went away with a shudder. As he passed down the hatchway, he looked back. Amyas had taken out his hone and was whetting his sword-edge as if there were some dreadful doom upon him, to whet and whet forever.

The weary day wore on. The strip of blue sky was curtained over again, and all was dismal, though sultrier every moment. Nothing could be done to lessen the distance; the *Vengeance* had lost all her boats but one, which was too small to tow her. While the men finished dinner, Amyas worked on his sword, looking up suddenly at the Spaniard as if to satisfy himself that it was not a vision.

About two o'clock, Yeo came up.
"He is ours safely now, sir. The tide has been running to the eastward for this two hours."
"Safe as a fox in a trap. Satan himself cannot take him from us!"
"But God may," said Brimblecombe, simply.
"Who spoke to you, sir? If I thought that He—"
"There comes the thunder at last!"

An angry growl from the westward heavens answered his wild words, rolling and loudening nearer and nearer, till right over their heads it crashed against some cloud-cliff far above, and all was still. Each man looked in the other's face, but Amyas was unmoved.
"The storm is coming," he said. "The wind in it. It will be Eastward-ho now, for once, my merry men all!"
"Eastward-ho never brought us luck," said Jack in an undertone to Cary. But by this time all eyes were turned to the north-west, where a black line along the horizon began to define the boundary of sea and air.
"There comes the breeze."
"And there the storm, too."

With a strangely accelerating pace, the thunder rang peal on peal along the cloudy floor.
"Here comes the breeze. Round with the yards, or we shall be taken aback."
The yards creaked round; the sea grew crisp; the hot air swept their cheeks, tightening every rope, filling every sail, bending the ship over. A cheer burst from the men as the helm went up, and they staggered away before the wind, right down upon the Spaniard, who lay still becalmed.
"There is more behind, Amyas," said Cary. "Shall we not shorten sail a little?"
"No. Hold on every stitch," said Amyas. "Give me the helm, man. Boatswain, pipe away to clear for fight."

It was done. In ten minutes, the men were at quarters while the thunder rolled louder and the breeze freshened.
"The dog has it now. There he goes!" said Cary.
"Right before the wind. He has no liking to face us."
"He is running into the jaws of destruction," said Yeo. "An hour more will send him either right up the Channel, or smack on shore somewhere."

"There! he has put his helm down. I wonder if he sees land?"
"He is like a March hare beat out of his country," said Cary, "and doesn't know whither to run next."

Cary was right. In ten minutes more, the Spaniard fell off again, going dead down wind while the *Vengeance* gained on him fast. After two hours, the four miles had diminished to one, while lightning flashed nearer as the storm came up. From the vast mouth of a black cloud-arch poured a fierce breeze, and Amyas yielded unwillingly to hints growing into open murmurs, bidding them shorten sail.

On they rushed with scarcely lessened speed, the black arch following fast, curtained by the flat gray sheet of pouring rain before which the water boiled in a long white line. Every moment behind the watery veil, a keen blue spark leapt down into the sea or darted zigzag through the rain.
"We shall have it now, and with a vengeance; this will try your tackle, master," said Cary.
The functionary answered with a shrug, turning up the collar of his rough frock as the first drops flew stinging round his ears. Another minute, and the squall burst full upon them in rain that cut like hail, lashing the sea into froth and whirling off the heads of the surges into one white seething waste. Above, behind, and before them, lightning leapt and ran, dazzling and blinding, while the deep roar of thunder changed to sharp, ear-piercing cracks.

"Get the arms and ammunition under cover, and then below with you all," shouted Amyas from the helm.
"And heat the pokers in the galley fire," said Yeo, "to be ready if the rain puts our linstocks out. I hope you'll let me stay on deck, sir, in case—"
"I must have some one, and who better than you? Can you see the chase?"
No; she was wrapped in the gray whirlwind. She might be within half a mile of them, for aught they could see.

Now Amyas and his old liegeman were alone. Neither spoke; each knew the other's thoughts. The squall blew fiercer, the rain poured heavier. Where was the Spaniard?
"If he has laid-to, we may overshoot him, sir!"
"If he has tried to lay-to, he will not have a sail left in the bolt-ropes, or perhaps a mast on deck. I know the stiff-neckedness of those Spanish tubs. Hurrah! there he is, right on our larboard bow!"

There she was indeed, two musket-shots off, staggering away with canvas split and flying.
"He has been trying

The morning of October first broke with a deceptive stillness. The sky was a patchwork of soft gray clouds drifting from east to west, as if the heavens themselves were resting after the feverish summer of war and storm. The *Vengeance* slid silently over the bar, her anchor dropping off Appledore with her flag hanging heavy at half-mast. On board lay the cold weight of Salvation Yeo.

A small boat pulled away from the ship, heading for the western end of the strand. Cary and Brimblecombe helped the newly blinded Amyas Leigh to his feet, guiding him slowly up the hill toward his home. Behind them, a crowd had already formed—a sea of faces filled with cheers, blessings, and the raw, weeping pity of women who knew exactly what had happened to their hero.

"Spare me, my good friends," Amyas murmured, his voice steady despite the darkness that now filled his world. "I have landed here only to go quietly home, without becoming a spectacle in the town. Think not of me, good folks, nor speak of me. Come behind me decently, as Christian men, and follow to the grave the body of a better man than I."

As he spoke, a second boat approached, carrying the coffin of Salvation Yeo, draped in the red cross of England. The people obeyed his wish. A runner was sent ahead to Burrough to summon Mrs. Leigh. When the coffin was lifted from the boat, Amyas and his friends took their places behind it as chief mourners, the ship's crew following in orderly ranks. The crowd swelled behind them, growing with every step until, before they reached Northam town, the funeral procession numbered nearly five hundred souls.

The sexton had been summoned the previous day to dig the grave. By the time they entered the churchyard, the parson waited at the gate. Mrs. Leigh remained inside her home, unable to face the throng. Though her heart ached for her son, she found a strange comfort in knowing he honored his ancient master. She sat in the bay-window, Ayacanora beside her, opening her Prayer-book to the Burial-service as the bell tolled its final notes.

"Ayacanora," she whispered, "they are burying old Master Yeo, who loved you and sought you across the wide world, saving you from the crocodile's teeth. Are you not sorry for him, child, that you look so bright today?"

Ayacanora blushed, lowering her head. Her mind was occupied with nothing but Amyas.

When the service concluded and the parson stepped back, the crowd lingered, crowding around the fresh earth. Amyas stood motionless at the head of the grave. It had been dug at the west end of the church, near the base of the tall, gray, windswept tower that watched over the land and sea. Perhaps the old man wished to see the ships crossing the bar or hear the winter wind roar through the belfry above. It was a simple fancy, yet Amyas felt a kinship with the spot; perhaps Yeo would like it too.

The people continued to stare, first at the grave, then at the blind giant standing over it, sensing by some instinct that something more was due. And so it was. Amyas straightened to his full height, waving his hand majestically as if ready to address a throne. All eyes fixed upon him.

Twice he tried to speak, but his words choked in his throat. Finally, he found his voice.

"Good people all, and seamen, among whom I was bred and to whom I return home blind this day, to dwell with you till death—Here lies the flower and pattern of all bold mariners; the truest of friends and the most terrible of foes; unchangeable in purpose, crafty in council, and swift in execution. In triumph, sober; in failure, possessing an endurance beyond mortal man. He was the first Briton to humble the pride of the Spaniard at Rio de la Hacha and Nombre, and the first to sail those South Seas which, by God's grace, shall one day be as free to English keels as the bay outside. Having been purged from his youthful sins by strange afflictions and torments unspeakable, suffering at the hands of the Popish enemy, he learned there to fear God and naught else. Having acquitted himself worthily as a righteous scourge of the Spaniard and a faithful soldier of the Lord Jesus Christ, he is now exalted to his reward, as Elijah was of old, in a chariot of fire unto heaven. Letting fall, I trust and pray, upon you who remain, the mantle of his valor and his godliness, that these shores may never lack brave and pious mariners who count their lives worthless in the cause of their Country, their Bible, and their Queen. Amen."

Feeling for his companions' hands, Amyas walked slowly from the churchyard, across the village street, and up the lane to Burrough Gate. The crowd parted in solemn silence, treating him as an awe-inspiring figure, shut up alone with all his strength and fame within the dark prison of his blindness.

He knew precisely when they reached the gates, unlocking them with his own hands and stepping boldly onto the gravel path. Cary and Brimblecombe followed, trembling; they expected a violent outburst of emotion from him or his mother, their own hearts fluttering like a girl's. He went to the door as if he could see it, felt for the latch, stepped inside, and called quietly, "Mother!"

In an instant, his mother was in his arms.

For a while, neither spoke. She wept silently, her eyes dry, while he stood firm and cheerful, his great arms wrapped around her.

"Mother," he said finally, "I am come home, you see, because I needs must. Will you take me in and look after this useless carcase? I shall not be so very troublesome, mother, shall I?" He looked down, smiling, and kissed her brow.

She said nothing, but passed her arm gently around his waist and led him inside. "Take care of your head, dear child; the doors are low." They entered together.

"Will! Jack!" Amyas called, turning back. But the two good fellows had already walked briskly away.

"I'm glad we are gone," Cary said later. "I should have made a baby of myself in another minute, watching that angel of a woman. How her face worked and how she kept it in!"

"Ah, well!" Jack replied. "There goes a brave servant of the Queen's cut off before his work was a quarter done. Heigho! I must home now to see my old father, and then—"

"And then home with me," Cary insisted. "You and I never part again! We have pulled in the same boat too long, Jack. You must not go spending your prize-money in riotous living. I must see after you, old Jack ashore, or we shall have you treating half the town in taverns for a week to come."

"Oh, Mr. Cary!" Jack protested, scandalized.

"Come home with me, and we'll poison the parson; my father shall give you the rectory."

"Oh, Mr. Cary!"

Thus, the two departed for Clovelly that very day.

Amyas sat alone. His mother had stepped out briefly to speak to the seamen who had brought his luggage, setting them down to eat and drink. Amyas remained in the old bay-window where he had sat as a tiny boy, reading "King Arthur," "Fox's Martyrs," and "The Cruelties of the Spaniards." He reached out, feeling for the books; they lay side by side just as they had twenty years prior. The window was open, and a cool air brought in the familiar scents of four-season roses, rosemary, and autumn gilliflowers.

On the table sat a dish of apples. He knew them by their scent—the very same old apples he used to gather as a boy. He reached out, taking them, feeling over their skins, playing with them as if the intervening twenty years had never existed. As his fingers traced their curves, his entire past life rose before him like a dream flashing across the mind of a drowning man. He saw every place he had ever visited, heard every word spoken to him—until he arrived at that fairy island on the Meta. He heard the roar of the cataract once more, saw the green tops of palm trees sleeping in the sunlight far above the spray, and stepped amid the smooth trunks across the flower-fringed boulders, leaping down to the gravel beach beside the pool.

Then, rising from the fern-grown rocks, appeared the beautiful vision of Ayacanora. *Where was she?* He had not thought of her until now. How he had wronged her! Let it be; he had been punished, and the account was squared. Perhaps she cared for him no longer. Who would care for a great blind ox like him, who must be fed and tended like a baby for the rest of his lazy life? *Tut!* How long his mother was away!

He began playing again with his apples, thinking of nothing but them and his climbs with Frank in the orchard years ago.

Suddenly, one slipped through his fingers and fell to the floor. He stooped to feel for it, but could not find it. *Vexatious!* He turned hastily to search another direction and struck his head sharply against the table.

Was it the pain? The slight disappointment? Or was it the sudden, humiliating realization of his blindness brought home in such a ludicrous, commonplace way? Or perhaps it was the revulsion of overstrained nerves triggered by the shock? Had he become a child once more? He knew not. But he stamped on the floor in pettishness, then checked himself and burst into a violent flood of tears.

A quick rustle passed him. The apple was replaced in his hand, and Ayacanora's voice sobbed out:

"There! There it is! Do not weep! Oh, do not weep! I cannot bear it! I will get you all you want! Only let me fetch and carry for you, tend you, feed you, lead you, like your slave, your dog! Say that I may be your slave!"

Falling to her knees at his feet, she seized both his hands and covered them with kisses.

"Yes!" she cried. "I will be your slave! I must be! You cannot help it! You cannot escape from me now! You cannot go to sea! You cannot turn your back upon wretched me. I have you safe now! Safe!" She clutched his hands triumphantly. "Ah! And what a wretch I am, to rejoice in that! To taunt him with his blindness! Oh, forgive me! I am but a poor wild girl—a wild Indian savage, you know—but—but—" and she burst into tears.

A great spasm shook the body and soul of Amyas Leigh. He sat silent for a minute, then said solemnly:

"And is this still possible? Then God have mercy upon me, a sinner!"

Ayacanora looked up at his face, inquiring. Before she could speak again, he bent down, lifting her as a lion lifts a lamb, pressing her to his bosom and covering her face with kisses.

The door opened. There was the rustle of a gown. Ayacanora sprang from him with a little cry, standing half-trembling, half-defiant, as if to declare, *He is mine now; no one dare part him from me!*

"Who is it?" asked Amyas.

"Your mother."

"You see that I am bringing forth fruits meet for repentance, mother," he said with a smile.

He heard her approach. Then a kiss and a sob passed between the women, and he felt Ayacanora sink once more upon his bosom.

"Amyas, my son," said the silver voice of Mrs. Leigh, low and dreamy, like the far-off chimes of angels' bells from the highest heaven, "fear not to take her to your heart again; for it is your mother who has laid her there."

"It is true, after all," Amyas thought to himself. "What God has joined together, man cannot put asunder."

***

From that hour, Ayacanora's power of song returned to her. Day by day, year after year, her voice rose within that happy home, soaring on skylark's wings into the highest heaven, bearing with it the peaceful thoughts of the blind giant back to the Paradises of the West, in the wake of the heroes who from that time forth sailed out to colonize another and a vaster England, to the heaven-prospered cry of *Westward-Ho!*
